Airy limit for β \beta -additions through Dunkl operators
David Keating, Jiaming Xu

Abstract
It is well known that the edge limit of Gaussian/Laguerre Beta-ensembles, as well as a large class of β \beta -ensembles is given by the Airy ⁡ ( β ) \mathrm{Airy}(\beta) point process. We extend this universality result to a general class of additions of Gaussian and Laguerre ensembles, which were identified in [ AN ] as projection of the ergodic measures of the β \beta -corners process. In order to make sense of the β \beta -addition, we introduce the Type-A Bessel function as the characteristic function of our matrix ensemble, following the approach of [ GM ] , [ BCG ] . Then we extract its moment information through the action of Dunkl operators, and obtain certain limiting functional expressed via conditional Brownian bridges for the Laplace transform of Airy ⁡ ( β ) \mathrm{Airy}(\beta) . Our limit expression is universal up to proper rescaling among all of our additions, and agrees with the single-time Laplace transform expression from the concurrent work [ GXZ ] .



1
Introduction

1.1
Overview

The focus of this article is the intersection of three topics in random matrix theory: addition of random matrices,
\beta
-ensembles, and edge universality. The studies of addition of independent large random matrices date back to
[
Vo
]
, in which the author considers

C(N)=A(N)+B(N),

where
A(N)
,
B(N)
are two independent
N\times N
real/complex self-adjoint matrices with deterministic eigenvalues
a_{1}(N)\geq\ldots\geq a_{N}(N)
,
b_{1}(N)\geq\ldots\geq b_{N}(N)
and uniformly random eigenvectors. Voiculescu
[
Vo
]
proves that if the empirical measures of
A(N)
,
B(N)
satisfy

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{a_{i}(N)}\rightarrow\mu_{A},\quad\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{b_{i}(N)}\rightarrow\mu_{B}

for some deterministic measures
\mu_{A}
,
\mu_{B}
on
\mathbb{R}
, then

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{c_{i}(N)}\rightarrow\mu_{C},

where

\mu_{C}=\mu_{A}\boxplus\mu_{B}

is also a deterministic measure on
\mathbb{R}
known as the
free convolution
of
\mu_{A}
and
\mu_{B}
.
This classical result was an early breakthrough in the subject of free probability, which has various connections with different types of random matrix additions, see e.g.
[
B1
]
,
[
B2
]
,
[
MSS
]
.

In random matrix literature,
\beta
-ensembles
are random
N
-tuple of real numbers
x_{1}\geq x_{2}\geq\ldots\geq x_{N}
with density function

\frac{1}{Z_{N,\beta}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{\beta}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\exp\left[-V(x_{i})\right],

(1)

where
Z_{N,\beta}
is a normalizing constant and
V(x)
is the potential function satisfying certain analytic conditions. When
\beta=1,2,4
, such density corresponds to invariant matrix ensembles with real/complex/quaternion entries, see e.g.
[
D
]
for more details. For general
\beta>0
, the parameter is understood as the inverse temperature. Two of the most classical objects in this context are
\beta
-ensembles (G
\beta
E), when
V(x)=\frac{\beta}{2}x^{2}
, and Laguerre
\beta
-ensembles (L
\beta
E), when
V(x)=\left[\frac{\beta}{2}(a+1)-1\right]\log(x)-\frac{\beta}{2}x
. For G
\beta
E and L
\beta
E, Dumitriu and Edelman
[
DE
]
give a concrete tridiagonal matrix realization of the density function. This construction is later extended to Jacobi ensembles by Killip and Nenciu
[
KN
]
, then to a larger class of potentials
V(x)
by Krishnapur, Rider, and Virag
[
KRV
]
. The tridiagonal structure is a main tool in studying
\beta
-ensembles.

To study the addition of invariant random matrices for general
\beta>0
, rather than using the density in Eqn. (
1
), an alternate algebraic approach was developed. This is defined in
[
GM
]
, where the Type-A Bessel function, a multivariate special function with representation theoretic background, plays the role of the characteristic function for the matrix ensembles. On the level of Law of Large Numbers,
[
CX
]
and
[
Yao
]
prove that for any fixed
\beta>0
, the asymptotic empirical measure of self-adjoint additions is still characterized by the free convolution as was known for
\beta=1,2
. In addition,
[
GM
]
,
[
Me
]
,
[
MeP
]
,
[
BCG
]
study the asymptotic empirical measures of the additions as
N\rightarrow\infty
in the low and high temperature regimes (namely, when
\beta\rightarrow\infty
and
\beta\rightarrow 0
, respectively.), and obtain two other versions of convolutions that differ from the classical free convolution. Another similar story happens with the additions of
N\times M
rectangular matrices with certain invariant properties:
[
B1
]
,
[
B2
]
study the asymptotic empirical measures of singular values for fixed
\beta=1,2
when
N,M\rightarrow\infty
,
[
Xu
]
generalize the additions to
\beta>0
by using the Type-BC Bessel function, and
[
GrM
]
,
[
Gri
]
,
[
Xu
]
obtained several limiting results about the rectangular empirical measures for low and high temperatures.

One common feature of the literature on matrix additions mentioned above is that they only consider global asymptotic results. In this text we study the edge behavior of the random spectrum. For a single matrix ensemble without any additions, much is known about the edge behavior. For example, it is known that the largest eigenvalue of a large class of random matrix ensembles, including G
\beta
E and
L\beta E
, converges weakly to the Tracy-Widom distribution (
\mathrm{TW}_{\beta}
) after proper rescaling. Moreover, one can consider the largest
k
eigenvalues, where
k
is any fixed positive integer, the same arguments show that the rescaled
k
-tuple converge to the largest
k
particles of the
\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
process (abbreviated
\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
). The universal edge limit of large random matrices was also extended to Wigner matrices by
[
Sos
]
, and to generalized real/complex self-adjoint matrices in
[
PS
]
,
[
TV
]
,
[
EYY
]
,
[
LY
]
,
[
BEY
]
and other works. For
\beta\neq 1,2,4
, when there are no invariant matrices,
[
RRV
]
,
[
GSh
]
,
[
DG
]
,
[
PaSh
]
,
[
Sh
]
,
[
BEY
]
and other works show the edge universality for a large class of
\beta
-ensembles defined by Eqn.(
1
). We refer the readers to
[
BEY
, Section 1]
for a more detailed survey.

One of the main contributions of this article is to extend the edge universality of the self-adjoint matrices to a class of additions consisting of finitely many G
\beta
E and L
\beta
E, for general
\beta>0
. For
\beta=1,2
, this problem was previously studied in
[
Ahn
]
and
[
JP
]
using different approaches. In these works the authors add invariant matrices of more general form, however, the results and proofs only hold for
\beta=1,2
. In the current work on
\beta
-additions, we use the same ensembles as in
[
GM
]
,
[
BCG
]
, whose definition rely on multivariate special functions.

The proof of our main result relies on the moment method. Heuristically, when taking power sums

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lambda_{i}(N)^{M}

of all eigenvalues, where
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
is large enough compared to
N
, the statistic is dominated by the
\lambda_{i}(N)
s with largest absolute value. This allows one to study the edge behavior. A similar approach was initiated in
[
Sos
]
(see also
[
SS1
]
,
[
SS2
]
), and later applied in
[
Pa
]
,
[
Sod
]
,
[
GSh
]
to study the edge limit of various random matrix ensembles. One common feature of the above works is that the eigenvalue power sums are obtained by directly calculating powers of the corresponding Wigner matrices/tridiagonal realization of
\beta
-ensembles, and then taking the trace. Such an approach does not work in our setting, since to the best of our knowledge, a concrete matrix structure no longer exists for
\beta
-additions. Instead, we extract the moment information of the additions from their characteristic function using Type-A Dunkl operators, a class of differential-difference operators established in special function literature, see Section
2
for details. The applications of Dunkl operators to random matrices was initiated by
[
BCG
]
, using the same type of operators as in this text, and later
[
Xu
]
extended such connection to rectangular matrices using Dunkl operators of type BC. Both these works study the expectation of
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lambda_{i}(N)^{k}
for fixed
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, while in
[
GXZ
]
, which is concurrent to this text, Type-A Dunkl operators are used to give explicit expressions of the Laplace transform of the
\mathrm{Airy}_{\beta}
line ensemble, an infinite collection of continuous random curves, whose one-time marginal distribution gives
\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
.

1.2
Matrix additions for general
\beta

We start with the
\beta=2
case which corresponds to additions of complex self-adjoint matrices. Fix
c,\alpha_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, and a sequence
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
with
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}^{2}<\infty
. We consider the addition of matrices of the form

\displaystyle cI+\sqrt{\alpha_{0}}\frac{X+X^{*}}{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}\left(\frac{1}{2}V_{i}V_{i}^{*}-I\right).

(2)

where
X
is an
N\times N
matrix with i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,N)+i\mathcal{N}(0,N)
entries and
V_{i}
are i.i.d. columns of size
N
with i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)+i\mathcal{N}(0,1)
entries. The matrix
\frac{X+X^{*}}{2}
is the Gaussian Unitary ensemble. When
\alpha_{1}=\cdots=\alpha_{L}=a
, with all other
\alpha_{i}=0
,
i>L
,

\sum_{i=1}^{L}\frac{\alpha}{2}V_{i}V_{i}^{*}=\frac{\alpha}{2}YY^{*}

is the
N\times L
Laguerre Unitary ensemble (with weight
\alpha
), where
Y
is a
N\times L
rectangular matrix with i.i.d. complex Gaussian entries. We note that Eqn. (
2
) is studied in asymptotic representation theory: it is the
N\times N
corner of a random infinite complex Hermitian matrix, whose distribution is invariant under conjugation of
U(\infty)
and ergodic (see
[
OV
]
).

In this text we ignore the terms proportional to the identity by taking
c=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}
, and study the
\beta
-version of above addition. Here
\beta>0
is a parameter that is commonly viewed as the inverse temperature, and GUE, LUE are replaced by Gaussian
\beta
-ensemble (G
\beta
E) and Laguerre
\beta
-ensemble, (L
\beta
E) respectively. Since for
\beta\neq 1,2,4
there is no concrete invariant matrix of
G\beta E
and
L\beta E
, the
\beta
-addition is defined in terms of multiplication of the Type-A Bessel generating functions of the summands, as in
[
GM
]
,
[
BCG
]
. We discuss this in more detail in Section
2
.

We also highlight a connection of our ensembles and asymptotic representation theory. It is shown in
[
OV
]
for
\beta=2
, and later proved in
[
AN
]
,
[
BR
]
for all
\beta>0
that the class of
\beta
-additions in Eqn. (
2
) can be identified with the (level
N
projection of) the ergodic measures of the
\beta
-corners process. In particular, each of them gives a legitimate probability measure
P_{N}
of an
N
-tuple
\lambda_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{N}
in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. By classical free probability theory, the empirical measure of
\lambda_{i}
s converges to a deterministic measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}
as
N\rightarrow\infty
. The main focus of this text is the study of the upper edge behavior near
\mu_{+}
, the right endpoint of
\operatorname{supp}(\mu)
. This corresponds to the fluctuation of the largest eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{k}
, for a finite fixed
k
.

1.3
Main result

As a random point process on the real line parameterized by
\beta>0
, the
\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
process is known as the universal object characterizing the scaling limit of the largest eigenvalues of various random matrix ensembles. While for
\beta=1,2,4
there have been several characterizations of the limiting point configuration and the corresponding Tracy-Widom distributions in the past literature, for general
\beta
the explicit expressions for
\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
are less understood. In
[
RRV
]
, the authors make sense of the stochastic Airy operator

SAO_{\beta}=-\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}+x+\frac{2}{\sqrt{\beta}}B^{\prime}(x)

(3)

on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
with Dirichlet boundary condition at zero, where
B(x)
denotes the Brownian motion and the differential makes sense as Schwartz distribution, see
[
RRV
, Section 2]
for the precise analytic issues in defining such an operator.
SAO_{\beta}
is known to have an orthonormal basis of eigenfunctions with eigenvalues
-\eta_{i}\ (i=1,2,\ldots)
, and the particles in the
\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
process are given by these eigenvalues.

On the other hand,
[
GSh
]
introduces integral operators
\mathcal{U}(T)\ (T\geq 0)
, called the
stochastic Airy semigroup
, such that by
[
GSh
, Corollary 2.12]
,

\mathcal{U}(T)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\exp\left(-\frac{T}{2}SAO_{\beta}\right)

(4)

for each
T
. The right-hand side above is the unique stochastic operator with the same eigenfunctions as
SAO_{\beta}
and eigenvalues
\exp\left(\frac{T\eta_{i}}{2}\right)
. In addition,
\mathcal{U}(T)
is almost surely trace class (see
[
GSh
, Proposition 2.4]
), and the same reference gives an alternate characterization of
\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
in terms of its Laplace transform. In particular, we have the following expression.

Proposition 1.1
.

[
GSh
, Proposition 2.14]

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Trace}(\mathcal{U}(T))]=&\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}e^{\frac{T\eta_{i}}{2}}\right]\\
=&\frac{2}{\pi}T^{-\frac{3}{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{T^{\frac{3}{2}}}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\mathfrak{E}(t)dt+\frac{T^{\frac{3}{2}}}{2\beta}\int_{0}^{\infty}(l_{y})^{2}dy\right)\right],\end{split}

(5)

where
\mathfrak{E}
is a standard Brownian excursion on
[0,1]
, and each
l_{y}
is the total local time of
\mathfrak{E}
at level y.

Note that Proposition
1.1
gives an explicit formula for the first moment of

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}e^{\frac{T\eta_{i}}{2}},

(6)

the Laplace transform of
\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
. Moreover,
[
GSh
]
expresses the trace of
\mathcal{U}(T)
via an explicit random kernel built from Brownian bridges conditioned to stay nonnegative. It might also be possible to extract explicit expressions for higher moments of Eqn. (
6
) from the kernel although we do not pursue it here.

Our main result verifies that the upper edge limit of the following class of
\beta
-additions is given by
\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
. We introduce the following notations necessary for stating the theorem.

Fix
\beta>0
. Let
X(N)
be a
N\times N
Gaussian
\beta
-ensemble,

V_{N,L_{1}}(N),\ldots,V_{N,L_{k}}(N)

be independent
N\times L_{i}\ (L_{i}\geq N)
Laguerre
\beta
-ensembles, and fix
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
. Assume that there exists
\gamma_{i}>0
such that, as
N\rightarrow\infty
,

\begin{split}\frac{L_{i}}{N}\rightarrow\gamma_{i}\geq 1\end{split}

(7)

for each
i=1,2,\ldots,k
.

Fix
\alpha_{0}\geq 0
and consider the
\beta
-addition

\sqrt{\alpha_{0}}X(N)\boxplus_{N}^{\beta}\alpha_{1}V_{N,L_{1}}(N)\boxplus_{N}^{\beta}\ldots\boxplus_{N}^{\beta}\alpha_{k}V_{N,L_{k}}(N).

(8)

For
\beta=1,2,4
this is the usual additions of independent Gaussian and Wishart matrices, and for general
\beta>0
we give a generalized definition in Section 2. Without loss of generality assume

|\alpha_{1}|>|\alpha_{2}|>\ldots>|\alpha_{k}|>0.

(9)

For
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, let

\kappa_{2}=\alpha_{0}+\alpha^{2}_{1}\gamma_{1}+\ldots+\alpha^{2}_{k}\gamma_{k},\ \kappa_{2}(N)=\alpha_{0}+\alpha^{2}_{1}\frac{L_{1}(N)}{N}+\ldots+\alpha^{2}_{k}\frac{L_{k}(N)}{N},

(10)

and

\kappa_{l}=\alpha^{l}_{1}\gamma_{1}+\ldots+\alpha^{l}_{k}\gamma_{k},\ \kappa_{l}(N)=\alpha^{l}_{1}\frac{L_{1}(N)}{N}+\ldots+\alpha^{l}_{k}\frac{L_{k}(N)}{N},

(11)

for
l\neq 2
. We will only consider the case in which, for each
N=1,2,\ldots
,

\kappa_{l}(N)\geq 0

(12)

for all
l
. Let

V(z)=\frac{1}{z}+\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\kappa_{l}z^{l-1}

(13)

be a meromorphic function on
\mathbb{C}
locally near 0.

Theorem 1.2

(Edge universality of
\beta
-ensemble additions)
.

Let
\lambda_{i}(N)\ (i=1,2,\ldots,N)
denote the eigenvalues of our
N\times N
addition in Eqn. (
8
). Assume that (
7
), (
9
) and (
12
) hold, then there exists a positive constant
\tilde{C}
and a sequence of positive real numbers
\mu_{+}(N)
, such that

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\left\{\lambda_{i}^{\prime}:=\frac{\lambda_{i}(N)-\mu_{+}(N)N}{N^{\frac{1}{3}}}\right\}_{i=1}^{N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\tilde{C}\cdot\mathrm{Airy}(\beta),

(14)

the
\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
process.

The following statement gives explicit descriptions of the parameters in Theorem
1.2
.

Proposition 1.3
.

Let
z_{c}(N)>0,\ z_{c}>0
be the unique root of
V^{\prime}_{N}(z),\ V^{\prime}(z)
on
(0,\frac{1}{a_{1}})
respectively. Then
V_{N}(z_{c}(N))>0,\ V(z_{c})>0
, and

\begin{split}&\mu_{+}(N)=V_{N}(z_{c}(N))\rightarrow\mu_{+}=V(z_{c})\ \text{as}\ N\rightarrow\infty,\\
&\tilde{C}=2^{-\frac{1}{3}}V^{\prime\prime}(z_{c})^{\frac{1}{3}}.\end{split}

(15)

Remark 1.4
.

A key restriction of our ensembles is that
\kappa_{l}\geq 0
for all
l
. This is guaranteed when we have only additions of Laguerre ensembles, namely, all
\alpha_{i}>0
,
i\geq 1
. In general, by taking
a_{1}>0
, It is not hard to see that
\kappa_{l}>0
for all
l
large enough. However, what is not covered in our result is when the terms being subtracted contribute enough to make
\kappa_{l}<0
for some
l
. We conjecture that our result are still true in that case, see Section
7
for more details.

Remark 1.5
.

Note that in the addition (
8
) we allow the absence of the Gaussian ensemble in the sum. It is possible that if
\alpha_{0}>0
is large enough, the result in Theorem
1.2
can be (partially) obtained by the approach in
[
AH
]
, which relies on the analysis of Dyson Brownian motion. However, their approach does not cover the cases
\beta\in(0,1)
, or
\alpha_{0}=0
.

Example 1.6
.

A special case of Theorem
1.2
gives the classical edge limit of a single Laguerre
\beta
-ensemble of variance 1. In this case we have

\begin{split}V(z)=\frac{1}{z}+\frac{\gamma}{1-z},\quad z_{c}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma}+1},\quad\mu_{+}=V(z_{c})=(\sqrt{\gamma}+1)^{2},\end{split}

(16)

and

\tilde{C}=\frac{(\sqrt{\gamma}+1)^{\frac{4}{3}}}{\gamma^{\frac{1}{6}}}.

(17)

Plugging in these parameters gives exactly
[
RRV
, Theorem 1.4]
.

1.4
Proof ingredients

Define
\lambda_{i}^{\prime}=N^{-\frac{1}{3}}(\lambda_{i}(N)-\mu_{+}(N)N)
as in Theorem
1.2
. The main target in this text is to calculate the asymptotic distribution of the
\lambda_{i}^{\prime}
s in terms of the Laplace transform

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\exp\left(\frac{T\lambda_{i}^{{}^{\prime}}}{\mu_{+}}\right),

which is a collection of random variables parameterized by
T>0
.
More precisely, we compute the (mixed)
l^{th}
moments of the Laplace transform

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{l}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\exp\left(\frac{\mathbf{k}_{j}\lambda_{i}^{\prime}}{\mu_{+}}\right)\right)\right]

for all
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
by taking the asymptotics of

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{l}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\frac{\lambda_{i}(N)}{\mu_{+}(N)N}\right)^{M_{j}}\right)\right],

where
M_{j}\approx\mathbf{k}_{j}N^{\frac{2}{3}}
. In particular, we show that for each element in the class of additions we consider, the limiting moment expressions are all the same, after appropriate rescaling of
\lambda_{i}^{\prime}
s. This extends the edge universality, since we know that among these
\beta
-additions the single Laguerre ensemble has an
\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
edge limit.

The way we make sense of
\beta-
additions for general
\beta>0
is based on the
Type-A multivariate Bessel function

B_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta),

which is a symmetric function in the variables
x_{i}
and plays the role of characteristic function of our ensembles. We consider the actions of
Type-A Dunkl operators
, a class of differential-difference operators
\mathcal{D}_{i}(i=1,2,\ldots,N)
, on
\mathbb{E}[B_{(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{N})}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta)]
. Since the power sums of
\mathcal{D}_{i}
have Type-A Bessel functions as eigenfunctions, such actions extract the moment information of the
\lambda_{i}
s.

The approach of using Dunkl actions to study random matrices follows the same line as
[
BCG
]
and
[
Xu
]
. But compared to these works, which take the global limit of the empirical measures, we focus on edge behavior and therefore we take large powers of
\lambda_{i}(N)
s of order
O(N^{\frac{2}{3}})
. This makes the asymptotic analysis of the explicit actions much more involved. A similar approach to this paper is taken in the concurrent work
[
GXZ
]
, which takes Dunkl actions on
\beta
-Dyson Brownian motions and G
\beta
E corners processes, and establishes the first Laplace transform expression of Airy
β
line ensemble. Below we summarize the topics of the remaining sections, and simultaneously outline the common ingredients as well as the difference of these two works.

In Section
2
, we give the necessary backgrounds of Dunkl operators and Bessel generating functions (abbreviated as BGF). Thanks to the nice behavior of Gaussian and Laguerre ensembles, the BGF of our
\beta
-additions have an explicit form given in Eqn. (
29
). The main object in
[
GXZ
]
, however, is a single (multi-time/multi-level version of) G
\beta
E. In that case the BGF further simplifies to

\exp\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}N}{\beta}\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}^{2}\right).

While it is well known that under proper scaling, the global limit shape of G
\beta
E is the semicircle law of radius 2, the global limit shape of Eqn. (
8
), and in particular
\mu_{+}
in Theorem
1.2
, is obtained via a free convolution of one copy of G
\beta
E and
k
copies of L
\beta
Es. We present the detailed connection to free probability in Section
2.4
.

Section
3
studies the Dunkl action on our BGF for finite
N
and arbitrary
\mathbf{k}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{k}_{l}>0
. Similar to
[
GXZ
]
, we introduce a probabilistic interpretation of the action as a weighted sum over certain (conditional) random walk bridges with i.i.d. increments. While the two works share the same class of operators, the expressions in
[
GXZ
]
are in terms of Bernoulli walk bridges with increments
\pm 1
due to the simpler form of its BGF, while our walk bridges are known as
Łukasiewicz paths
in combinatorics and the increments take values in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-1}
. To more conveniently apply the probabilistic tools on these walk bridges, we specify the weights of the increments in such a way that they have mean 0. It turns out that the partition function of the increments then coincides with the scaling factor
\mu_{+}(N)
, which is essential in our characterization of the edge limit. In contrast to Bernoulli walks, there are much fewer explicit formulas and quantitative bounds for random Łukasiewicz paths. We state a collection of such results in Section
4
and Appendix A. To the best of our knowledge, these results have not been written down in the literature, and we believe that they are of independent interest. While our result covers all the finite additions of G
\beta
E and L
\beta
Es, part of the linear combinations involving minus signs seem out of reach from our probabilistic approach. We discuss such difficulties in more details in Section
7
.

After the above preparations, we present a self-contained framework that computes the asymptotics of the Dunkl actions as
N\rightarrow\infty
in Section
5
. In particular, under proper scaling the random walk bridges converge weakly
1
1

1

Instead,
[
GXZ
]
gives a quantitative bound between the pre-limit and limit expressions by coupling Bernoulli walks and Brownian bridges, see
[
GXZ
, Lemma 4.40]
.
to certain Brownian bridges. This observation, together with other significant technical efforts, lead to the convergence in moments in Theorem
5.11
. Our framework has a number of ingredients in common with
[
GXZ
, Section 4]
, and therefore the limit expression in Theorem
5.11
agrees with the single-time Laplace transform
\mathbf{L}_{\beta}(\left(2P_{-1}\sigma\right)^{-2/3}\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\bm{\tau}}=0)
defined in
[
GXZ
, Section 2]
, up to a factor
\left(2P_{-1}\sigma\right)^{-2/3}
that depends on the parameters in Eqn. (
8
). While sharing some common techniques, the two works have different focuses in that one deals with multi-time/multi-level limit, and another identifies the limit expressions from a class of ensembles. Moreover, by combining the ideas from both works, one can prove that the edge limit of a class of dynamics/corners processes of
\beta
-additions is Airy
β
line ensemble.

Finally, in Section
6
we pass from the moment convergence to weak convergence of the rescaled eigenvalues. In particular, we show that the Laplace transform uniquely determines
\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
.

This text (as well as
[
GXZ
]
) gives a new explicit expression of the
\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
process. It is then natural to identify
\mathbf{L}_{\beta}(\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\bm{\tau}}=0)
where
l=1
with Eqn. (
5
), given in
[
GSh
]
, or even identify
\mathbf{L}_{\beta}(\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\bm{\tau}}=0)
for general
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
with some corresponding formulas of
\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
given in the literature, by separate combinatorial arguments. We leave this as an interesting open problem.

2
Bessel generating functions

In this section we give a brief review of Type-A Dunkl operators and Bessel generating functions. See
[
Ro
]
,
[
A
]
and references therein for more details. In Section
2.3
, we use these generating function to define the notion of ‘addition’ for general
\beta
. Throughout the whole text we take
\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

2.1
Dunkl operators

Consider functions of
N
variables,
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}
. Define a differential-difference operator acting on these functions, known as the
Dunkl operator
(of Type-A), by

\mathcal{D}_{i}:=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}+\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{j:j\neq i}\frac{1-s_{i,j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}},\qquad i=1,\ldots,N

(18)

where
s_{i,j}
permutes the variables
x_{i}
and
x_{j}
. A key property is that the Dunkl operators commute (see
[
Du
]
), that is,

\mathcal{D}_{i}\mathcal{D}_{j}=\mathcal{D}_{j}\mathcal{D}_{i},\qquad i,j=1,\ldots,N.

(19)

We also define

\mathcal{P}_{k}:=\mathcal{D}_{1}^{k}+\cdots+\mathcal{D}_{N}^{k},\qquad k>0,

(20)

known as the
symmetric Dunkl operators
.

2.2
Multivariate Bessel functions

Let
(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{N})
be an
N
-tuple of real numbers satisfying
a_{1}\geq a_{2}\geq\ldots\geq a_{N}
. The
multivariate Bessel function
2
2

2

Our notation slightly differs to the literature, where the parameter
\beta
is replaced by
\theta:=\beta/2
.

B_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta)
has many definitions. For example, it can be seen as the expectation of a certain observable of the
\beta
-corner process or as a limit of Jack symmetric polynomials, see e.g.
[
OO
]
,
[
BCG
, Section 2.2]
.

Here we list several well-known properties of these functions:

•

They are symmetric in
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}
. This follows from their definition as a limit of Jack polynomials.

•

They can be extended to an entire function of
2N
variables
a_{1},\ldots,a_{N},x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}
.

•

They have the normalization
B_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})}(0,\ldots,0;\beta)=1
.

•

They are eigenfunctions of the
\mathcal{P}_{k}
operators. In particular,

\mathcal{P}_{k}B_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta)=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}a_{i}^{k}\right)B_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta).

2.3
Bessel generating functions

Let
W_{N}:=\{(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}|a_{1}\geq a_{2}\geq\ldots\geq a_{N}\}
. Given a probability measure
\mu
on
W_{N}
, we define the
Bessel generating function for
\mu
by

G_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta,\mu)=\int_{a_{1}\leq\ldots\leq a_{N}}\;B_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta)\;d\mu(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}).

(21)

Later in this text, we will drop
\mu
from the notation of BGF when the measure is clear from the context. It follows from the symmetry of the Bessel functions that
G_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta)
is also symmetric in the variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}
, and from the normalization of the Bessel functions we see that
G_{N}(0,\ldots,0;\beta)=1
.

We will be interested in measures
\mu
that satisfy a certain decay condition.

Definition 2.1
.

[
BCG
, Definition 2.7]

We say that a measure
\mu
on
W_{N}
is
exponentially decaying
with exponent
R>0
if

\int_{a_{1}\geq\ldots\geq a_{N}}e^{NR\max_{i}|a_{i}|}d\mu(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})<\infty.

This guarantees that the integral (
21
) converges for all
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
in the domain

\Omega_{R}:=\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{C}^{N}:|\Re x_{i}|<R,i=1,\ldots,N\},

see
[
BCG
, Lemma 2.9]
. We will only consider such measures on
W_{N}
.

When
\beta=1,2,4
, the Bessel function is known as the spherical transform for certain symmetric space (see
[
Hel
]
). For example, when
\beta=2
, we have

B_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta)=\int_{U}\;\exp{\text{Tr}(UAU^{*}X)}\;dU

(22)

where
A=\text{diag}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})
,
X=\text{diag}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
and
dU
is the Haar measure on
N\times N
Unitary matrices. One can view

M_{A}=UAU^{*}

as a random hermitian matrix with fixed eigenvalues given by
a_{1}\geq\ldots\geq a_{N}
.

Suppose we take the sum of two such random matrices,
M_{A}
and
M_{B}
, with fixed eigenvalues
a_{1}\geq\ldots\geq a_{N}
and
b_{1}\geq\ldots\geq b_{N}
, respectively. Consider the eigenvalues
c_{1},\ldots,c_{N}
of
M_{A}+M_{B}
. By Eqn. (
22
) and the linearity of the trace we have

\mathbb{E}[B_{(c_{1},\ldots,c_{N})}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta)|\vec{a},\vec{b}]=B_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta)B_{(b_{1},\ldots,b_{N})}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta)

It follows that

G_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta,\mu_{C})=G_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta,\mu_{A})G_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta,\mu_{B}).

When
\beta\neq 1,2,4
there is no underlying invariant matrices to sum, so we define the operation of addition in terms of the generating functions.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\mu_{A}
and
\mu_{B}
be exponentially decaying measures on
W_{N}
. Define
\mu_{C}:=\mu_{A}\boxplus_{N}^{\beta}\mu_{B}
(also written as
C=A\boxplus_{N}^{\beta}B
) to be the measure whose Bessel generating function is given by

G_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta,\mu_{C})=G_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta,\mu_{A})G_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta,\mu_{B}).

(23)

We note that it is a well-known open problem in the general case to construct a (unique) probability measure on
N
-tuple
(c_{1},\ldots,c_{N})
for which Eqn. (
23
) holds. However, for the sums we consider this has been done in
[
AN
]
. We denote this measure as
P_{N,\beta}
.

We will focus on two particular matrix ensembles: the Gaussian
\beta
-ensembles and the Laguerre
\beta
-ensembles.

Definition 2.3
.

For
\beta>0
, an
N\times N

Gaussian
\beta
-ensemble
is a probability measure on
N
-tuple
(\lambda_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
with density function

\frac{1}{Z_{N,\beta}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j})^{\beta}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda_{i}^{2}}{2N}\right),

(24)

and an
N\times L

Laguerre
\beta
-ensemble
(
N\leq L)
is a probability measure on
N
-tuple
(\lambda_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
with density function

\frac{1}{Z_{N,L,\beta}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\left[\lambda_{i}^{\frac{\beta}{2}(L-N+1)-1}\exp\left(-\frac{\beta\lambda_{i}}{2}\right)\right]\prod_{1\leq i\leq j\leq N}(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j})^{\beta}.

(25)

Z_{N,\beta}
and
Z_{N,L,\beta}
are certain normalizing constants.

Proposition 2.4
.

For the
N\times N
G
\beta
E, the Bessel generating function is given by

G_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta)=\exp\left(\frac{N(x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{N}^{2})}{\beta}\right).

For the
N\times L
L
\beta
E, the Bessel generating function is given by

G_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\left(1-\frac{2x_{i}}{\beta}\right)^{-L\beta/2}.

Sketches of proofs of these two facts are given in
[
BCG
]
and
[
Ne
]
, respectively.

Fix
\beta>0
,
\alpha_{0}\geq 0
,
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
. Consider the
\beta
-addition

\sqrt{\alpha_{0}}X(N)\boxplus_{N}^{\beta}\alpha_{1}V_{N,L_{1}}(N)\boxplus_{N}^{\beta}\ldots\boxplus_{N}^{\beta}\alpha_{k}V_{N,L_{k}}(N).

(26)

If
\lambda_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{N}
are the random eigenvalues of this sum, then by Definition
2.2
and Proposition
2.4
, their Bessel generating function is given by

\displaystyle G_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta)=\mathbb{E}[B_{(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{N})}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta)]=\prod_{k=1}^{N}\mathcal{F}(x_{k})

(27)

where

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}(x)=&\exp\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}N}{\beta}x^{2}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left(1-\frac{2\alpha_{i}}{\beta}x\right)^{-L_{i}\beta/2}\\
=&\exp\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}N}{\beta}x^{2}+\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{2^{l}}{l}\frac{\alpha_{1}^{l}L_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{k}^{l}L_{k}}{\beta^{l}}x^{l}\right).\end{split}

(28)

We see that

G_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta)=\exp\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}N}{\beta}p_{2}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})+\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{2^{l}}{l}\frac{\alpha_{1}^{l}L_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{k}^{l}L_{k}}{\beta^{l}}p_{l}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\right),

(29)

where
p_{k}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
is the
k
-th power sum symmetric function.

Remark 2.5
.

This class of matrices was chosen in large part because the multivariate Bessel generating function factors into the product of single variable functions
\mathcal{F}(z)
. This greatly simplifies the computation in what follows. However, the measure
P_{N,\beta}
supporting this BGF is also of independent interest, known as (up to shift by a constant) the extremal consistent distribution on the infinite families of interlacing arrays under the Dixon-Anderson conditional
probability distribution, see
[
AN
]
. The result in that paper generalizes the classification of ergodic unitary invariant infinite complex random matrices in
[
OV
]
to all
\beta>0
.

One of the key features of multivariate Bessel functions is that they are the (unique up to normalization) eigenfunctions of the Dunkl operators, see Proposition
2.6
below. A probabilistic version of this result is given in Proposition
2.7
. This will be the starting point of our moment calculations in the remaining sections.

Let
p_{k}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
be the degree
k
symmetric power sum of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}
, where
x_{i}(i=1,2,\ldots,N)
are formal variables.

Proposition 2.6
.

[
Op
]

Fix
\beta>0
,
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. For any
k=1,2,\ldots
,

p_{k}(\mathcal{D}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{N})B_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N};\beta)}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})=p_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})B_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta).

(30)

Here we evaluate the polynomial
p_{k}
on the operators
\mathcal{D}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{D}_{N}
.

Proposition 2.7
.

Fix
\beta>0
,
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. Define
G_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta)
as in Eqn. (
29
), and let
\lambda_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{N}
be its random eigenvalues distributed according to
P_{N,\beta}
. Then for any
k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{m}p_{k_{i}}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{N})\right]=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m}p_{k_{i}}(\mathcal{D}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{N})\right)G_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta)|_{x_{1}=\ldots=x_{N}=0}.

(31)

Proof.

This follows immediately from Proposition
2.6
and the fact
B_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})}(0,\ldots,0;\beta)=1
, (see
[
BCG
, Proposition 2.10]
for more details), once we check that
P_{N,\beta}
is exponentially decaying.

Consider the addition

C=A\boxplus_{N}^{\beta}B,

where
A
,
B
have deterministic eigenvalues
\vec{\lambda}(A)=(\lambda_{1}(A)\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{N}(A))
,
\vec{\lambda}(B)=(\lambda_{1}(B)\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{N}(B))
and C has random eigenvalues
\vec{\lambda}(C)=(\lambda_{1}(C)\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{N}(C))
. By
[
A
, Lemma 3.23]
,

\max_{i}|\lambda_{i}(C)|\leq\max_{i}|\lambda_{i}(A)|+\max_{i}|\lambda_{i}(B)|.

Moreover, it is clear form the density functions of G
\beta
E and L
\beta
E that for a given ensemble of the form Eqn. (
8
), there exist constants
C>0
,
R>0
such that
\mathbb{P}[\max_{i}|\lambda_{i}|>x]<C\exp(-2NRx)
, where
\vec{\lambda}
are the eigenvalues of a summand in Eqn. (
8
)
(note that here
N
is a fixed parameter). A union bound then gives that Eqn. (
8
) itself is exponentially decaying with exponent
R/(k+1)
.
∎

2.4
Connection to free probability

We briefly recall some basic properties of free convolution that relate to the asymptotic behavior of self-adjoint matrix additions. Given a measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}
, the free cumulants
\{\kappa_{l}(\mu)\}_{l=1}^{\infty}\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
are a collection of quantities that linearize the free convolution, namely, for
\mu_{C}=\mu_{A}\boxplus\mu_{B}

\kappa_{l}(\mu_{C})=\kappa_{l}(\mu_{A})+\kappa_{l}(\mu_{B})

for each
l=1,2,\ldots
For a more detailed exposition, see e.g.
[
No
]
,
[
NS
]
.

Since in this text we study the additions of
\beta
-Hermitian matrices that generalize
[
Vo
]
, it is not surprising that free cumulants again come into play. More precisely, they appear as the coefficients of the
\log G_{N}
as we will now describe.

Recall that for each
N=1,2,\ldots
, we consider the addition

\begin{split}\sqrt{\alpha_{0}}X(N)\boxplus_{N}^{\beta}\alpha_{1}V_{N,L_{1}}(N)\boxplus_{N}^{\beta}\ldots\boxplus_{N}^{\beta}\alpha_{k}V_{N,L_{k}}(N),\end{split}

(32)

where
X(N)
is a
N\times N
Gaussian ensemble and
V_{N,L_{i}}(N)
is a
N\times L_{i}(N)
Laguerre ensemble. For simplicity, we will always assume
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}

are independent of
N
.

Define the finite-
N
version of the free cumulants as follows. Let

\begin{split}&\kappa_{1}(N)=\frac{1}{N}\Big(\alpha_{1}L_{1}(N)+\ldots+\alpha_{k}L_{k}(N)\Big)\\
&\kappa_{2}(N)=\frac{1}{N}\Big(\alpha_{0}N+\alpha^{2}_{1}L_{1}(N)+\ldots+\alpha_{k}^{2}L_{k}(N)\Big)\\
&\kappa_{l}(N)=\frac{1}{N}\Big(\alpha^{l}_{1}L_{1}(N)+\ldots+\alpha^{l}_{k}L_{k}(N)\Big)\qquad l\geq 3.\end{split}

(33)

We assume that
\gamma_{1}(N):=L_{1}(N)/N\to\gamma_{1}>0,\ldots,\gamma_{k}(N):=L_{k}(N)/N\to\gamma_{k}>0
as
N\to\infty
, and without loss of generality assume that
\alpha_{1}>0
(the case
\alpha_{1}<0
only differs by a factor -1). In terms of the Bessel generating function,

\displaystyle G_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}N}{\beta}p_{2}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})+\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{2^{l}}{l}\frac{\alpha_{1}^{l}L_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{k}^{l}L_{k}}{\beta^{l}}p_{l}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\right)

(34)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(N\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{2^{l-1}}{l}\frac{\kappa_{l}(N)}{\beta^{l-1}}p_{l}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\right).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\partial_{i}\ln G(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta)|_{x_{1}=\ldots=x_{N}=0}=

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}(N)\rightarrow\kappa_{1},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\partial^{2}_{i}\ln G(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta)|_{x_{1}=\ldots=x_{N}=0}=

\displaystyle\frac{2\kappa_{2}(N)}{\beta}\rightarrow\frac{2\kappa_{2}}{\beta},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\partial^{l}_{i}\ln G(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta)|_{x_{1}=\ldots=x_{N}=0}=

\displaystyle(l-1)!\frac{2^{l-1}\kappa_{l}(N)}{\beta^{l-1}}\rightarrow(l-1)!\frac{2^{l-1}\kappa_{l}}{\beta^{l-1}},\qquad l\geq 3,

where

\begin{split}&\kappa_{1}=\alpha_{1}\gamma_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{k}\gamma_{k}\\
&\kappa_{2}=\alpha_{0}+\alpha^{2}_{1}\gamma_{1}+\ldots+\alpha^{2}_{k}\gamma_{k}\\
&\kappa_{l}=\alpha^{l}_{1}\gamma_{1}+\ldots+\alpha^{l}_{k}\gamma_{k}\qquad l\geq 3.\\
\end{split}

(35)

Since
\alpha_{1}>0
, we see that
\kappa_{l}(N)>0
for all
l
large enough.

Under the setting above, by
[
CX
, Theorem 1.1]
, the empirical measure

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\lambda_{i}/N}

of our addition converges weakly in probability to a deterministic probability measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}
as
N\rightarrow\infty
. The
\mu_{+}
in Theorem
1.2
and Proposition
1.3
is the right endpoint of
\operatorname{supp}(\mu)
. In addition, the
\kappa_{l}
are precisely the free cumulants of
\mu
. They completely determine
\mu
because of the one-to-one correspondence to moments of
\mu
(see Eqn. (
57
)), and the fact that
\mu
is exponentially decaying.

Example 2.8

(Gaussian ensemble)
.

Letting
\alpha_{i}=0
for
i\geq 1
and
\alpha_{0}=1
results in the G
\beta
E. Note that all the free cumulants are zero, except for
\kappa_{2}=1
. This implies that the limiting spectral measure is given by Wigner’s semi-circle law, which has density

\displaystyle d\mu(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sqrt{4-x^{2}},\qquad|x|\leq 2

(36)

and
0
otherwise. The non-zero moments are given by

\displaystyle m_{2n}=\int_{-2}^{2}x^{2n}d\mu(x)=C_{n}

(37)

where
C_{n}=\frac{1}{n+1}\binom{2n}{n}
is the
n
-th Catalan number.

Example 2.9

(Laguerre ensemble)
.

Suppose we take
\alpha_{1}=1
and
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\ldots
to be 0; then we get a
N\times L
L
\beta
E. When
N,L\to\infty
such that
L/N\rightarrow\gamma>1
, the limiting spectral measure is given by the Marchenko-Pastur distribution

\displaystyle d\mu(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{\sqrt{(x-\mu_{-})(\mu_{+}-x)}}{x},\qquad\mu_{-}\leq x\leq\mu_{+}

(38)

where
\mu_{\pm}=(\sqrt{\gamma}\pm 1)^{2}
. It is known that the Voiculescu
R
-transform characterizes the distribution, and for
\mu
it is given by

\displaystyle R(w)=\frac{\gamma}{1-w}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\gamma w^{n}.

(39)

The
R
-transform
R(w)
is also the generating function of the free cumulants. In this case, we see that
\kappa_{l}=\gamma
for each
l
.

3
Dunkl actions and configurations

3.1
Walks and blocks

Fix
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
,
\mathbf{k}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{k}_{l}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, and let
M_{1}(N),\ldots,M_{l}(N)
be sequences of positive integers (in the remainder of this text, we omit the dependence of
M_{j}
s on
N
in the notation), such that for some constant
C>0
uniform in
N
and
j=1,2,\ldots,l
,

\left|M_{j}-\mathbf{k}_{j}N^{2/3}\right|<C.

Let
M=M_{1}+\ldots+M_{l}
. In this section, we calculate the asymptotics of mixed moments of the form

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{l}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\mu_{+}(N)N}\right)^{M_{j}}\right)\right].

(40)

Recall from Proposition
2.7
that, for
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, the above can be obtained from the action

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{\mu_{+}(N)^{M}}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{l}\left[\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{1}}{N}\right)^{M_{j}}+\ldots+\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{N}}{N}\right)^{M_{j}}\right]G_{N}(\vec{x};\beta)\\
&=\frac{1}{\mu_{+}(N)^{M}}\cdot\sum_{i_{l}=1}^{N}\cdots\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{N}\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{i_{l}}}{N}\right)^{M_{l}}\cdots\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{i_{1}}}{N}\right)^{M_{1}}G_{N}(\vec{x};\beta).\end{split}

(41)

Consider the action of a single term in the above expansion,

\frac{1}{\mu_{+}(N)^{M}}\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{i_{l}}}{N}\right)^{M_{l}}\cdots\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{i_{1}}}{N}\right)^{M_{1}}G_{N}(\vec{x};\beta)\Bigg|_{\vec{x}=0}.

(42)

Recall that for the ensemble we consider,

G_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta)=\exp\left(N\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{2^{l-1}}{l}\frac{\kappa_{l}(N)}{\beta^{l-1}}p_{l}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\right).

and, from Eqn. (
18
), that the Dunkl operator is given by

\frac{1}{N}\mathcal{D}_{i}=\frac{1}{N}\partial_{i}+\frac{\beta}{2N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{1-s_{ij}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}.

(43)

The Dunkl operator consists of two parts: a partial derivative term
\partial_{i}
and the swapping term
\frac{1-s_{ij}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}
. We will consider the action of each term separately.

Let
c\in\mathbb{R}
,
n_{1},\ldots,n_{N}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and consider a monomial in
x_{1}
,…,
x_{N}
given by

c\cdot x_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdots x_{N}^{n_{N}}.

In order to understand the repeated action of the Dunkl operators on the Bessel generating function, we will compute the action of
N^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{i}
on functions of the form

\left(c\prod_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}^{n_{i}}\right)G_{N}(\vec{x};\beta).

By straightforward calculation, we have that the action of the partial derivative can be written as

\begin{split}\left(\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{2^{l-1}\kappa_{l}(N)}{\beta^{l-1}}x_{i}^{l-1}+\frac{n_{i}}{N}x_{i}^{-1}\mathbf{1}[n_{i}\geq 1]\right)\left(c\prod_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}^{n_{i}}\right)G_{N}(\vec{x};\beta),\end{split}

(44)

while, for
j\neq i
, the action of the swapping term can be written

\left(\frac{\beta}{2N}\cdot\frac{1-s_{ij}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\right)\left[\left(c\prod_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}^{n_{i}}\right)G_{N}(\vec{x};\beta)\right]=\begin{cases}\frac{\beta}{2N}\left(\sum_{k=n_{j}}^{n_{i}-1}x_{i}^{k}x_{j}^{n_{i}+n_{j}-1-k}\right)\left(c\prod\limits_{k\neq i,j}x_{k}^{n_{k}}\right)G_{N}(\vec{x};\beta),\quad&n_{i}>n_{j}\\
-\frac{\beta}{2N}\left(\sum_{k=n_{i}}^{n_{j}-1}x_{i}^{k}x_{j}^{n_{i}+n_{j}-1-k}\right)\left(c\prod\limits_{k\neq i,j}x_{k}^{n_{k}}\right)G_{N}(\vec{x};\beta),\quad&n_{i}<n_{j}\\
0,&n_{i}=n_{j}.\end{cases}

(45)

In either case, the Bessel generating function remains unchanged and only the form of the prefactor is altered. We introduce the following linear operators on monomials of the form
\prod_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}^{n_{i}}
,
n_{1},\ldots,n_{N}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

•

For
1\leq i\leq N
,
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
,

U^{(a)}_{i}(x_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdots x_{N}^{n_{N}}):=\frac{2^{a-1}\kappa_{a}(N)}{\beta^{a-1}}x_{i}^{a-1}\cdot x_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdots x_{N}^{n_{N}}.

•

For
1\leq i\leq N
,

d_{i}(x_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdots x_{N}^{n_{N}}):=n_{i}\mathbf{1}[n_{i}\geq 1]\cdot x_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdots x_{i-1}^{n_{i-1}}x_{i}^{n_{i}-1}x_{i+1}^{n_{i+1}}\cdots x_{N}^{n_{N}}.

•

For
1\leq i\neq j\leq N
,

d_{i,j}(x_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdots x_{N}^{n_{N}}):=\begin{cases}x_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdots x_{i-1}^{n_{i-1}}x_{i}^{n_{i}-1}x_{i+1}^{n_{i+1}}\cdots x_{N}^{n_{N}},&n_{i}>n_{j};\\
0,&n_{i}\leq n_{j}.\end{cases}

•

For
1\leq i\neq j\leq N
,

\tilde{d}^{(a)}_{i,j}(x_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdots x_{N}^{n_{N}}):=\begin{cases}x_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdots x_{N}^{n_{N}}\cdot{x_{i}^{-a}x_{j}^{a-1}},&\text{if }n_{i}>n_{j},\ 1<a\leq n_{i}-n_{j}\\
-x_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdots x_{N}^{n_{N}}\cdot x_{i}^{a-1}x_{j}^{-a},&\text{if }n_{i}<n_{j},\ 1\leq a\leq n_{j}-n_{i}\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Here we have split the partial derivative term with respect to
x_{i}
into

\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}U^{(a)}_{i}+\frac{1}{N}d_{i}

and swapping term into

\frac{\beta}{2N}\sum_{j\neq i}\left(d_{i,j}+\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\tilde{d}_{i,j}^{(a)}\right).

By Eqn. (
44
) and (
45
),

\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{\mu_{+}(N)^{M}}\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{i_{l}}}{N}\right)^{M_{l}}\cdots\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{i_{1}}}{N}\right)^{M_{1}}G_{N}(\vec{x};\beta)\\
&=\frac{1}{\mu_{+}(N)^{M}}\left(\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}U^{(a)}_{i_{l}}+\frac{1}{N}d_{i_{l}}+\frac{\beta}{2N}\sum_{j\neq i_{l}}\left(d_{i_{l},j}+\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\tilde{d}^{(a)}_{i_{l},j}\right)\right)^{M_{l}}\cdots\left(\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}U^{(a)}_{i_{1}}+\frac{1}{N}d_{i_{1}}+\frac{\beta}{2N}\sum_{j\neq i_{1}}\left(d_{i_{1},j}+\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\tilde{d}^{(a)}_{i_{1},j}\right)\right)^{M_{1}}(1)\cdot G_{N}(\vec{x};\beta)\\
&=\frac{1}{\mu_{+}(N)^{M}}\left(\sum_{T(t):1\leq t\leq M}\prod_{t=1}^{M}T(t)\right)(1)\cdot G_{N}(\vec{x};\beta),\end{aligned}

(46)

where the summation in the last line is over the finitely many choices of operators
T(t)
for each
t\in\llbracket 1,M\rrbracket
. In particular, let

\displaystyle C_{0}=0,\quad C_{p}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}M_{j},\quad p=1,2,\ldots,l,

then for
t\in\llbracket C_{p-1}+1,C_{p}\rrbracket
,
T(t)
is chosen from the
U^{(a)}_{i_{p}}
,
\frac{1}{N}d_{i_{p}}
,
\frac{\beta}{2N}\tilde{d}^{(a)}_{i_{p},j}
and
\frac{\beta}{2N}d_{i_{p},j}
. In this way, we may view each term in the sum as a discrete-time process of monomials in
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}
starting at time zero from the monomial
P(0)=1
with the monomial at time
t>0
given by
P(t)=T(t)\cdots T(1)(1),\ t=1,2,\ldots,M
.

Definition 3.1
.

For
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, let
P(\vec{x})=c\cdot x_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdots x_{N}^{n_{N}}
be a monomial in
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}
. We denote

\operatorname{Deg}(P):=\sum_{i=1}^{N}n_{i},\quad\operatorname{Deg}_{i}(P):=n_{i}\quad\text{for}\ i=1,2,\ldots,N.

Observe that after the action of
T(t)\ (t\in\llbracket 1,M\rrbracket)
, if
P(t)\neq 0
, the degree has either

•

increased by
a-1
if
T(t)=U^{(a)}_{i}
for some
i
,
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, or

•

decreased by 1 otherwise.

Moreover, only those sequences
\{P(t)\}_{t=0}^{M}
for which
P(M)
is a scalar will make a nontrivial contribution to Eqn. (
42
) as we always evaluate at
\vec{x}=\vec{0}
. These observations motivate us to construct a correspondence between each process
\{P(t)\}_{t=0}^{M}
that contributes to the action and a Łukasiewicz path
E(t)
, which we also call a
walk
.

Definition 3.2
.

Fix
N,l\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
M_{1},\ldots,M_{l}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
,
M_{1}+\ldots+M_{l}=M
. For each sequence of monomials
\{P(s)\}_{s=0}^{M}
, coming form the action
\frac{1}{\mu_{+}(N)^{M}}\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{i_{l}}}{N}\right)^{M_{l}}\cdots\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{i_{1}}}{N}\right)^{M_{1}}G_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta)|_{\vec{x}=0}
, let
E(t):[0,M]\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be a Cádlág function defined by

E(t)=\operatorname{Deg}(P(\lfloor t\rfloor)).

(47)

We refer to
E(t)
as a walk.

While the walk records the total degree, we also need to keep track of the degree of each individual variable. Due to the actions of the
\tilde{d}_{i,j}
, each
\operatorname{Deg}_{i}(P(t))
changes in certain nontrivial ways, which we record by a collection of
blocks

q_{i}(t)
. More precisely, we have the following.

Definition 3.3
.

Fix
N,l\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
M_{1},\ldots,M_{l}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
,
M_{1}+\ldots+M_{l}=M
. For each sequence of monomials
\{P(s)\}_{s=0}^{M}
, coming form the action
\frac{1}{\mu_{+}(N)^{M}}\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{i_{l}}}{N}\right)^{M_{l}}\cdots\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{i_{1}}}{N}\right)^{M_{1}}G_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta)|_{\vec{x}=0}
, let
q_{i}(t):[0,M]\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be a piecewise function, which is Cádlág on
[0,M]\setminus\{C_{0},\ldots,C_{l}\}
, defined by

q_{i}(t)=\begin{cases}\operatorname{Deg}_{i}(P(\lfloor t\rfloor)),\quad&t\in[0,M]\setminus\bigcup\limits_{j:i_{j}=i}(C_{j-1},C_{j}]\\
0,\quad&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(48)

Let

q(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}q_{i}(t),\quad t\in[0,M].

We call
\vec{q}(t)=(q_{1}(t),\ldots,q_{N}(t))
the blocks of
\{P(s)\}_{s=0}^{M}
.

We call the pair
(E,\vec{q})
a
configuration
created by the action
\frac{1}{\mu_{+}(N)^{M}}\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{i_{l}}}{N}\right)^{M_{l}}\cdots\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{i_{1}}}{N}\right)^{M_{1}}G_{N}(\vec{x};\beta)|_{\vec{x}=0}
, and define
\mathcal{B}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})
to be the set of all such configurations. Additionally, for
t\in[0,M]
, we call
x_{i}
the
main variable
, when
t\in\bigcup_{j:i_{j}=i}(C_{j-1},C_{j}]
. A key observation is that on
\bigcup_{j:i_{j}=i}(C_{j-1},C_{j}]
,

E(t)-q(t)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}=\operatorname{Deg}_{i}(P(\lfloor t\rfloor)).

When
t\in[0,M]\setminus\bigcup_{j:i_{j}=i}(C_{j-1},C_{j}]
(so
x_{i}
is not the main variable), each
q_{i}(t)
has finitely many discontinuities which we call
jumps of
x_{i}
. Each jump is corresponds to the action of one of the operators
\tilde{d}^{(a)}_{i,j}
, and each
q_{i}(t)
is determined by the jumps

\{(s_{m}(i),h_{m}(i))\}_{m=1,2,\ldots,\delta_{i},\ i=1,2,..N},

such that
(s_{m}(i),h_{m}(i))
gives the
occurrence time

s_{m}(i)\in\llbracket 0,M\rrbracket\setminus\bigcup_{j:i_{j}=i}(C_{i_{j}-1},C_{i_{j}}]
, and the
height

h_{m}(i)=q_{i}(s_{m}(i))-q_{i}(s_{m}(i)-)\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}.

Note that by Eqn. (
45
), for any jump of
x_{i}
at time
t
, the jump height
h:=q_{i}(t)-q_{i}(t-)
satisfies the following two rules:

\begin{split}&|h|\leq|E(t-)-q(t-)-q_{i}(t-)-\mathbf{1}[h>0]|;\\
&h\cdot(E(t-)-q(t-)-q_{i}(t-))>0.\end{split}

(49)

We call
x_{i}
an
auxiliary variable
if
i\notin\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}\}
, and it has at least one jump (in other words, the degree of
x_{i}
is nonzero at some point during the action).

By the above construction, each sequence of monomials
\{P(t)\}_{t=0}^{M}
such that
P(M)|_{\vec{x}=0}\neq 0
corresponds to a unique configuration
(E,\vec{q})
. We assign a weight
w(E,\vec{q})
to each of these configurations as follows. Let
\delta=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{i}
be the number of jumps in
(E,\vec{q})
,
\Delta\subset\llbracket 1,M\rrbracket
be the set of jump times,
m_{i}(t)
be the number of variables
x_{j}
such that
\operatorname{Deg}_{j}(P(t))<\operatorname{Deg}_{i}(P(t))
, which gives the number of
d_{i,j}
that act non-trivially on
P(t)
, and let
D(E)\subset\llbracket 1,M\rrbracket
be the set of times
3
3

3

One can think of this as the set of the “usual down-steps”.
at which
E(t)-E(t-)=-1
, and
t\notin\Delta
.

We then define the weight of a configuration according to

\begin{split}w(E,\vec{q})=\frac{1}{\mu_{+}(N)^{M}}&\prod_{l=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{2^{l-1}\kappa_{l}(N)}{\beta^{l-1}}\right)^{\#\ t:\ E(t)-E(t-)=l-1}\\
\times&\frac{\beta^{\delta}\prod_{t\in\Delta}(-1)^{\mathbf{1}[q(t)<q(t-)]}}{2^{\delta}N^{\delta}}\prod_{t:\ t+1\in D(E)}\left(\frac{\beta m_{i}(t)}{2N}+\frac{E(t)-q(t)}{N}\right)\\
=\frac{1}{\mu_{+}(N)^{M}}&\prod_{l=1}^{\infty}\kappa_{l}(N)^{\#\ t:\ E(t)-E(t-)=l-1}\\
\times&\frac{\prod_{t\in\Delta}(-1)^{\mathbf{1}[q(t)<q(t-)]}}{N^{\delta}}\prod_{t:\ t+1\in D(E)}\left(\frac{m_{i}(t)}{N}+2\frac{E(t)-q(t)}{\beta N}\right)\end{split}

(50)

The second equality above holds, since the action of the three terms except for
\frac{1}{N}d_{i}
in

\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}U^{(a)}_{i}+\frac{1}{N}d_{i}+\frac{\beta}{2N}\sum_{j\neq i}\left(d_{i,j}+\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\tilde{d}_{i,j}\right)

satisfy that, after each step, the power of
(\beta/2)^{-1}
introduced equals the change in height of the walk, and the total height change of
E
after time
M
is 0.

By the above discussions, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mu_{+}(N)^{M}}\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{i_{l}}}{N}\right)^{M_{l}}

\displaystyle\cdots\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{i_{1}}}{N}\right)^{M_{1}}G_{N}(\vec{x};\beta)\Bigg|_{\vec{x}=0}=\sum_{(E,\vec{q})\in\mathcal{B}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})}w(E,\vec{q}).

3.2
A probabilistic point of view

Note that the first line of the right-hand side of the second equality in Eqn. (
50
) can be rewritten as

\prod_{t\in\llbracket 1,M\rrbracket}\frac{\kappa_{E(t)-E(t-)+1}(N)}{\mu_{+}(N)},

(51)

where we take
\kappa_{0}(N)
to be 1. In this section, we introduce a probabilistic point of view for Eqn. (
51
) and use it to specify
\mu_{+}(N)
.

Given a sequence
\{\kappa_{l}(N)\}_{l=1}^{\infty}
defined by Eqn. (
33
), we treat them as the free cumulants of a probability measure, and let

V_{N}(z)=\frac{1}{z}+\sum_{d=1}^{\infty}\kappa_{l}(N)z^{l-1}

be their Voiculescu transform. Starting from this section, we assume that

\kappa_{l}(N)\geq 0\ \ \text{for\ all}\ \ l\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}.

Then it is direct to check that

•

V_{N}^{\prime}(z)
has a unique positive zero
z_{c}(N)
in its radius of convergence, and

•

V^{\prime\prime}_{N}(z_{c}(N))>0
.

We then define a random variable
X(N)
taking values in
\{-1,0,1,2,\ldots\}
by

\displaystyle P_{-1}(N):=\mathbb{P}(X(N)=-1)=\frac{1}{V_{N}(z_{c}(N))z_{c}(N)},

(52)

\displaystyle P_{l-1}(N):=\mathbb{P}(X(N)=l-1)=\frac{\kappa_{l}(N)z_{c}(N)^{l-1}}{V_{N}(z_{c}(N))},\qquad l\geq 1.

Denote the distribution of
X(N)
by
\mu(N)
. A simple calculation shows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[X(N)]=\frac{z_{c}(N)V_{N}^{\prime}(z_{c}(N))}{V_{N}(z_{c}(N))}=0,\qquad\mathbb{E}[X(N)^{2}]=\frac{z_{c}(N)^{2}V_{N}^{\prime\prime}(z_{c}(N))}{V_{N}(z_{c}(N))}:=\sigma_{N}^{2}.

(53)

Moreover, by Eqn. (
35
),

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}(X(N)=-1)\to P_{-1}:&=\frac{1}{V(z_{c})z_{c}},\quad\mathbb{P}(X(N)=l-1)\rightarrow P_{l-1}:=\frac{\kappa_{l}z_{c}^{l-1}}{V(z_{c})},\\
\sigma_{N}^{2}\rightarrow\sigma^{2}:&=\frac{z_{c}^{2}V^{\prime\prime}(z_{c})}{V(z_{c})}\quad\text{as}\ N\rightarrow\infty.\end{split}

(54)

We set

\mu_{+}(N):=V_{N}(z_{c}(N))\rightarrow\mu_{+}=V(z_{c})\quad\text{as}\;N\rightarrow\infty.

(55)

3.3
Conditional walk bridges

Using the interpretation in Section
3.2
, we will consider
E(t)
as a (conditional) walk bridge with i.i.d. increments of distribution
\mu(N)
. When
l=1
,
E(t)
is simply a walk excursion of length
M=M_{1}
. However, for general
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, it will be more natural to view
E(t)
as a concatenation of walk bridges with nonnegative heights and general boundary points. As a preparation, we introduce the following notions.

Let
L\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1},H_{1},H_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. We say that
W(t):[n,n+L]\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a walk bridge of length
L
from
H_{1}
to
H_{2}
, if

•

W(t)
is a Cádlág function, such that
W(t)=W(\lfloor t\rfloor)
,

•

W(n)=H_{1}
,
W(n+L)=H_{2}
.

We identify two walk bridges
W_{1}:[n_{1},n_{1}+L]\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
and
W_{2}:[n_{2},n_{2}+L]\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
if
W_{2}(t)=W_{1}(t+n_{2}-n_{1})
.

Definition 3.4
.

For
H_{1},H_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
L\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
,
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
,
\mathcal{\tilde{W}}(H_{1},H_{2},L;N)
is the set of walk bridges of length
L
from
H_{1}
to
H_{2}
.

\mathcal{W}(H_{1},H_{2},L;N)
is the set of walk bridges
W(t)
of length
L
from
H_{1}
to
H_{2}
, such that
W(t)\geq 0
for all
t
in its domain.

For each element in
\mathcal{\tilde{W}}(H_{1},H_{2},L;N)
or
\mathcal{W}(H_{1},H_{2},L;N)
, by viewing the increments of
W(t)
as i.i.d. random variables with distribution
\mu(N)
, defined in Eqn. (
52
), we assign a weight to each walk bridge as

\text{weight}(W)=\prod_{l=-1}^{\infty}[P_{l}(N)V_{N}(z_{c}(N))]^{\#t:\ W(t)-W(t-)=l}.

Let

|\mathcal{\tilde{W}}(H_{1},H_{2},L;N)|=\sum_{W\in\mathcal{\tilde{W}}(H_{1},H_{2},L;N)}\text{weight}(W)

be the
partition function
of
\mathcal{\tilde{W}}(H_{1},H_{2},L;N)
. The partition function of
\mathcal{W}(H_{1},H_{2},L;N)
is defined similarly.

In Section
4
and
5
, we will consider conditional walk bridges in
\mathcal{W}(H_{1},H_{2},L;N)
, and the asymptotics of their partition functions of all the four possible types: 1.
H_{1}=H_{2}=0
, 2.
H_{1}>0,\ H_{2}=0
, 3.
H_{1}=0,\ H_{2}>0
and 4.
H_{1},H_{2}>0
.
Each type above describes a segment of
E(t)
in our configuration
(E,\vec{q})
.

Remark 3.5
.

When
P_{-1}(N)=P_{1}(N)=\frac{1}{2}
,
|\mathcal{\tilde{W}}(H_{1},H_{2},L;N)|
can be counted by binomial theorem, and
|\mathcal{W}(H_{1},H_{2},L;N)|
is given by the reflection principle. This corresponds to the moments of G
\beta
E, which is studied in great details in
[
GXZ
]
. On the other hand, in our context the increments take value in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-1}
, which creates extra technicalities in the computation of the partition functions.

Unlike for Bernoulli walks, there is an explicit formula of
|\mathcal{W}(H_{1},H_{2},L;N)|
only when
H_{2}=0
.

Lemma 3.6
.

For
H\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
L\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, the partition function for the walk bridges with length
L
from
H
to
0
is

|\mathcal{W}(H,0,L;N)|=\frac{H+1}{L+1}|\tilde{W}(H+1,L+1;N)|z_{c}(N)=\frac{H+1}{L+1}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint\;\left(\frac{z}{z_{c}(N)}\right)^{H}V_{N}(z)^{L+1}\;dz

(56)

where our contour only encloses the pole at zero.

We prove Lemma
3.6
in Appendix A, by solving a ballot problem that generalizes the reflection principle for Bernoulli walks. Our approach, however, seems to no longer work when
H_{1}=0,H_{2}>0
.

Remark 3.7
.

When taking
H=0
, the walk bridge reduces to a walk excursion of length
L
, and we recover a classical result in free probability. It is well known (see e.g.
[
No
, Section 1.6]
) that for a probability measure
\mu
with finite moments, its
L^{th}
moment
m_{L}
is given in terms of its free cumulants
\kappa_{l},\ l\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
by

m_{L}=\sum_{\pi\in NC(L)}\prod_{B\in\pi}\kappa_{|B|},

(57)

where the sum is over non-crossing partitions
\pi=B_{1}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup B_{n}
of the set
\{1,2,\ldots,L\}
,
B\in\pi
is a block of the partition, and
|B|
is the size of the block. Another classical result (see e.g.
[
NS
, Proposition 9.8]
) gives a combinatorial bijection between a non-crossing partition of size
L
and a Łukasiewicz path of length
L
. One can also find the contour integral of
m_{L}
in the literature, see e.g.
[
BCG
, Appendix]
.

4
Properties of random walk bridges

In this section, we provide several results regarding the walks sampled from
\mathcal{W}(H_{1},H_{2},L;N)
or
\mathcal{\tilde{W}}(H_{1},H_{2},L;N)
.

4.1
Conditional functional CLT’s

Here we state a general result on the convergence of random walks to Brownian excursions due to
[
CC
]
(see also
[
S
]
). Let
W(n)=W(0)+X_{1}(N)+\ldots+X_{n}(N),n=0,1,2,\ldots
be an integer-valued aperiodic random walk with i.i.d. increments
X(N)
whose distribution depends on
N
, and
\mathbb{E}[X(N)]=0
. We assume that
W
lies in the domain of attraction of the standard Gaussian law, namely, there exists
\sigma>0
such that

\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{L}X_{i}(N)}{\sigma\sqrt{L}}\implies\mathbf{N}(0,1)\quad\text{as}\ L\rightarrow\infty,\ N\rightarrow\infty.

(58)

For
t>0
,
L,N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
,
let
P_{t,L,N}^{*,x,y}
be the law of
W(t)
conditioned to be non-negative and satisfy
W(0)=x\geq 0,W(\lfloor tL\rfloor)=y\geq 0
. Define
\Omega
to be the space of cádlág functions on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
endowed with the standard Skorohod topology and define
\psi_{t,L}:\mathbb{Z}^{\lfloor tL\rfloor}\to\Omega
by

\psi_{t,L}(W(1),\ldots,W(\lfloor tL\rfloor))(s)=\left(\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{L}}W(\lfloor sL\rfloor)\right)_{0\leq s\leq t}.

(59)

Theorem 4.1
.

[
CC
, Theorem. 2.4 and Corollary 2.5]

As
L\to\infty
,
N\rightarrow\infty
, the following weak convergence holds in
\Omega
:

P_{t,L,N}^{*,0,0}\circ(\psi_{t,L})^{-1}\implies B_{e}(\cdot)

(60)

where
B_{e}:\ [0,t]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is the law of a Brownian excursion of length
t
.

Theorem
4.1
applies when we calculate the asymptotics of Eqn. (
40
) when
l=1
.
For general
l\geq 1
, we also consider the conditional random walk bridges with different starting and ending heights.

Theorem 4.2
.

[
CC
, Theorem. 2.4 and Corollary 2.5]

Suppose
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
are two sequences of integers such that

\frac{H_{1}}{\sigma\sqrt{L}}\rightarrow u>0,\qquad\frac{H_{2}}{\sigma\sqrt{L}}\rightarrow v>0

as
L\rightarrow\infty
. Then as
L\rightarrow\infty
,
N\rightarrow\infty
, we have the weak convergence

P_{t,L,N}^{*,0,H_{1}}\circ(\psi_{t,L})^{-1}\implies B_{3}(\cdot),

(61)

P_{t,L,N}^{*,H_{2},0}\circ(\psi_{t,L})^{-1}\implies B_{3}(t-\cdot),

(62)

where
B_{3}
is a Brownian bridge of length
t
from
0
to
u
conditioned on staying nonnegative, and
B_{3}(t-\cdot)
is a Brownian bridge from
v
to
0
conditioned on staying nonnegative.

Moreover,

P_{t,L,N}^{*,H_{1},H_{2}}\circ(\psi_{t,L})^{-1}\implies B(\cdot),

(63)

where
B
is a Brownian bridge from
u
to
v
conditioned on staying nonnegative.

0
u

u
v
0

v
