Figure 1:
The three cases for the paths in Theorem
4.2
.

4.2
Conditional density functions

We start with calculating the asymptotics of the partition function of a walk excursion. When the walk increments are Bernoulli random variables, as in
[
GXZ
]
, this is given by (the asymptotics of) Catalan number.

Lemma 4.3
.

As
L\rightarrow\infty,N\rightarrow\infty
, we have

L^{\frac{3}{2}}\cdot\frac{|\mathcal{W}(0,0,L;N)|}{V_{N}(z_{c}(N))^{L}}\to\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\frac{1}{P_{-1}\sigma}.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.6
, we have that

|\mathcal{W}(0,0,L;N)|=\frac{1}{L+1}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint\;V_{N}(z)^{L+1}\;dz.

(64)

The result then follows from a standard steepest descent analysis, which we sketch as follows.
Denote
f_{N}(z)=\log V_{N}(z),
which is analytic near
z=z_{c}(N)
since
V_{N}(z)>0
on
(0,\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}})
. Let
C^{\epsilon}_{z}(N)
be the arc of the circle
C_{z}(N)
with argument in
(-\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{L+1}},\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{L+1}})
. Then we have

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{C_{z}(N)}V_{N}(z)^{L+1}dz\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\approx}}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{C^{\epsilon}_{z}(N)}V_{N}(z)^{L+1}dz\\
=&\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{C^{\epsilon}_{z}(N)}\exp\Big[(L+1)f_{N}(z)\Big]dz\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\approx}}&\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{C^{\epsilon}_{z}(N)}\exp\Big[(L+1)f_{N}(z_{c}(N))\Big]\exp\Big[(L+1)\frac{f^{\prime\prime}_{N}(z_{c}(N))}{2}(z-z_{c}(N))^{2}\Big]dz\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\approx}}&V_{N}(z_{c}(N))^{L+1}\cdot\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{C^{\epsilon}_{z}(N)}\exp\Big[(L+1)\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(z_{c})}{2}(z-z_{c})^{2}\Big]dz\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\approx}}&\frac{V_{N}(z_{c}(N))^{L+1}}{\sqrt{L+1}}\cdot\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\exp\Big[-\frac{|f^{\prime\prime}(z_{c})|}{2}t^{2}\Big]dt\\
\approx&\frac{V(z_{c}(N))^{L}}{\sqrt{L+1}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\frac{V(z_{c})}{\sqrt{|f^{\prime\prime}(z_{c})|}}.\end{split}

(65)

The approximations (a)-(d) require some verification, whose details we omit. Since

\frac{V(z_{c})}{\sqrt{|f^{\prime\prime}(z_{c})|}}=\frac{V(z_{c})^{3/2}}{\sqrt{V^{\prime\prime}(z_{c})}}=\frac{1}{P_{-1}\sigma}

by Eqn. (
54
), the result then follows.
∎

Definition 4.4
.

For
x>0
,
h,g>0
, we define

\mathbf{F}(x;h,g)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi x}}\left(\exp\left(-\frac{(g-h)^{2}}{2x}\right)-\exp\left(-\frac{(g+h)^{2}}{2x}\right)\right).

Note that (by reflection principle)
\mathbf{F}(x;h,g)
is the transition density of a Brownian bridge from
g
to
h
, conditioned on staying nonnegative.

Let

\mathbf{F}_{0}(x,h)=\frac{2h}{\sqrt{2\pi x^{3}}}\exp\left(-\frac{h^{2}}{2x}\right),

and let

\mathbf{F}_{0,0}(x)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{2\pi x^{3}}}.

Using Lemma
4.3
, we have a precise estimate of the partition functions of
\mathcal{W}(H,0,L;N)
and
\mathcal{W}(0,H,L;N)
, in terms of
\mathbf{F}_{0}
and
\mathbf{F}_{0,0}
. Note that the asymptotic expressions depend on the parameters
P_{-1}
and
\sigma
defined in Eqn. (
54
), and differ in the two cases.

Lemma 4.5
.

Fix
h>0
,
x>0
. As
N\rightarrow\infty
, we have

\begin{split}&N^{2/3}\cdot\frac{|\mathcal{W}(\lfloor h\sigma N^{1/3}\rfloor,0,\lfloor xN^{2/3}\rfloor;N)|}{V_{N}(z_{c}(N))^{\lfloor xN^{2/3}\rfloor}}\rightarrow(2P_{-1})^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{F}_{0}(x,h),\\
&N^{2/3}\cdot\frac{|\mathcal{W}(0,\lfloor h\sigma N^{1/3}\rfloor,\lfloor xN^{2/3}\rfloor;N)|}{V_{N}(z_{c}(N))^{\lfloor xN^{2/3}\rfloor}}\rightarrow\sigma^{-2}\cdot\mathbf{F}_{0}(x,h),\\
&N\cdot\frac{|\mathcal{W}(0,0,\lfloor xN^{2/3}\rfloor;N)|}{V_{N}(z_{c}(N))^{\lfloor xN^{2/3}\rfloor}}\to(2P_{-1}\sigma)^{-1}\mathbf{F}_{0,0}(x).\end{split}

(66)

Proof.

Note that by our assumptions, the random walk law is supported by
\mathbb{Z}
and aperiodic. Then by the classical local limit theorem (see e.g.
[
DM
, Theorem 2]
) and Lemma
3.6
,

\begin{split}&\frac{|\mathcal{W}(\lfloor h\sigma N^{1/3}\rfloor,0,\lfloor xN^{2/3}\rfloor;N)|}{V_{N}(z_{c}(N))^{\lfloor xN^{2/3}\rfloor}}\frac{1}{z_{c}(N)V_{N}(z_{c}(N))}\\
=&\frac{|\mathcal{W}(\lfloor h\sigma N^{1/3}\rfloor,0,\lfloor xN^{2/3}\rfloor;N)|z_{c}(N)^{-1}}{|\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(\lfloor h\sigma N^{1/3}\rfloor+1,0,\lfloor xN^{2/3}\rfloor+1;N)|}\frac{|\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(\lfloor h\sigma N^{1/3}\rfloor+1,0,\lfloor xN^{2/3}\rfloor+1;N)|}{V_{N}(z_{c}(N))^{\lfloor xN^{2/3}\rfloor+1}}\\
=&\frac{\lfloor h\sigma N^{1/3}\rfloor+1}{\lfloor xN^{2/3}\rfloor+1}\cdot\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{xN^{2/3}}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left(-\frac{h^{2}}{2x}\right)+o(1)\right).\\
\end{split}

(67)

The first line of Eqn. (
66
) then follows from dividing both sides by
P_{-1}(N)
and taking
N\rightarrow\infty
.

For the second line, consider a random walk excursion
W^{\prime}(t)
of length
2\lfloor xN^{2/3}\rfloor
, with i.i.d. increments
X(N)
as defined in Eqn. (
52
), conditioned to stay non-negative. The midpoint density at
t=\frac{1}{2}
of a standard Brownian excursion of length 1 is
4
4

4

One can see this by e.g. a limit transition from the Bernoulli walks.

\frac{16}{\sqrt{2\pi}}y^{2}\exp\left(-2y^{2}\right).

So by Theorem
4.1
and the local limit theorem for non-negative walks (see e.g.
[
CC
, Proposition 4.1]
),

\begin{split}&\frac{|\mathcal{W}(0,\lfloor h\sigma N^{1/3}\rfloor,\lfloor xN^{2/3}\rfloor;N)|\cdot|\mathcal{W}(\lfloor h\sigma N^{1/3}\rfloor,0,\lfloor xN^{2/3}\rfloor;N)|}{|\mathcal{W}(0,0,2\lfloor xN^{2/3}\rfloor;N)|}=\mathbb{P}[W^{\prime}(\lfloor xN^{2/3}\rfloor)=\lfloor h\sigma N^{1/3}\rfloor]\\
=&\frac{1}{\sigma}\frac{1}{\sqrt{xN^{2/3}}}\left(\frac{4h^{2}}{\sqrt{\pi}x}\exp\left(-\frac{h^{2}}{x}\right)+o(1)\right),\end{split}

(68)

By Lemma
4.3
,

\displaystyle\frac{|\mathcal{W}(0,0,2\lfloor xN^{2/3}\rfloor;N)|}{V_{N}(z_{c}(N))^{2\lfloor xN^{2/3}\rfloor}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\left(\frac{1}{P_{-1}\sigma}+o(1)\right)(2\lfloor xN^{2/3}\rfloor)^{-\frac{3}{2}}.

Multiplying this with Eqn. (
68
) gives

\displaystyle\frac{|\mathcal{W}(0,\lfloor h\sigma N^{1/3}\rfloor,\lfloor xN^{2/3}\rfloor;N)|\cdot|\mathcal{W}(\lfloor h\sigma N^{1/3}\rfloor,0,\lfloor xN^{2/3}\rfloor;N)|}{V_{N}(z_{c}(N))^{2\lfloor xN^{2/3}\rfloor}}=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{1}{P_{-1}\sigma^{2}}\frac{1}{(xN^{2/3})^{2}}\left(\frac{h^{2}}{x}\exp\left(-\frac{h^{2}}{x}\right)+o(1)\right).

The second line of Eqn. (
66
) then follows from further dividing the above expression by the first line. The third line is a rephrase of Lemma
4.3
.
∎

4.3
Time homogeneity of down-steps

Recall that the only negative increment of our walk-bridges is -1, and we say that the walk takes a down-step at a certain time when this occurs.

Lemma 4.6
.

For
H_{1},H_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\ L\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, sample the walk
W(t)
from
\mathcal{W}(H_{1},H_{2},L,N)
, with i.i.d.. increments as in Eqn. (
52
), where

\frac{H_{1}}{\sqrt{L}}\rightarrow u,\ \frac{H_{2}}{\sqrt{L}}\rightarrow v

for some given
u,v\geq 0
.
For any
0<t_{1}<t_{2}<1
, let
\rho_{-1}(t_{1},t_{2})
denote the proportion of down-steps in
\llbracket t_{1}L,t_{2}L\rrbracket
. Then we have

\rho_{-1}(t_{1},t_{2})\rightarrow P_{-1}\quad\text{in\ probability}

(69)

as
L,N\rightarrow\infty
, where
P_{-1}=\frac{1}{z_{c}V(z_{c})}
, as defined in Eqn. (
54
).

Remark 4.7
.

By the definition of
X(N)
, we recall from Section
3.2
that

P_{-1}=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}[X(N)=-1].

(70)

Proof.

Since
t_{1}>0,t_{2}<1
,
H_{1},H_{2}\sim\sqrt{L}
, under the scaling of
W(t)
according to Theorem
4.1
or
4.2
, we have that with high probability,

\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}W(\lfloor t_{1}L\rfloor),\ \frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}W(\lfloor t_{2}L\rfloor)>\epsilon>0

for some
\epsilon(t_{1},t_{2})
uniform in
L,N
large enough. Therefore it suffices to take
t_{1}=0
,
t_{2}=1
, and
u,v>0
.

Let
\tilde{W}(t)(t=0,1,2,\ldots,L)
be a walk with the same increments and weights as
W(t)
, except that it does not have the conditioning to stay nonnegative. Observe that

\mathbb{P}[\tilde{W}(t)\geq 0\ \text{for\ all\ }t|\tilde{W}(0)=H_{1},\tilde{W}(L)=H_{2}]>\epsilon^{\prime}

(71)

for some
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon^{\prime}(u,v)>0
uniformly in
L,N
large enough.
Therefore, we have for any
\delta>0
,

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\rho_{-1}-P_{-1}\right|>\delta\Big|\tilde{W}(t)\geq 0\ \text{for\ all\ }t\right]=\frac{\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\rho_{-1}-P_{-1}\right|>\delta,\tilde{W}(t)\geq 0\ \text{for\ all\ }t\right]}{\mathbb{P}\left[\tilde{W}(t)\geq 0\ \text{for\ all\ }t\right]}\\
\leq&\frac{1}{\epsilon}\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\rho_{-1}-P_{-1}\right|>\delta\right].\\
\end{split}

(72)

It remains to show that the last line above goes to 0 as
L\rightarrow\infty
,
N\rightarrow\infty
. This is done by a straightforward calculation.

Denote
H_{2}-H_{1}
by
H(L)
,
L+H(L)
by
H^{\prime}(L)
. Then for
n_{-1},n_{0},n_{1},n_{2},\ldots\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

n_{-1}+n_{0}+n_{1}+\ldots+n_{H^{\prime}(L)}=L,

(73)

where
J_{i}
is the number of jumps of size
i
, and

-n_{-1}+n_{1}+2n_{2}+\ldots+H^{\prime}(L)n_{H^{\prime}(L)}=H(L).

(74)

We have

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}\left[\text{there\ are\ }n_{i}\ \text{jumps\ of\ size}\ i\ \text{in\ the\ walk, for each $i=-1,0,1,\ldots,H^{\prime}(L)$ }\right]\\
=&\binom{L}{n_{-1}\ n_{0}\ n_{1}\ \ldots\ n_{H^{\prime}(L)}}p_{-1}(N)^{n_{-1}}p_{0}(N)^{n_{0}}p_{1}(N)^{n_{1}}\cdots p_{H^{\prime}(L)}(N)^{n_{H^{\prime}(L)}}\\
\approx&(2\pi)^{-\frac{H^{\prime}(L)}{2}}L^{L+\frac{1}{2}}n_{-1}^{-(n_{-1}+\frac{1}{2})}n_{0}^{-(n_{0}+\frac{1}{2})}\cdots n_{H^{\prime}(L)}^{-(n_{H^{\prime}(L)}+\frac{1}{2})}\\
\qquad\quad&\cdot p_{-1}(N)^{n_{-1}}p_{0}(N)^{n_{0}}\cdots p_{H^{\prime}(L)}(N)^{n_{H^{\prime}(L)}}.\end{split}

(75)

The third line of Eqn. (
75
) follows from Stirling’s formula.

By Eqn. (
73
) and (
74
), we rewrite
n_{-1},n_{1}
in terms of the other terms, such that

\begin{split}n_{-1}=&\frac{1}{2}\left[L-n_{0}+n_{2}+2n_{3}+\ldots+(H^{\prime}(L)-1)n_{H^{\prime}(L)}\right]-\frac{1}{2}H(L)\\
n_{1}=&\frac{1}{2}\left[L-n_{0}-3n_{2}-4n_{3}-\ldots-(H^{\prime}(L)+1)n_{H^{\prime}(L)}\right]+\frac{1}{2}H(L).\\
\end{split}

(76)

Our goal is to find
(n_{-1},n_{0},n_{1},\ldots)
that maximize the probability in Eqn. (
75
). We take the logarithm of the right-hand side of Eqn. (
75
), plug in the expression in Eqn. (
76
), and take the partial derivatives of the resulting expression in
n_{i}
. Then after taking
\frac{\partial}{\partial n_{i}}\ (i=0,2,3,\ldots)
we have:

\begin{split}0=&\log(p_{i}(N))-\frac{i+1}{2}\log(p_{1}(N))+\frac{i-1}{2}\log(p_{-1}(N))\\
-&\left[\log(n_{i})+\frac{n_{i}+\frac{1}{2}}{n_{i}}-\frac{i+1}{2}\log(n_{1})-\frac{n_{1}-\frac{1}{2}}{n_{1}}\frac{i+1}{2}+\frac{i-1}{2}\log(n_{-1})+\frac{n_{-1}+\frac{1}{2}}{n_{-1}}\frac{i-1}{2}\right]\\
=&\log(p_{i}(N))-\frac{i+1}{2}\log(p_{1}(N))+\frac{i-1}{2}\log(p_{-1}(N))\\
-&\Big[\log(\frac{n_{i}}{L})+\frac{n_{i}+\frac{1}{2}}{n_{i}}-\frac{i+1}{2}\log(\frac{n_{1}}{L})-\frac{n_{1}-\frac{1}{2}}{n_{1}}\frac{i+1}{2}+\frac{i-1}{2}\log(\frac{n_{-1}}{L})+\frac{n_{-1}+\frac{1}{2}}{n_{-1}}\frac{i-1}{2}\Big]\\
\approx&\log(p_{i}(N))-\frac{i+1}{2}\log(p_{1}(N))+\frac{i-1}{2}\log(p_{-1}(N))\\
-&\left[\log(n_{i})-\frac{i+1}{2}\log(n_{1})+\frac{i-1}{2}\log(n_{-1})\right],\end{split}

(77)

where in the approximation in the last line we take
\frac{n_{i}+\frac{1}{2}}{n_{i}}\approx 1
for
i=-1,0,1,2,\ldots

There are
H^{\prime}(L)-1
(approximately linear) equations with
H^{\prime}(L)-1
unknowns
\log(n_{0})
,
\log(n_{2}),\ldots,\log(n_{H^{\prime}(L)})
. We observe that for any fixed
i=0,2,\ldots

\frac{n_{i}}{L}\approx p_{i}(N)

solves Eqn. (
77
) as
L,N\rightarrow\infty
. Plugging these back to Eqn. (
76
) gives
\frac{n_{-1}}{L}\approx P_{-1}
.
It is straightforward to see from Eqn. (
75
) that as
L\rightarrow\infty
,
N\rightarrow\infty
the distribution function concentrates near its maximum, which in particular implies that for any
\delta>0
,

\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\frac{n_{-1}}{H^{\prime}(L)}-\mathbb{P}\left[X_{1}(N)=-1\right]\right|>\delta\right]\rightarrow 0.

Since
\rho_{-1}=\frac{n_{-1}}{L}
,
X_{1}(N)\rightarrow X_{1}
in distribution, the result follows.

∎

4.4
Tail estimate of the walks

We give several results about the extreme values of random walks, which will be used in the tail estimate argument in Section
5.3
.

Let

\mathcal{\tilde{W}}(0,\cdot,L;N)=\bigcup_{H=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{\tilde{W}}(0,H,L;N),\quad\mathcal{\tilde{W}}(\cdot,H,L;N)=\bigcup_{H^{\prime}=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{\tilde{W}}(H^{\prime},H,L;N).

Here,
\mathcal{\tilde{W}}(0,\cdot,L;N)
denotes the set of walks starting at
W(0)=0
and ending at an arbitrary
W(L)=H\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Similarly,
\mathcal{\tilde{W}}(\cdot,H,L;N)
denotes the set of walks starting at an arbitrary
W(0)=H^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and ending
5
5

5

One can view
W
as a time-reversed walk in this case.
at
W(L)=H
, for
H\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. These sets are equipped with the same increment weights as in Eqn. (
52
).

Lemma 4.8
.

For
H\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\ L\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 100}
, sample the walk
W(t)
from
\mathcal{\tilde{W}}(0,\cdot,L;N)
and
\mathcal{\tilde{W}}(\cdot,0,L;N)
respectively.
Then there exist constants
C>0
,
c>0
uniform in
L,N
, such that for all
h\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[W\geq 0,W(L)=\lfloor h\sqrt{L}\rfloor]\leq\frac{C}{L}\quad

\displaystyle\text{if}\ \ W(t)\in\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(0,\cdot,L;N),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[W\geq 0,W(0)=\lfloor h\sqrt{L}\rfloor]\leq\frac{C}{L}\exp(-ch^{2})\quad

\displaystyle\text{if}\ \ W(t)\in\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(\cdot,0,L;N).

Proof.

For
W(t)\in\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(0,\cdot,L;N)
or
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(\cdot,0,L;N)
we have
6
6

6

This can be obtained from the estimate of the first ladder epoch
T_{1}
, see e.g.
[
C
, (2.3) and (2.5)]
.

\mathbb{P}[W(t)\geq 0,0\leq t\leq L]\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{L}}
for some
C>0
. Then the first bound of the lemma is a direct corollary of
[
C
, Theorem 2]
. On the other hand, for
W(t)\in\tilde{W}(\cdot,0,L;N)
, by Proposition
A.1
we have

\frac{\mathbb{P}[W\geq 0,W(0)=\lfloor h\sqrt{L}\rfloor]}{\mathbb{P}[W(0)=\lfloor h\sqrt{L}\rfloor]}\leq\frac{Ch}{\sqrt{L}}.

Therefore it suffices to prove

\mathbb{P}[W(0)=\lfloor h\sqrt{L}\rfloor]\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{L}}\exp(-ch^{2}).

Note that since the increment
X(N)\geq-1
,
W(0)\leq L
. For
0\leq h\leq\rho\sqrt{L}
where
\rho>0
is some small enough constant, this follows directly from
[
LL
, Theorem 2.3.11]
. For
\rho\sqrt{L}\leq h\leq\sqrt{L}
, it suffices for us to prove

\mathbb{P}[W(0)=\lfloor h\sqrt{L}\rfloor]\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{L}}\exp(-cL).

This can be proved by slightly adapting the classical characteristic function argument in
[
LL
, Theorem 2.3.12]
, whose details we omit.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

For
L\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 100}
, take discrete intervals
[\![X,Y]\!]
contained in
[\![0,L]\!]
where
Y-X\geq 1
.
Consider three types of random walks
W(t):[\![0,L]\!]\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
as follows. Let
V=W(X)-\min_{[\![X,Y]\!]}W+1
.

(1). For
H\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, sample the walk
W(t)
in
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(\cdot,H,L;N)
.
Then there exist constants
C>0
,
c>0
, which are uniform in
H,L,N
, such that for all
h\geq 0
,
\tilde{h}\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}[W\geq 0,\ \max_{t}W(t)-H>h\sqrt{L},\ V>\tilde{h}\sqrt{Y-X}]\leq\begin{cases}C\exp(-ch^{2})\exp(-c\tilde{h}^{2}),\quad&\text{if}\ \ H>0\\
\frac{C}{\sqrt{L}}\exp(-ch^{2})\exp(-c\tilde{h}^{2}),\quad&\text{if}\ \ H=0.\end{cases}

(2). For
H\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, sample the walk from
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(\cdot,H,L,N)
.
Then there exist constants
C>0
,
c>0
, which are uniform in
L,N
, such that for all
h\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}[W(0)=0,\ W\geq 0,\ \max_{t}W(t)-H>h\sqrt{L},\ V>\tilde{h}\sqrt{Y-X}]\leq\frac{C}{L}\exp(-ch^{2})\exp(-c\tilde{h}^{2}).

(3). Sample the walk from
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(\cdot,0,L,N)
.
Then there exist constants
C>0
,
c>0
, which are uniform in
L,N
, such that for all
h\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}[W(0)=0,\ W\geq 0,\ \max_{t}W(t)>h\sqrt{L},\ V>\tilde{h}\sqrt{Y-X}]\leq\frac{C}{L^{3/2}}\exp(-ch^{2})\exp(-c\tilde{h}^{2}).

Proof.

In the proof we take
h\geq 1
, since for
0\leq h\leq 1
the factor
\exp(-ch^{2})
is absorbed in the constant
C
, and there is no need to introduce the quantity
T
below. Let

\displaystyle\tilde{T}=

\displaystyle\max_{X\leq t\leq Y}\{\tilde{t}:\ W(\tilde{t})=\min_{X\leq t\leq Y}W(t)\},

\displaystyle T=

\displaystyle\max\Big(\max_{0\leq t\leq X}\{t:\ W(t)=W(X)+\lfloor h\sqrt{L}/3\rfloor+1\},\max_{X+1\leq t\leq\tilde{T}}\{t:\ W(t)=W(\tilde{T})+\lfloor h\sqrt{L}/3\rfloor+1\},

\displaystyle\max_{\tilde{T}+1\leq t\leq L}\{t:\ W(t)=W(L)+\lfloor h\sqrt{L}/3\rfloor+1\},\Big),

and define the events

E_{T}=\{W\geq 0\ on\ \llbracket T,L\rrbracket\},

if they exist.

We first consider case (1),
H>0
. We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[W\geq 0,\ \max_{t}W(t)-H>h\sqrt{L},\ V>\tilde{h}\sqrt{Y-X}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[X\leq\tilde{T}<T\leq L|E_{T}]+\mathbb{P}[X\leq T<\tilde{T}\leq Y|E_{T}]+\mathbb{P}[T<X\leq\tilde{T}\leq L|E_{T}].

Note that we currently omit the factor
\mathbb{P}[E_{T}]
.
It then suffice to get the desired bound for all the above three
cases. Order the tuple
(X,\tilde{T},T)
as
(X_{1}\leq X_{2}\leq X_{3})
, and let
W_{1}(t)=W(t)|_{0\leq t\leq X_{1}}
,
W_{2}(t)=W(t)|_{X_{1}\leq t\leq X_{2}}
,
W_{3}(t)=W(t)|_{X_{2}\leq t\leq X_{3}}
,
W_{4}(t)=W(t)|_{X_{3}\leq t\leq L}
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[X\leq\tilde{T}<T\leq L|E_{T}]\leq\sum_{\tilde{t}=X}^{Y}\sum_{X_{3}=X_{2}+1}^{Y}\mathbb{P}[W_{2}(X_{1})-W_{2}(X_{2})>\tilde{h}\sqrt{Y-X}|X_{2}=\tilde{t},\ E_{T}]

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[X_{2}=\tilde{t}|E_{T}]\cdot\mathbb{P}[W_{4}\leq W_{4}(X_{3}),\ W_{4}(L)-W_{4}(X_{3})=-\lfloor h\sqrt{L}/3\rfloor|E_{T}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\tilde{t}=X}^{Y}\sum_{X_{3}=X_{2}+1}^{Y}\exp(-c\tilde{h}^{2})\cdot\mathbb{P}[X_{2}=\tilde{t}|E_{T}]\cdot\frac{C}{L}\exp(-ch^{2})\leq C\exp(-c\tilde{h}^{2})\exp(-ch^{2}),

where we use Lemma
4.8
in the second inequality.

Similarly,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[X\leq T<\tilde{T}\leq Y|E_{T}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\tilde{t}=X}^{Y}\sum_{X_{2}=X}^{\tilde{t}-1}\Big(\mathbb{P}[W_{3}(X_{2})\geq W_{3},\ W_{3}(X_{2})-W_{3}(X_{3})>\tilde{h}\sqrt{Y-X}/2,

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad W_{3}(X_{2})-W_{3}(X_{3})=\lfloor h\sqrt{L}/3\rfloor|X_{3}=\tilde{t},\ E_{T}]\cdot\mathbb{P}[X_{3}=\tilde{t}|E_{T}]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[W_{2}(X_{1})-W_{2}(X_{2})>\tilde{h}\sqrt{Y-X}/2|E_{T}]\times\mathbb{P}[W_{3}(X_{2})\geq W_{3},\ W_{3}(X_{2})-W_{3}(X_{3})=\lfloor h\sqrt{L}/3\rfloor|X_{3}=\tilde{t},\ E_{T}]

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[X_{3}=\tilde{t}|E_{T}]\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\tilde{t}=X}^{Y}\sum_{X_{2}=X}^{\tilde{t}-1}\frac{C}{L}\exp(-c\tilde{h}^{2})\exp(-ch^{2})\cdot\mathbb{P}[X_{3}=\tilde{t}|E_{T}]+\sum_{\tilde{t}=X}^{Y}\sum_{X_{2}=X}^{\tilde{t}-1}\exp(-c\tilde{h}^{2})\cdot\frac{C}{L}\exp(-ch^{2})\cdot\mathbb{P}[X_{3}=\tilde{t}|E_{T}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\exp(-c\tilde{h}^{2})\exp(-ch^{2}),

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[T<X\leq\tilde{T}\leq L|E_{T}]\leq\sum_{\tilde{t}=X}^{Y}\sum_{X_{1}=0}^{X-1}\mathbb{P}[W_{2}\leq W_{2}(X_{1}),\ W_{2}(X_{2})-W_{2}(X_{1}))=-\lfloor h\sqrt{L}/3\rfloor|W\geq 0]

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[W_{3}(X_{2})-W_{3}(X_{3})>\tilde{h}\sqrt{Y-X}|X_{3}=\tilde{t},\ E_{T}]\times\mathbb{P}[X_{3}=\tilde{t}|E_{T}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\tilde{t}=X}^{Y}\sum_{X_{1}=0}^{X-1}\frac{C}{L}\exp(-ch^{2})\cdot\exp(-c\tilde{h}^{2})\cdot\mathbb{P}[X_{3}=\tilde{t}|E_{T}]\leq C\exp(-c\tilde{h}^{2})\exp(-ch^{2}).

This finishes the proof for case (1),
H>0
.

For case (1) where
H=0
, the same argument as above still holds, while we upgrade the conditioning event
E_{T}
as
\{W\geq 0\ \text{on}\ \llbracket T,L\rrbracket|W(L)=0\}
, which gives one more factor

\mathbb{P}[W\geq 0\ \text{for}\ T\leq t\leq L|W(L)=0]\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{L}}.

For case (2), we still condition on
E_{T}
, and there is an extra restriction on
W(t)|_{0\leq t\leq T}

W(0)=0,\ W\geq 0\ \text{on}\ \llbracket 0,T\rrbracket\setminus\llbracket X,\tilde{T}\rrbracket,

which gives a factor
\leq\frac{C}{L}
to
\mathbb{P}[W\geq 0,\ \max_{t}W(t)-H>h\sqrt{L},\ V>\tilde{h}\sqrt{Y-X}|E_{T}]
by the second line of Lemma
4.8
.
For case (3) we have both the upgraded conditioning event and the extra restriction on
W(t)|_{0\leq t\leq T}
.
Combining the extra factors with the previous bounds gives all the desired estimates.
∎

Lemma 4.10
.

Take discrete intervals
[\![X_{1},Y_{1}]\!]
,…,
[\![X_{k},Y_{k}]\!]
contained in
[\![0,L]\!]
. Assume that
Y_{i}-X_{i}\geq 1
and that the intervals are mutually disjoint. For each
i=1,2,\ldots,k
, denote
V_{i}=W(X_{i})-\min_{[\![X_{i},Y_{i}]\!]}W+1
. Take
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and let
a=a_{1}+\ldots+a_{k}
. Consider the following two cases:

(1). For
H\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1},\ L\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, sample the walk
W(t)
in
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(\cdot,H,L;N)
.
Then there exist constants
C>0
,
c>0
uniform in
H,L,N
, such that for all
h\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{V_{i}^{a_{i}}}{a_{i}!}\mathbf{1}_{W\geq 0}\mathbf{1}_{\max W>h\sqrt{L}}\right]<C^{a}\log(a+2)^{a+2}a^{-\frac{a}{2}}L^{\frac{a}{2}}\exp(-ch^{2}),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{V_{i}^{a_{i}}}{a_{i}!}\mathbf{1}_{W\geq 0,\ W(0)=0}\mathbf{1}_{\max W>h\sqrt{L}}\right]<C^{a}\log(a+2)^{a+2}a^{-\frac{a}{2}}L^{\frac{a}{2}}\exp(-ch^{2})\cdot L^{-1}.

(2). Sample the walk
W(t)
in
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(\cdot,0,L;N)
.
Then there exist constants
C>0
,
c>0
uniform in
H,L,N
, such that for all
h\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{V_{i}^{a_{i}}}{a_{i}!}\mathbf{1}_{W\geq 0}\mathbf{1}_{\max W>h\sqrt{L}}\right]<C^{a}\log(a+2)^{a+2}a^{-\frac{a}{2}}L^{\frac{a}{2}}\exp(-ch^{2})\cdot L^{-1/2},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{V_{i}^{a_{i}}}{a_{i}!}\mathbf{1}_{W\geq 0,\ W(0)=0}\mathbf{1}_{\max W>h\sqrt{L}}\right]<C^{a}\log(a+2)^{a+2}a^{-\frac{a}{2}}L^{\frac{a}{2}}\exp(-ch^{2})\cdot L^{-3/2}.

Remark 4.11
.

A similar statement to this lemma is proved for Bernoulli walks, see
[
GXZ
, Lemma A.7]
. A key difference is that to deal with our walks, whose increment is unbounded in the upward direction, we define
V_{i}
to be
W(X_{i})-\min_{\llbracket X_{i},Y_{i}\rrbracket}W+1
instead of the larger quantity
\max_{\llbracket X_{i},Y_{i}\rrbracket}W-\min_{\llbracket X_{i},Y_{i}\rrbracket}W+1
, which is a sufficient upper bound for the number of last jumps (defined in Section
5.1
) in
\llbracket X_{i},Y_{i}\rrbracket
. We also make the observation that instead of considering the more general walk bridges in
\mathcal{W}(H_{1},H_{2},L;N)
for arbitrary
H_{1},H_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, if suffices to consider
H_{1}=0
, or consider a walk meander with undetermined left endpoint. This allows us to drop the drift term that appears in the upper bound of
[
GXZ
, Lemma A.7]
.

Proof.

We give the proof for the first bound in case (1), and the general cases follow from exactly the same argument.
Let
Z_{i}=\frac{V_{i}}{\sqrt{Y_{i}-X_{i}}}
for
i=1,2,\ldots,k
and let
Z=\max_{i}Z_{i}
. By Lemma
4.9
and a union bound in
i
we have
\mathbb{P}[Z>h]<Ck\exp(-ch^{2})
for all
h>0
. Therefore summing over
h=0,1,2,\ldots
gives

\mathbb{E}[Z^{m}]<(C\log(k)m)^{m/2}\leq(C\log(a)m)^{m/2}

for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

Upper bound each
V_{i}
by
Z\sqrt{Y_{i}-X_{i}}
. Then by the above inequality, there exists constant
C>0
uniform in
H,L,N
,
W(Y_{i})
, such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}V_{i}^{a_{i}}\right]\leq C^{a}\log(a+2)^{a+2}\prod_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}^{a_{i}/2}(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{a_{i}/2}.

Using the bound
a_{i}!\geq c^{a_{i}}a_{i}^{a_{i}}
for some uniform constant
c>0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{V_{i}^{a_{i}}}{a_{i}!}\right]\leq C^{a}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left(\frac{Y_{i}-X_{i}}{a_{i}}\right)^{a_{i}/2}.

An elementary calculation shows that

\prod_{i=1}^{k}(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{a_{i}/2}\leq\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}^{a_{i}/2}}{a^{a/2}}L^{a/2}.

Therefore

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{V_{i}^{a_{i}}}{a_{i}!}\right]\leq C^{a}\log(a+2)^{a+2}a^{-a/2}L^{a/2}.

∎

5
Asymptotics

Recall from Section
3.1
that we are considering the action

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mu_{+}(N)^{M_{1}+\ldots+M_{l}}}\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{i_{l}}}{N}\right)^{M_{l}}\cdots\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{i_{1}}}{N}\right)^{M_{1}}G_{N}(\vec{x};\beta)\Bigg|_{\vec{x}=0}=\sum_{(E,\vec{q})\in\mathcal{B}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})}w(E,\vec{q}),

where the right-hand side is a weighted sum over the configurations. In this section, we calculate the asymptotics of the right-hand side as
N\to\infty
, and then sum over all the tuples
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})
. Our limit expression is given as a functional of the conditional Brownian bridges, see Section
5.5
.

5.1
Classifying local configurations

Fix
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})
. We will further classify the configurations in
\mathcal{B}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})
according to their local behavior near each
C_{j}
,
j=1,2,\ldots,l-1
.
The total weights of configurations with different local behaviors are of different orders when
N\rightarrow\infty
, and only a subset of them make a nontrivial contribution in the limit. In this section, we provide a list that completely classifies the possible local behaviors for our further analysis.
For
i\in\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}\}
, let

a_{i}=\min\{j=1,2,\ldots,l:i_{j}=i\},\quad b_{i}=\max\{j=1,2,\ldots,l:i_{j}=i\}.

(78)

Let
\tau_{i}
be a stopping time such that

\tau_{i}=\min\{t:E(C_{a_{i}-1}+t)=E(C_{a_{i}-1})-1,0<t\leq\left\lfloor\frac{1}{4}(C_{a_{i}}-C_{a_{i}-1})\right\rfloor\},

(79)

i.e., the waiting time after
C_{a_{i}-1}
until the walk goes below
E(C_{a_{i}-1})
. If the set is empty, we set
\tau_{i}=\varnothing
.
Let

\tilde{\tau}_{i}=\min_{t>0}\{t:q(C_{a_{i}-1}+t)\neq q(C_{a_{i}-1}+t-)\},

(80)

i.e., the first jump time after
C_{a_{i}-1}
at which a jump occurs. And we set
\tilde{\tau}_{i}=\varnothing
if there is no jump after time
C_{a_{i}-1}
. These two quantities characterize the behavior of the configuration locally on the right of
C_{a_{i}-1}
, or, roughly speaking, how
x_{i}
enters the configuration.

More precisely, for each main variable
x_{i}
, we classify
x_{i}
in the following types, according to the local behavior on the right of
C_{a_{i}-1}
:

•

Type I:
\tau_{i}\neq\varnothing
, and
7
7

7

This implies that
E(C_{a_{i}-1})>q(C_{a_{i}-1})
.

\tau_{i}<\tilde{\tau}_{i}
or
\tilde{\tau}_{i}=\varnothing
.

•

Type II: No jump of
x_{i}
before time
C_{a_{i}-1}
, hence
E(C_{a_{i}-1})=q(C_{a_{i}-1})
. Moreover,
\tau_{i}=\tilde{\tau}_{i}<\infty
, and for some auxiliary variable
x_{j}
,
q_{j}(C_{a_{i}-1}+\tau_{i}-)>0
,
q_{j}(t)=0
for all
t\geq C_{a_{i}-1}+\tau_{i}
.

•

Type III: No jump of
x_{i}
before time
C_{a_{i}-1}
, hence
E(C_{a_{i}-1})=q(C_{a_{i}-1})
. Moreover,
\tau_{i}=\tilde{\tau}_{i}<\infty
, and for some main variable
x_{j}
,
q_{j}(C_{a_{i}-1}+\tau_{i}-)>0
,
q_{j}(t)=0
for all
8
8

8

In Type II and III,
x_{j}
has its last jump at time
C_{a_{i}-1}+\tau_{i}
.

t\geq C_{a_{i}-1}+\tau_{i}
.

•

Type IV:
\tilde{\tau}_{i}<\tau_{i}
, or
\tilde{\tau}_{i}\neq\varnothing
and
\tau_{i}=\varnothing
.

•

Type V:
\tau_{i}=\tilde{\tau}_{i}=\varnothing
.

Types I to V give a disjoint collection of local behavior on the right of
C_{a_{i}-1}
, while there are still some uncovered situations, which we collect in Type VI. More precisely,
x_{i}
is in Type VI if
\tau_{i}=\tilde{\tau}_{i}\neq\varnothing
, and one of the following happens

•

Type VI.1: No jump of
x_{i}
before time
C_{a_{i}-1}
, hence
E(C_{a_{i}-1})=q(C_{a_{i}-1})
. Moreover,
\tau_{i}=\tilde{\tau}_{i}<\infty
, and for some main or auxiliary variable
x_{j}
,
q_{j}(C_{a_{i}-1}+\tau_{i}-)>q_{j}(C_{a_{i}-1}+\tau_{i})>0
. In other words, there is a jump of
x_{j}
at time
C_{a_{i}-1}+\tau_{i}
, which does
not
send
q_{j}
to 0.

•

Type VI.2:
E(C_{a_{i}-1})>q(C_{a_{i}-1})
.

•

Type VI.3:
E(C_{a_{i}-1})=q(C_{a_{i}-1})
, while there were jumps of
x_{i}
before time
C_{a_{i}-1}
.

See Figure
2
for an illustration of the different cases.

An illustration of the classification of main variables into types I-VI. In each figure the walk
E(t)
is drawn in black, the sum of blocks
q(t)
is drawn in blue, and a particular block
q_{j}
is drawn in red if relevant.

Top:
The left shows Type I in which
\tau_{i}<\tilde{\tau}_{i}
. The right shows Types II and III in which
\tau_{i}=\tilde{\tau}_{i}
. The difference between the two types depends on whether the block
q_{j}
, drawn in red, belongs to an auxiliary variable (Type II) or a main variable (Type III).

Middle:
The left shows Type IV in which
\tilde{\tau}_{i}<\tau_{i}
. The right shows Type V in which both
\tau_{i}
and

are

. Note that the illustrations show that
E(C_{a_{i}-1})>q(C_{a_{i}-1})
but
E(C_{a_{i}-1})=q(C_{a_{i}-1})
is also allowed for these types.

Bottom:
The illustrations for the three subtypes of Type VI. In each of the subtypes
\tau_{i}=\tilde{\tau}_{i}
. In Type VI.1, the block
q_{j}
drawn in red can be any main or auxiliary variable. In Type VI.3 the red block
q_{i}
represents variable
x_{i}
.

We will introduce a cancellation between Type I and Type II in Section
5.2
, to resolve the blow-up issue related to the weight given by these two types. Types III, IV, and V give nontrivial contributions to the limit, but to simplify the limiting expression, we perform another cancellation of terms of Type III in Section
5.4
. One can think of each of Type VI.1-3 as certain “atypical” deformations of Types I to V, and we show in Section
5.5
that the contribution of all of these vanishes in the limit.

For
i\in\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}\}
, we also give another classification about how
x_{i}
is removed during the action. More precisely, we say that
x_{i}
is of Type A, B, or C, according to the following rules:

•

Type A:
q_{i}(t)=0
for all
t\geq C_{b_{i}}
.

•

Type B: for some main variable
x_{j}
of Type III, and
a_{j}-1\geq b_{i}
,
q_{i}(C_{a_{j}}+\tau_{j}-)>0
,
q_{i}(t)=0
for all
t\geq C_{a_{j}}+\tau_{j}
.

•

Type C: all the remaining cases.

See Figure
3
for an illustration.

We will show that Type A gives nontrivial contributions to the limit in Section
5.5
, while the total weight of configurations with some main variable of Type C vanishes as
N\rightarrow\infty
. Each
x_{i}
of Type B is paired with some
x_{j}
of Type III, so the weight of a configuration with any main variable of Type B is canceled out in Section
5.4
.

C_{b_{i}}
Type A

C_{a_{j}-1}
\tau_{j}=\tilde{\tau}_{j}
Type B

Figure 3:
An illustration of Types A and B. In both cases the block
q_{i}
is drawn in red.

Let
(j_{1},\ldots,j_{m})
be the
m
-tuple that gives the indices in
\{i_{1}\}\cup\ldots\cup\{i_{l}\}
without repetition and ordered according to their first appearance in
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})
. Let
J=\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{m}\}
be the set of main variable indices, and let
J_{I},\ldots,J_{VI.3},\ J_{A},\ldots,J_{C}
be the set of main variable indices of the corresponding type. We call the tuple

I(E,\vec{q})=(I_{1},I_{2})=(I_{1,1},\ldots,I_{1,m},I_{2,1},\ldots,I_{2,m})\in\{I,II,\ldots,VI\}^{m}\times\{A,B,C\}^{m}

(81)

the
index
of
(E,\vec{q})
, where each
I_{1,p},I_{2,p}
gives the type of
x_{j_{p}}
.

As a preparation, we define the following notions for the jumps in a given configuration. Let
U
be the set of auxiliary variable indices. For
i\in U
or
i\in J_{B}\cup J_{C}
, let

l_{i}=\max\{t:q_{i}(t-)>0\}.

(82)

For
i\in J_{VI.3}
, let

l^{\prime}_{i}=\max\{t\leq C_{a_{i}-1}:q_{i}(t)>0\}.

(83)

We call the jumps at time
l_{i}
and
l^{\prime}_{i}
the
last jumps
of
x_{i}
, and denote the set of last jump times of
x_{i}s,\ i\in J\cup U
by
\mathcal{L}
. By definition,
|\mathcal{L}|=|U|+|J_{B}|+|J_{C}|+|J_{VI.3}|
.

Similarly, let

f_{i}=\begin{cases}\min\{t:\ q_{i}(t)>0\},\quad&i\in U;\\
\min\{t\leq C_{a_{i}-1}:q_{i}(t)>0\},\quad&i\in J.\end{cases}

We call
f_{i}
a
first jump
of
x_{i}
, and denote the set of first jump times of the variables
x_{i}
,
i\in J\cup U
by
\mathcal{F}
. Note that for
i\in J
,
x_{i}
might not have a first jump. Denote the total number of first jumps of
x_{i}s,\ i\in J
by
f_{J}
, so that by definition
|\mathcal{F}|=|U|+f_{J}
. We say a jump is a
free jump
if it is not a first or last jump, and denote the set of free jump times by
\mathcal{R}
.

Recall that
\Delta
denotes the set of all jump times. If
x_{i}
is of Type A, let

\eta_{i}=\max\{C_{b_{i}}-\left\lfloor\frac{1}{4}(C_{b_{i}}-C_{b_{i}-1})\right\rfloor\leq t<C_{b_{i}}:t\in\Delta\setminus\mathcal{L}\}.

(84)

If the set is empty, we set
\eta_{i}=\varnothing
.

The classifications we give in this section are the same as those in the concurrent work
[
GXZ
, Section 4.4]
. While in this text we deal with more complicated walk-bridges, the only negative increments of the walk are still
-1
, and their scaling limits are still Brownian bridges. This allows the two works to share some common techniques.

5.2
Blowing up terms and cancellations

In this section, we introduce an equivalence relation between configurations created by the same Dunkl action as in Eqn. (
42
), but with different indices.
The reason for doing this is that, if the configuration has at least one main variable of Type I or II, then the total weight of configurations
(E,\vec{q})
with possibly different
E
and a fixed index will blow up in the limit. This will be clearer by looking at the following example.

Example 5.1
.

Let us consider the case when
l=2
. We are interested in the action of

\frac{N(N-1)}{\mu_{+}(N)^{2M}}\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{2}}{N}\right)^{M}\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{1}}{N}\right)^{M}

on the BGF. First, let us consider the case in which there are no jumps. In this case, the index
I_{1}=(V,V)
, and the walk must hit zero at the
M
-th step as shown in the figure below with
M=14
.

Now consider the case in which we have exactly one jump. There are two possibilities.
The jump could add powers of
x_{2}
in the section where
x_{1}
is the main variable, in which case the index is (V,I), and the height of the
x_{2}
block must be equal to the height of the walk at time
M
. Or we could have a jump removing leftover powers of
x_{1}
in the section where
x_{2}
is the main variable, in which case the index is (V,III). Examples of the two possibilities are given below with the block drawn in dashed blue.

}

One can check that as
M=O(N^{2/3}),N\rightarrow\infty
, the total weight of configurations with index
I_{1}=(V,I)
is of order
O(\sqrt{M})
, and the total weight of configurations with
I_{1}=(V,III)
is of order
O(1)
. The former weight blows up in the limit.

To resolve this issue, we say that a configuration

(E,\vec{q})\in\mathcal{I}\quad\text{if}\quad|J_{II}|=0.

A configuration
(E,\vec{q})
is
grouped with
(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{I}
, written as
(E,\vec{q})\sim(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})
, if one can obtain
(E,\vec{q})
from
(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})
by the following procedure.
For all the main variables
x_{i}
with
i\in J_{II}
, let
x_{j}
denote the unique variable such that
q_{j}(C_{a_{i}-1}+\tau_{i}-)>0
,
q_{j}(t)=0
for all
t\geq C_{a_{i}-1}+\tau_{i}
. Remove the last jump of
x_{j}
at time
C_{a_{i}-1}+\tau_{i}
, and replace all of the remaining jumps of
x_{j}
by jumps of
x_{i}
. Note that
i\in J_{II}
in
(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})
implies that
i\in J_{I}
in
(E,\vec{q})
.

From the above algorithm, one can see that each
(E,\vec{q})\in\mathcal{I}
provides an equivalence class of configurations
(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})\sim(E,\vec{q})
, indexed by the
2^{|J_{I}|}
elements in
\{I,II\}^{|J_{I}|}
. Moreover, each
(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})
is grouped with a unique element in
\mathcal{I}
. If a main variable
x_{i}
is of Type II in
(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})
, the extra jump at time
C_{a_{i}-1}+\tau_{i}
gives a minus sign in the weight, so the weights of the grouped configurations are of opposite sign. Because of this, the inner cancellation of weights in each equivalence class resolves the blow-up issue. We give a more detailed discussion in the next section.

Example 5.2
.

The configurations with
I_{1}=(V,I)
in Example
5.1
will be grouped with configurations that have two jumps, with
I_{1}=(V,II)
, which looks as follows:

Here the red dashed line indicates the block for some
x_{i}
,
i\neq 1,2
. Note that to the left of
C_{1}
we have kept the same jump location and height (but have changed the index) and we have added a new jump to the right of
C_{1}
at the unique location that can send the block to zero. In addition, the weights of these two types of configurations are of opposite signs, and their sum is of order
O(1)
when
N\rightarrow\infty
.

5.3
Tail estimates

In this section, we prove the main technical lemma of this text, which plays two roles. Firstly, it shows that the weight contribution of a configuration decays exponentially, hence it bounds the tail of Eqn. (
50
) asymptotically. Secondly, the exponent of
N
shows that the contribution of configurations with Type VI local behavior is of smaller order in
N
, while configurations with Type I local behavior cause a blow-up issue.

For a configuration
(E,\vec{q})\in\mathcal{B}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})
, we refer to the following collection as the
characters
of
(E,\vec{q})
.:

•

I(E,\vec{q})
, the index of
(E,\vec{q})
defined in Eqn. (
81
).

•

\tau_{j_{1}},\ldots,\tau_{j_{m}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\cup\varnothing
.

•

\eta_{j_{p}}
defined in Eqn. (
84
) for
j_{p}\in J_{A}
, and
J_{\varnothing,A}
, the set of
j_{p}
where
\eta_{j_{p}}=\varnothing
.

Lemma 5.3
.

There exists a constant
C>1
that depends on
\mathbf{k}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{k}_{l}
and
\sigma
, and is uniform in
N
, such that for all
N\geq 100
,

\begin{split}\sum_{(E,\vec{q})\in\mathcal{B}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})\cap\mathcal{I}}\left|w(E,\vec{q})\right|\leq&C\prod_{j_{p}\in J_{A}:\eta_{j_{p}}\neq\varnothing}(C_{b_{j_{p}}}-\eta_{j_{p}}+1)^{-1/2}\cdot\prod_{p:j_{p}\in J_{IV}}\tau_{j_{p}}^{-1/2}\prod_{p:j_{p}\in J_{I}\cup J_{III}\cup J_{VI}}\tau_{j_{p}}^{-3/2}\\
\times&N^{-m}N^{-\frac{1}{3}(|J_{A}|-|J_{\varnothing,A}|)}N^{-\frac{1}{3}|J_{IV}|}N^{\frac{1}{3}|J_{I}|-\frac{1}{3}|J_{VI}|-\frac{1}{3}|J_{VI.3}|-\frac{1}{3}|J_{C}|},\end{split}

(85)

where the sum is over all configurations with given characters.

Proof.

We divide the proof into the following three parts.

Part I. Enumerations

We enumerate the total weight of the configurations with the given characters and the following fixed data:

•

\delta
, the total number of jumps,

•

|U|
, the number of auxiliary variables, and

•

k_{p}
, the number of jumps of
x_{j_{p}}
for
p=1,2,\ldots,m
.

Recall that the contribution of each walk/block configuration can be both positive or negative, and by counting without regard to the sign we get a summation of their absolute values. To do this we bound the count of all the data that must be determined in order to specify a walk/block configuration consistent with the above data. This is done in several steps.

0. Choose
|U|
auxiliary variables from
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}
. The number of choices is bounded by

\binom{N}{|U|}\leq\frac{N^{|U|}}{|U|!}.

(86)

1. Determine all the first and free jump times. In particular, for the main variables we determine the following.

•

When
j_{p}\in J_{VI}
, we determine whether there is a jump of
x_{i},\ i\in J
at
C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+\tau_{j_{p}}
(if yes, then its jump time is fixed), and we denote the total number of such jumps by
|J_{m,VI}|
.

•

When
j_{p}\in J_{A}
, we determine whether there is a jump of
x_{i},\ i\in J
at
\eta_{j_{p}}
(if yes, then its jump time is fixed), and we denote the total number of such jumps by
|J_{m,A}|
.

Let
k^{\prime}_{p}\leq k_{p}
be the number of first and free jump times of
x_{j_{p}}
not fixed by the above items.

For each auxiliary variable
x_{i}
,
i\in U
, we determine the first jump time of
x_{i}
. Then we determine the free jump times of all the auxiliary variables
x_{i}
without specifying their corresponding variables. In particular, for
p=1,2,\ldots,m
we determine the following.

•

When
j_{p}\in J_{VI}
, we determine whether there is a jump of
x_{i},\ i\in U
at
C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+\tau_{j_{p}}
(if yes, then its jump time is fixed), and we denote the total number of such jumps by
|J_{a,VI}|
. By definition,
|J_{m,VI}|+|J_{a,VI}|=|J_{VI}|
.

•

When
j_{p}\in J_{A}
, we determine whether there is a jump of
x_{i},\ i\in U
at
\eta_{j_{p}}
(if yes, then its jump time is fixed), and we denote the total number of such jumps by
|J_{a,A}|
. By definition,
|J_{m,A}|+|J_{a,A}|=|J_{A}|-|J_{\varnothing,A}|
.

There are no more than
(l+1)^{2l}
possible cases in the above four items, so we just consider an arbitrary case of them. Also note that for
j_{p}\in J_{IV}
, there is at least one first/free jump between
C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}
and
C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+\tau_{j_{p}}
. Therefore
9
9

9

There are also restrictions that

•

There is no jump in
\llbracket C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+1,C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+\tau_{j_{p}}\rrbracket
if
x_{j_{p}}
is of Type I and III. There is no jump in
\llbracket C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+1,C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+\tilde{\tau}_{j_{p}}-1\rrbracket
if
x_{j_{p}}
is of Type VI. There is no jump in
\llbracket C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+1,C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+\left\lfloor\frac{1}{4}(C_{a_{j_{p}}}-C_{a_{j_{p}}-1})\right\rfloor\rrbracket
if
x_{j_{p}}
is of Type V.

•

There is no jump in
\llbracket\eta_{j_{p}}+1,C_{b_{j_{p}}}\rrbracket
.

But we do not take these restrictions into account when giving the bound.
,
the total number of choices of the first and free jump times not fixed by the above is bounded by

\begin{split}&\prod_{p:\ j_{p}\in J_{IV}}\tau_{j_{p}}\cdot\binom{M}{\underbrace{1,\ldots,1}_{|U|},k_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,k_{m}^{\prime},\delta-\sum_{p=1}^{m}k^{\prime}_{p}-2|U|-|J_{IV}|-|J_{VI}|-|J_{m,A}|-|J_{a,A}|-|J_{B}|-|J_{C}|}\\
\leq&\prod_{p:\ j_{p}\in J_{IV}}\tau_{j_{p}}\cdot\frac{M^{\delta-|J_{A}|+|J_{\varnothing,A}|-|J_{B}|-|J_{C}|-|U|-|J_{IV}|-|J_{VI}|}}{(k_{1}-l-1)!\cdots(k_{m}-l-1)!(\delta-\sum_{p=1}^{m}k_{p}-2|U|-2l)!},\end{split}

(87)

where here and below we take
n!=1
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
.

2. For
p=1,2,\ldots,m
, let

X_{j_{p}}=\begin{cases}C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+\tau_{j_{p}}\quad\quad&\text{if}\ \ \tau_{j_{p}}\neq\varnothing,\\
C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+\left\lfloor\frac{1}{4}(C_{a_{j_{p}}}-C_{a_{j_{p}}-1})\right\rfloor\quad&\text{if}\ \ \tau_{j_{p}}=\varnothing\end{cases}

and let

Y_{j_{p}}=\begin{cases}\eta_{j_{p}},&\text{if}\ \eta_{j_{p}}\neq\varnothing,\\
C_{b_{j_{p}}}-\left\lfloor\frac{1}{4}(C_{b_{j_{p}}}-C_{b_{j_{p}}-1})\right\rfloor,\quad&\text{if}\ \eta_{j_{p}}=\varnothing.\end{cases}

Split
\llbracket 0,M\rrbracket
into disjoint segments by
C_{0},\ldots,C_{l}
, the points
X_{j_{p}}
and the points
Y_{j_{p}}
. Denote the segments by
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
, ordered backward in time. More precisely, all the above positions (except
C_{0}=0
) serve as the right boundary of the segment on its left. Note that
n\leq 3l
.

Split
\llbracket 0,M\rrbracket
into finer disjoint intervals by
C_{0},\ldots,C_{l}
,
X_{j_{p}}
,
Y_{j_{p}}
, all the first and free jump times of
x_{i}
,
i\in J\cup U
. Denote these intervals by
\tilde{S}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{S}_{\tilde{n}}
, and the left endpoint of
\tilde{S}_{\tilde{k}}
by
t_{\tilde{k}}
. More precisely, all the above positions (except
C_{0}=0
) serve as the right boundary of the interval on its left. Note that the intervals are subdivisions of the segments
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
, and

\delta-|U|-l\leq\tilde{n}\leq\delta-|U|+4l.

Recall the definitions of last jump from Eqn. (
82
) and (
83
). We make two determinations about the last jump
10
10

10

The last jump times should occur after the corresponding first jumps, but we do not take this restriction into account in this proof.
times. First, we determine the number of last jumps in each segment
\tilde{S}_{k}
. Second, we determine the order of these last jump times. The total number of choices is bounded by

\binom{\tilde{n}-1+|U|+2l}{|U|+2l}(|U|+2l)!\leq\frac{(\delta+6l-1)!}{(\delta-|U|+4l-1)!}.

(88)

3. For
k=1,2,\ldots,n
, starting from
k=1
(the segment ending at
M
), sample a walk
E_{k}(t):\{E(t)\}_{t\in S_{k}}
backward on
S_{k}
, which is compatible with all the “jump time data” determined in the previous steps. We view each segment of
E(t)
as a random walk with fixed right endpoint, and use Lemma
4.9
to bound the partition function

Par(k):=\sum_{E_{k}(t)}\prod_{l\geq 0}P_{l}(N)^{\#t:\ E_{k}(t)-E_{k}(t-)=l-1}

of the walk bridge. More precisely, let
C>0
be a large enough constant uniform in
N
,

•

E(t)\geq E(C_{C_{b_{j_{p}}}})
on
[Y_{j_{p}},C_{b_{j_{p}}}]
for all
x_{j_{p}}
of Type A. Then by Lemma
4.9
(1),

Par(k)\leq C\cdot(C_{b_{j_{p}}}-Y_{j_{p}}+1)^{-1/2}\leq\begin{cases}C\cdot N^{-1/3},\quad&j_{p}\in J_{\varnothing,A};\\
C\cdot(C_{b_{j_{p}}}-Y_{j_{p}}+1)^{-1/2},\quad&j_{p}\in J\setminus J_{\varnothing,A}\end{cases}.

(89)

•

E(t)\geq E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1})
on
[C_{a_{j_{p}}-1},C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+\left\lfloor\frac{1}{4}(C_{a_{j_{p}}}-C_{a_{j_{p}}-1})\right\rfloor]
for all
x_{j_{p}}
of Type V. Then by Lemma
4.9
(2),

\begin{split}Par(k)\leq\begin{cases}C\cdot N^{-2/3},\quad&\text{if\ there\ is\ no\ first\ jump\ of\ }x_{j_{p}};\\
C\cdot N^{-1/3},\quad&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}\end{split}

(90)

The first inequality holds
11
11

11

In particular, this is the case for
Par_{n}
since
E(0)=0
.
since, in this case, the value of
E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1})
is fixed to be
q\left(C_{a_{j_{p}-1}}+\left\lfloor\frac{1}{4}(C_{a_{i}}-C_{a_{i}-1})\right\rfloor\right)
. Denote the number of
S_{k}
such that the first case above holds by
\delta_{1}
.

•

E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1})=E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+\tau_{j_{p}}-1)=E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+\tau_{j_{p}})+1
when
\tau_{i}\neq\varnothing
. Then by Lemma
4.9
(3),

Par(k)\leq C\cdot\tau_{j_{p}}^{-3/2}.

(91)

•

E(t)=E(t-1)-1
at each jump time
t
. This further bounds the partition functions by a factor less than 1, which we ignore in this proof.

.

4. Moving backward in time, determine the height of all last jumps, and the height of all first and free jumps of auxiliary variables, in each segment
\tilde{S}_{\tilde{k}}\subset S_{k}
.

5. Repeat Steps 3 and 4 in segments
S_{k+1}
until
k=n
. Note that the walks on each segment are mutually independent, conditioned on the boundary heights.

Now we bound the total number of choices of jump heights, given the walk
E(t)
. On each
\tilde{S}_{\tilde{k}}
, denote the number of last jumps by
u(\tilde{k})
. Then the number of choices of last jump heights on each
\tilde{S}_{\tilde{k}}
is bounded by

\frac{[E(t_{\tilde{k}})-\min_{t\in\tilde{S}_{\tilde{k}}}E(t)]^{u^{\prime}(\tilde{k})}}{u^{\prime}(\tilde{k})!},

where
u^{\prime}(\tilde{k})=u(\tilde{k})
or
u(\tilde{k})-1
is the “degree of freedom” of the last jump heights.
u^{\prime}(\tilde{k})=u(\tilde{k})-1
when one of the last jump heights is fixed by the restriction
E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1})=q(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1})
for some
j_{p}\in J_{III}\cup J_{VI.3}
.

As for the choices of each first or free jump height and its corresponding variable, there are three cases:

•

no freedom if it is a first jump;

•

if it is not a first jump, its number of choices is bounded by
\max_{0\leq t\leq M}E(t)
; and

•

in some of the configurations, one of the free jump heights is fixed by the restriction
E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1})=q(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1})
for some
j_{p}\in J_{III}\cup J_{IV}\cup J_{V}\cup J_{VI.1}\cup J_{VI.3}
. Then there are at most
|U|
choices of this jump, given by the
|U|
choices of its jump variable.

Let
\delta_{2}
be the degree of freedom loss of the free jumps,
\delta_{3}=\sum_{\tilde{k}=1}^{\tilde{n}}u(\tilde{k})-u^{\prime}(\tilde{k})
be the total degree of freedom loss of the last jumps. Note that by definition
\delta_{2}\leq m\leq l
, and

\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}+\delta_{3}+f_{J}=m+|J_{VI.3}|.

(92)

Then we obtain the factor:

|U|^{\delta_{2}}[\max_{0\leq t\leq M}E(t)]^{|\mathcal{R}|-\delta_{2}}\prod_{\tilde{k}=1}^{\tilde{n}}\frac{[E(t_{\tilde{k}})-\min_{t\in S_{\tilde{k}}}E(t)]^{u^{\prime}(\tilde{k})}}{u^{\prime}(\tilde{k})!}.

(93)

Part II. Taking expectations

Recall the weight of each configuration from Eqn. (
50
). First note that for a walk
E(t)
such that

N^{-\frac{1}{3}}\max_{0\leq t\leq M}E(t)\in[h,h+1),

we have

N^{-\delta}\prod_{t:\ t+1\in D(E)}\left(\frac{m_{i}(t)}{N}+2\frac{E(t)-q(t)}{\beta N}\right)\leq N^{-\delta}\left(1+\frac{\max_{0\leq t\leq M}E(t)}{N}\right)^{M}\leq N^{-\delta}\exp(Ch).

(94)

Now we take the weighted sum over
E(t)
as an expectation on the conditional walk bridges described above. Denote
\max_{t\in S_{k}}E(t)=h_{k}N^{\frac{1}{3}}
, then by Lemma
4.9
, among any possible conditioning at
C_{i}
, there exists some uniform constant
C>0,\ c>0
, such that
\mathbb{P}[h\leq h_{k}<h+1]\leq C\exp(-ch^{2})
. To deal with the second part of Eqn. (
93
), we apply
12
12

12

The purpose of introducing this lemma is to get an upper bound uniformly in
\delta
and
\tilde{n}
, because the bound only depends on
n
, the number of segments
S_{k}
, which is still uniformly bounded.
Lemma
4.10
. Denote
u^{\prime}(k)=\sum_{\tilde{k}:\ \tilde{S}_{\tilde{k}}\subset S_{k}}u^{\prime}(\tilde{k})
the degree of freedom of the last jump heights in
S_{k}
. We combine the terms corresponding to segments
\tilde{S}_{\tilde{k}}
in the same segment
S_{k}
, and give an upper bound of Eqn. (
93
)

|U|^{\delta_{2}}(hN^{1/3})^{|\mathcal{R}|-\delta_{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{n}C^{u^{\prime}(k)}\log(u^{\prime}(k)+2)^{u^{\prime}(k)+2}u^{\prime}(k)^{-u^{\prime}(k)/2}(N^{1/3})^{u^{\prime}(k)}\cdot C\exp(-ch^{2}),

(95)

where we sum over the walks with
N^{-\frac{1}{3}}\max_{0\leq t\leq M}E(t)\in[h,h+1)
.

Further, to upper bound the product

\prod_{k=1}^{n}C^{u^{\prime}(k)}\log(u^{\prime}(k)+2)^{u^{\prime}(k)+2}u^{\prime}(k)^{-u^{\prime}(k)/2},

(96)

note that at least one
u^{\prime}(k)>\frac{|U|}{3l}
, and for this
u^{\prime}(k)
we have
13
13

13

We get 1 when the exponent
|U|
is 0.

u^{\prime}(k)^{-u^{\prime}(k)/2}\leq\left(\frac{3l}{|U|}\right)^{\frac{|U|}{6l}}.

(97)

For other
u^{\prime}(k)
, simply bound
u^{\prime}(k)^{-u^{\prime}(k)/2}
by 1.

Therefore, using the fact that
\sum_{k=1}^{n}u^{\prime}(k)+2\leq 2|J|+|U|
, we can bound Eqn. (
96
) by (taking
h\geq\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
)

C^{|U|}\log(2|J|+|U|)^{2|J|+|U|}\left(\frac{3l}{|U|}\right)^{\frac{|U|}{6l}}.

(98)

Using Eqn. (
98
), we multiply Eqn. (
95
) by Eqn. (
94
) then sum over
h\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. To simplify the notations, let

u_{1}=\frac{|U|}{6l},\ \text{and}\ u_{2}=|\mathcal{R}|-\delta_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

Replace
\sum_{k=1}^{n}u^{\prime}(k)
by
\mathfrak{L}-\delta_{3}
, where
|\mathcal{L}|
is the total number of last jumps. Then the product is a sum of three terms of the following form:

\begin{split}&|U|^{\delta_{2}}(N^{1/3})^{|\mathcal{R}|-\delta_{2}+|\mathcal{L}|-\delta_{3}}C^{|U|}\log(2|J|+|U|)^{2|J|+|U|}(3l)^{u_{1}}\sum_{h\geq 0}h^{u_{2}}(|U|)^{-u_{1}}\exp(Ch)\exp(-ch^{2})\\
\lesssim&|U|^{\delta_{2}}(N^{1/3})^{|\mathcal{R}|-\delta_{2}+|\mathcal{L}|-\delta_{3}}\log(2|U|)^{2|U|}C^{u_{2}}\sqrt{(u_{2})!}(|U|)^{-u_{1}}(3l)^{u_{1}}\\
\lesssim&|U|^{\delta_{2}}(N^{1/3})^{|\mathcal{R}|-\delta_{2}+|\mathcal{L}|-\delta_{3}}\log(2|U|)^{2|U|}C^{u_{2}}\left(\frac{u_{2}}{e}\right)^{u_{2}/2}(u_{2})^{\frac{1}{4}}(|U|)^{-u_{1}}(3l)^{u_{1}}.\end{split}

(99)

Part III. Combining the factors

We multiply all the remaining factors obtained from Steps 0 to 5, namely, multiplying Eqn. (
99
) by Eqn. (
86
), (
87
), (
88
), and the proper copies of Eqn. (
89
), (
90
) and (
91
). Then up to a constant independent of the characters, this gives an upper bound

\begin{split}&N^{-\delta}\frac{N^{|U|}}{|U|!}\prod_{j_{p}\in J_{A}:\eta_{j_{p}}\neq\varnothing}(C_{b_{j_{p}}}-\eta_{j_{p}}+1)^{-1/2}\cdot\prod_{p:j_{p}\in J_{IV}}\tau_{j_{p}}^{-1/2}\cdot\prod_{p:j_{p}\in J_{I}\cup J_{III}\cup J_{VI}}\tau_{j_{p}}^{-3/2}\\
\times&N^{-\frac{1}{3}\delta_{1}}N^{-\frac{1}{3}|J_{V}|}N^{-\frac{1}{3}|J_{\varnothing,A}|}N^{\frac{1}{3}(|\mathcal{R}|-\delta_{2}+|\mathcal{L}|-\delta_{3})}\\
\times&\frac{M^{\delta-|J_{A}|+|J_{\varnothing,A}|-|J_{B}|-|J_{C}|-|U|-|J_{IV}|-|J_{VI}|}}{(k_{1}-l-1)!\cdots(k_{m}-l-1)!(\delta-\sum_{p=1}^{m}k_{p}-2|U|-2l)!}\frac{(\delta+6l-1)!}{(\delta-|U|+4l-1)!}\\
\times&|U|^{\delta_{2}}\log(2|U|)^{2|U|}C^{u_{2}}\left(\frac{u_{2}}{e}\right)^{u_{2}/2}(u_{2})^{\frac{1}{4}}(|U|)^{-u_{1}}(3l)^{u_{1}}.\end{split}

(100)

It remains to upper bound Eqn. (
100
), and sum over
k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}
,
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
.
By definition,

|U|=6l\cdot u_{1},\quad u_{2}\leq\delta-2|U|\leq u_{2}+3l.

(101)

Then up to a constant independent of the characters, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(|U|)!}\frac{(\delta+6l-1)!}{(\delta-|U|+4l-1)!}\leq\frac{(\delta+4l-1)!}{|U|!(\delta-|U|+4l-1)!}(\delta+6l)^{2l}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle 2^{\delta}\delta^{2l}\lesssim 2^{u_{2}+2|U|}(u_{1}+u_{2})^{2l}\lesssim 2^{12l(u_{1}+u_{2})}(u_{1}+u_{2})^{2l}.

And for fixed
\delta
,
|U|
summing over
k_{1},\ldots,k_{p}
,

\sum_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{p}}\frac{1}{(k_{1}-l-1)!\cdots(k_{m}-l-1)!(\delta-\sum_{p=1}^{m}k_{p}-2|U|-2l)!}\lesssim\frac{(m+1)^{u_{2}}}{(u_{2}-(m+2)l-m)!}.

So, up to a constant independent of the characters, the sum of
|w(E,\vec{q})|
with fixed characters,
\delta
and
|U|
is upper bounded by

\begin{split}&\prod_{j_{p}\in J_{A}:\eta_{j_{p}}\neq\varnothing}(C_{b_{j_{p}}}-Y_{j_{p}}+1)^{-1/2}\cdot\prod_{p:j_{p}\in J_{IV}}\tau_{j_{p}}^{-1/2}\prod_{p:j_{p}\in J_{I}\cup J_{III}\cup J_{VI}}\tau_{j_{p}}^{-3/2}\\
&N^{-\delta}N^{|U|}\cdot N^{-\frac{1}{3}\delta_{1}}N^{-\frac{1}{3}|J_{V}|}N^{-\frac{1}{3}|J_{\varnothing,A}|}\cdot N^{\frac{2}{3}\left(\delta-|J_{A}|+|J_{\varnothing,A}|-|J_{B}|-|J_{C}|-|U|-|J_{IV}|-|J_{VI}|\right)}N^{\frac{1}{3}(|\mathcal{R}|-\delta_{2}+|\mathcal{L}|-\delta_{3})}\\
\times&\log(12l\cdot u_{1})^{12l\cdot u_{1}}(6l\cdot u_{1})^{\delta_{2}}C^{u_{2}}2^{12l\cdot u_{2}}\frac{(m+1)^{u_{2}}}{(u_{2}-(m+2)l-m)!}u_{1}^{l}(u_{2})^{u_{2}/2}(u_{2})^{\frac{1}{4}}u_{1}^{-u_{1}}(3l)^{u_{1}}(u_{1}+u_{2})^{2l}.\end{split}

(102)

By Eqn. (
92
) and
\delta=|\mathcal{R}|+|\mathcal{L}|+|\mathcal{F}|
,
|\mathcal{F}|=|U|+f_{J}
,
|J|=|J_{A}|+|J_{B}|+|J_{C}|=|J_{I}|+|J_{III}|+|J_{IV}|+|J_{V}|+|J_{VI}|
,
|J_{III}|=|J_{B}|
,

\displaystyle N^{-\delta}N^{|U|}N^{-\frac{1}{3}\delta_{1}}N^{-\frac{1}{3}|J_{V}|}N^{-\frac{1}{3}|J_{\varnothing,A}|}\cdot N^{\frac{2}{3}\left(\delta-|J_{A}|+|J_{\varnothing,A}|-|J_{B}|-|J_{C}|-|U|-|J_{IV}|-|J_{VI}|\right)}N^{\frac{1}{3}(|\mathcal{R}|-\delta_{2}+|\mathcal{L}|-\delta_{3})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N^{-m}N^{\frac{1}{3}|J_{\varnothing,A}|}N^{-\frac{2}{3}|J_{IV}|-\frac{2}{3}|J_{VI}|}N^{-\frac{1}{3}|J_{V}|-\frac{1}{3}|J_{VI.3}|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N^{-m}N^{-\frac{1}{3}(|J_{A}|-|J_{\varnothing,A}|)}N^{-\frac{1}{3}|J_{IV}|}N^{\frac{1}{3}|J_{I}|-\frac{1}{3}|J_{VI}|-\frac{1}{3}|J_{VI.3}|-\frac{1}{3}|J_{C}|}.

Moreover,

\sum_{u_{1}}\sum_{u_{2}}\log(12l\cdot u_{1})^{12l\cdot u_{1}}(6l\cdot u_{1})^{\delta_{2}}C^{u_{2}}2^{12l\cdot u_{2}}\frac{(m+1)^{u_{2}}}{(u_{2}-(m+2)l-m)!}u_{1}^{l}(u_{2})^{u_{2}/2}(u_{2})^{\frac{1}{4}}u_{1}^{-u_{1}}(3l)^{u_{1}}(u_{1}+u_{2})^{2l}\lesssim 1,

since the summand clearly decays exponentially as
u_{1}
or
u_{2}\rightarrow\infty
. Eqn. (
85
) then follows and the proof is finished.

∎

Remark 5.4
.

The reason why we count the jump heights of auxiliary variables backward in time is that, if we count them forward in time, then given
\vec{q}(t-1)(t\in\llbracket 0,M\rrbracket)
, the number of choices of
\vec{q}(t)
would only be bounded by
|U|E(t-1)\leq|U|\max_{t}E(t)
instead of
\max_{t}E(t)
. This is because there are at most
|U|
choices of jump variables, and each has freedom bounded by the current height of the walk.

We now combine Lemma
5.3
with the cancellation in Section
5.2
, and give the following estimate that resolves the blow-up issue of Type I local behavior.

Lemma 5.5
.

There exists a constant
C>1
that depends on
\mathbf{k}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{k}_{l}
and
\sigma
, and is uniform in
N
, such that for all
N\geq 100
,

\begin{split}\sum_{(E,\vec{q})\in\mathcal{I}}\left|\sum_{(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})\sim(E,\vec{q})}w(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})\right|\leq&C\prod_{j_{p}\in J_{A}:\eta_{j_{p}}\neq\varnothing}(C_{b_{j_{p}}}-Y_{j_{p}}+1)^{-1/2}\cdot\prod_{p:j_{p}\in J_{IV}}\tau_{j_{p}}^{-1/2}\prod_{p:j_{p}\in J_{III}\cup J_{VI}}\tau_{j_{p}}^{-3/2}\\
\times&\prod_{p:j_{p}\in J_{I}}\tau_{j_{p}}^{-1/2}\cdot N^{-m}N^{-\frac{1}{3}(|J_{A}|-|J_{\varnothing,A}|)}N^{-\frac{1}{3}|J_{IV}|}N^{-\frac{1}{3}|J_{I}|-\frac{1}{3}|J_{VI}|-\frac{1}{3}|J_{VI.3}|-\frac{1}{3}|J_{C}|}\end{split}

(103)

where the outer sum is over all configurations with given characters
I(E,\vec{q)},\ \tau_{j_{1}},\dots\tau_{j_{m}},\ \eta_{j_{p}}^{\prime}s:\ j_{p}\in J_{A}
of
(E,\vec{q})\in\mathcal{I}
.

Proof.

By the discussion in Section
5.2
, for a given
(E,\vec{q})\in\mathcal{I}
, let
|J|
and
|U|
be the number of main and auxiliary variables in
(E,\vec{q})\in\mathcal{I}
, respectively, we have

\begin{aligned} &\left|\sum_{(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})\sim(E,\vec{q})}w(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})\right|\leq\prod_{p\ j_{p}\in J_{I}}\Bigg[\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq t\leq\tau_{j_{p}}\text{ s.t}\\
E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+t)-E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+t-1)=-1\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+t)}{N}\right)\\
-&\frac{N-|U|-2|J|+1}{N}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq t<\tau_{j_{p}}\text{ s.t}\\
E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+t)-E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+t-1)=-1\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{N-E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1})}{N}+\frac{E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+t)-E(C_{a_{j_{p}-1}})}{N}\right)\Bigg]\cdot|w(E,\vec{q})|.\end{aligned}

(104)

Indeed, for configurations in the same equivalence class, on the interval
[C_{a_{j_{p}}-1},C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+\tau_{j_{p}}]
the quantity
q(t)
satisfies

0\leq q(t)\leq E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1})

when
x_{j_{p}}
is of Type I, and
q(t)\equiv E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1})
when
x_{j_{p}}
is of Type II. Also note that the number of variables
x_{j}
with nonzero degree at a certain time
t
is at most
q(t)
, which is
E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1})
in the second case above. Hence the factors

1+\frac{E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+t)}{N}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{N-E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1})}{N}+\frac{E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+t)-E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1})}{N}

appearing in Eqn. (
104
) provide upper (lower resp.) bounds for the corresponding terms
in Eqn. (
50
). Finally, for
x_{j_{p}}
of Type II, there is one more jump of
x_{j}:\ j\notin J\cup U
at
C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+\tau_{j_{p}}
, and
N-|U|-2|J|+1
gives a lower bound of the number of jump variables available here. This is how Eqn. (
104
) follows from Eqn. (
50
).

For
E(t):\ N^{-1/3}\max_{0\leq t\leq M}E(t)\in[h,h+1)
and
p:\ j_{p}\in J_{I}
, by the triangle inequality we have

\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq t\leq\tau_{j_{p}}\text{ s.t}\\
E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+t)-E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+t-1)=-1\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+t)}{N}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad-\frac{N-|U|-2|J|+1}{N}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq t<\tau_{j_{p}}\text{ s.t}\\
E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+t)-E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+t-1)=-1\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{N-E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1})}{N}+\frac{E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+t)-E(C_{a_{j_{p}-1}})}{N}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(\tau_{j_{p}}N^{-2/3}+|U|N^{-1})(h+1)\exp(Ch)

for some constant
C>0
uniform in
N
. When summing over
(E,\vec{q})\in\mathcal{I}
in the same way as in the proof of Lemma
5.3
, the (at most
|J_{I}|
copies of) extra factors
C(h+1)\exp(Ch)
and
14
14

14

So that the first term dominates the sum.

C|U|(h+1)\exp(Ch)
is absorbed in Eqn. (
99
), which gives the desired bound.
∎

Let
\mathcal{C}
denote the set of configurations
(E,\vec{q})
such that
|J_{VI}|=|J_{C}|=0
.

Corollary 5.6
.

There exists a constant
C>1
that depends on
\mathbf{k}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{k}_{l}
and
\sigma
, and is uniform in
N
, such that for all
N\geq 100
,

\begin{split}\left|\sum_{(E,\vec{q})}w(E,\vec{q})\right|\leq&C\cdot N^{-m};\\
\left|\sum_{(E,\vec{q})\notin\mathcal{C}}w(E,\vec{q})\right|\leq&C\cdot N^{-m-1/3}.\end{split}

(105)

Proof.

Note that
1\leq\tau_{j_{p}}\leq M\leq(\sum_{i=1}^{l}T_{i}+1)N^{2/3}
for
\tau_{j_{p}}\neq\varnothing
, and
1\leq C_{b_{j_{p}}}-Y_{j_{p}}+1\leq M\leq(\sum_{i=1}^{l}T_{i}+1)N^{2/3}
. Then this follows directly from Lemma
5.5
by summing over the characters.
∎

By Corollary
5.6
, in the remainder of this section, it suffices for us to consider only
(E,\vec{q})\in\mathcal{C}
.

5.4
A second type of cancellation

In this section, we introduce a second cancellation mechanism. Fix
N\geq 100
and
\mathbf{k}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{k}_{l}>0
, and consider only the configurations in the set
\mathcal{C}
, i.e.,
|J_{VI}|=|J_{C}|=0
.

For
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, recall that a configuration
(E,\vec{q})\in\mathcal{B}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})
if it is created by the action above with this set of indices
i_{j}
. We write
(E,\vec{q})\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})\subset\mathcal{B}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})
if none of its main variables is of Type III. We then say that
(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{B}(i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{l})

deforms to

(E,\vec{q})\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})
, and write
R(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})=(E,\vec{q})
, if
(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})
has at least one main variable of Type III, and one can obtain
\vec{q}
and
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})
from
\vec{q}^{\prime}
and
(i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{l})
by the following algorithm:

•

Let
i^{\prime}
denote the element in
J^{\prime}_{III}
, the set of Type III main variables of
(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})
, that has the largest
a_{i^{\prime}}
(defined in Eqn. (
78
)). Let
j
be the index in
J_{B}
where
q_{j}(C_{a_{i^{\prime}}-1}+\tau_{i^{\prime}}-)>0
and
q_{j}(t)=0
for
t\geq C_{a_{i^{\prime}}-1}+\tau_{i^{\prime}}
, namely, the last jump of
x_{j}
occurs at time
C_{a_{i^{\prime}}-1}+\tau_{i^{\prime}}
. Then remove the last jump of
x_{j}
at
C_{a_{i^{\prime}}-1}+\tau_{i^{\prime}}
, replace all the main variables
x_{i^{\prime}_{k}}\ (1\leq k\leq l)
by
x_{j}
for all
i^{\prime}_{k}=i^{\prime}
, and replace all the jumps of
x_{i^{\prime}}
by jumps of
x_{j}
.

•

Repeat the above process until
|J^{\prime}_{III}|=0
.

Example 5.7
.

Take
i_{1}=i_{2}=i_{4}=1
,
i_{3}=2
. Then
R(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})=(E,\vec{q})\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(1,1,2,1)
for those
(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{B}(1,i_{2}^{\prime},2,i_{4}^{\prime})
where
i_{2}^{\prime}\neq i_{4}^{\prime}
are elements of
\llbracket 3,N\rrbracket
,
x_{i_{2}^{\prime}}
has its last jump at
C_{3}+\tau_{i_{4}^{\prime}}
, and
x_{1}
has its last jump at
C_{1}+\tau_{i_{2}^{\prime}}
. Figure
4
gives an example of the corresponding configurations.

An example of the deformation algorithm given in Section
5.4
. In each configuration black is the walk, blue is the sum of the blocks, red is the block for
x_{1}
, and green is the block for
x_{i_{2}^{\prime}}
. The labels above each interval denote the index of the corresponding main variable.

We start with a configuration in
\mathcal{B}(1,i_{2}^{\prime},2,i_{4}^{\prime})
where
i_{2}^{\prime}\neq i_{4}^{\prime}

\llbracket 3,N\rrbracket
. Note that
x_{i_{2}^{\prime}}
is of Type III with
x_{1}
the corresponding variable of Type B, and
x_{i_{4}^{\prime}}
is of Type III with
x_{i_{2}^{\prime}}
the corresponding variable of Type B. To deform the configuration, we start with the rightmost Type III variable and delete the jump of
x_{i_{2}^{\prime}}
between
C_{3}
and
C_{4}
, then change the main variable for this interval to
x_{i_{2}^{\prime}}
.
Middle:
After the first deformation,
x_{i_{2}^{\prime}}
is still of Type III with
x_{1}
the corresponding variable of Type B. We deform the configuration by deleting the jump of
x_{1}
between
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
and changing all instances of the variable
x_{i_{2}^{\prime}}
to
x_{1}
. This changes the main variable in the intervals from
C_{1}
to
C_{2}
and
C_{3}
to
C_{4}
from
x_{i_{2}^{\prime}}
to
x_{1}
, as well as changing the block of
x_{i_{2}^{\prime}}
to a block of
x_{1}
.
Bottom:
The end result is a configuration in
\mathcal{B}(1,1,2,1)
without any Type III variables.

We group
(E,\vec{q})
with all
(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})
such that
(E,\vec{q})=R(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})
. Then, similarly to Section
5.2
, the weights of configurations in the same group have opposite signs and cancel out. More precisely, we have the following estimate.

Lemma 5.8
.

Fix a tuple
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})
that is not composed of mutually distinct indices, and define the set
J
from
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})
as before. Then there exists a constant
C>1
that depends on
\mathbf{k}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{k}_{l}
and
\sigma
, and is uniform in
N
, such that for all
N\geq 100
,

\sum_{(E,\vec{q})\in\mathcal{\tilde{B}}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})}\left|w(E,\vec{q})+\sum_{(E,\vec{q}^{\prime}):\ R(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})=(E,\vec{q})}w(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})\right|\leq C\cdot N^{-|J|}N^{-1/12}.

(106)

Proof.

From the definition, one can see that
(E,\vec{q})=R(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})
for some
(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{B}(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{l}^{\prime})
with mutually distinct
i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i_{l}^{\prime}
, unless
15
15

15

Here, the exponent
1/4
can be taken as any real number in
(0,\frac{1}{3})
.

•

i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}
are mutually distinct.

•

There exists some index
i\notin\{a_{j}:j\in J\}
, such that
E(C_{i-1}+t)\geq E(C_{i-1})
for all
0\leq t\leq N^{1/4}
, or

•

there is a jump at
C_{i-1}+t
for some
1\leq t\leq N^{1/4}
.

We briefly explain how the second and third situations reduce the weight contribution of the configurations, recalling the argument in the proof of Lemma
5.3
. Again, viewing
E(t)
as a conditional random walk bridge, there exists a constant
C>1
that depends on
\mathbf{k}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{k}_{l}
and
\sigma
and is uniform in
N
, such that the second case above occurs with probability
<CN^{-1/8}
. This gives an additional factor
N^{-1/8}
. If the third case occurs, then there are
N^{1/4}
choices of the jump time (and at most
|J|+|U|
choices of the jump variable). Fixing the jump time, if the jump is a first or free jump, then we update the bound in Eqn. (
87
) by decreasing one of the elements
1,\ldots,1,k_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,k_{m}^{\prime}
and
\delta-\sum_{p=1}^{m}k^{\prime}_{p}-2|U|-|J_{IV}|-|J_{m,VI}|-|J_{a,VI}|-|J_{m,A}|-|J_{a,A}|-|J_{B}|-|J_{C}|
by 1. This gives an additional factor
M^{-1}\lesssim N^{-2/3}
. If the jump is a last jump, then its height is fixed by the walk
E(t)
at the given jump time, and we decrease one of the
u^{\prime}(\tilde{k})
in Eqn. (
93
) by 1. This gives an additional factor
N^{-1/3}
. Together with the factor
N^{1/4}
, the total weight of the configurations with the third situation above is at most
O(N^{-1/12})
.

It then remains to consider the set of
(E,\vec{q})
which have a collection of preimages
(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})
with mutually distinct
i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{l}^{\prime}
, such that, for
1\leq j\leq l
,
i_{j}^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\setminus J
if
i_{j}\notin\{a_{j}:\ j\in J\}
. For this
(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})
, let
J^{\prime}_{III}
denote the set of its type III main variables. Then each element
(E,\vec{q}^{\prime\prime})
in
R^{-1}(E,\vec{q})
can be deformed from
(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})
by

•

Take a subset
J^{\prime\prime}_{III}
of
J^{\prime}_{III}
.

•

Let
i
denote the element in
J^{\prime\prime}_{III}
that has the largest
a_{i}
. Let
j
be the index in
J_{B}
where
q_{j}(C_{a_{i}-1}+\tau_{i}-)>0
and
q_{j}(t)=0
for
t\geq C_{a_{i}-1}+\tau_{i}
, that is, the last jump of
x_{j}
occurs at time
C_{a_{i}-1}+\tau_{i}
. Then let
i_{k}\ (1\leq k\leq l)
be
j
for all
i_{k}=i
, and let
q_{i}\equiv 0
, that is, remove the only jump of
x_{j}
at
C_{a_{i}-1}+\tau_{i}
.

•

Repeat the above process until
|J^{\prime\prime}_{III}|=0
.

Moreover, it is clear that
(E,\vec{q}^{\prime\prime})
is indexed by
J_{III}^{\prime\prime}
. Similarly to Eqn. (
104
), for
E(t)
such that
\ N^{-1/3}\max_{0\leq t\leq M}E(t)\in[h,h+1)
, we have

\begin{aligned} &\left|w(E,\vec{q})+\sum_{(E,\vec{q}^{\prime\prime}):\ R(E,\vec{q}^{\prime\prime})=(E,\vec{q})}w(E,\vec{q}^{\prime\prime})\right|\\
\leq&\prod_{p:\ j^{\prime}_{p}\in J^{\prime}_{III}}\Bigg[\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq t\leq\tau_{j^{\prime}_{p}}\text{ s.t}\\
E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+t)-E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+t-1)=-1\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{E(C_{a_{j^{\prime}_{p}}-1}+t)}{N}\right)\\
-&\frac{N-|U|-2|J|+1}{N}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq t<\tau_{j^{\prime}_{p}}\text{ s.t}\\
E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+t)-E(C_{a_{j_{p}}-1}+t-1)=-1\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{N-E(C_{a_{j^{\prime}_{p}}-1})}{N}+\frac{E(C_{a_{j^{\prime}_{p}}-1}+t)-E(C_{a_{j^{\prime}_{p}}-1})}{N}\right)\Bigg]\cdot|w(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})|\\
\leq&\sum_{(E,\vec{q}^{\prime}):\ i_{1}^{\prime}\neq\ldots\neq i_{l}^{\prime}}\prod_{p:\ j^{\prime}_{p}\in J^{\prime}_{III}}\left(C(\tau_{j^{\prime}_{p}}N^{-2/3}+|U|N^{-1})(h+1)\exp(Ch)\right)\cdot|w(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})|\\
\leq&N^{|J_{III}^{\prime}|}\prod_{p:\ j^{\prime}_{p}\in J^{\prime}_{III}}\left(C(\tau_{j^{\prime}_{p}}N^{-2/3}+|U|N^{-1})(h+1)\exp(Ch)\right)\cdot|w(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})|,\end{aligned}

(107)

where in the last expression we pick an arbitrary
(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})\in R^{-1}(E,\vec{q})
with mutually distinct
i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{l}^{\prime}
with
|J|
main variables and
|U|
auxiliary variables.
Summing over
(E,\vec{q})\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})
as in the Lemma
5.5
and Corollary
5.6
, we get an upper bound
16
16

16

Note that
l-|J|=|J_{III}^{\prime}|
.

C\cdot N^{-|J|}N^{-\frac{1}{3}(l-|J|)}\leq C\cdot N^{-|J|}N^{-1/12},

where the last inequality holds if
i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}
are not mutually distinct.
∎

Corollary 5.9
.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mu_{+}(N)^{M_{1}+\ldots+M_{l}}}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{l}\left[\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{1}}{N}\right)^{M_{j}}+\ldots+\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{N}}{N}\right)^{M_{j}}\right]G_{N}(\vec{x};\beta)\Bigg|_{\vec{x}=0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})\\
\text{mutually\ distinct}\end{subarray}}\sum_{(E,\vec{q})\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})\cap\mathcal{C}}w(E,\vec{q})+o(1)=N(N-1)\cdots(N-l+1)\sum_{(E,\vec{q})\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(1,\ldots,l)\cap\mathcal{C}}w(E,\vec{q})+o(1),

where the
o(1)
term vanishes as
N\to\infty
.

Proof.

The left-hand side of the equality above is equal to

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})\\
\text{mutually\ distinct}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\sum_{(E,\vec{q})\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})\cap\mathcal{C}}w(E,\vec{q})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})\\
\text{ not\ mutually\ distinct}\end{subarray}}\sum_{(E,\vec{q})\in\mathcal{B}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})}\left(w(E,\vec{q})+\sum_{(E,\vec{q}^{\prime}):\ R(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})=(E,\vec{q})}w(E,\vec{q}^{\prime})\right).

Summing over the
O(N^{|J|})
choices of
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})
, the second line is of order
o(1)
by Lemma
5.8
. This verifies the first equality. The second equality follows directly from the symmetry of the Dunkl operators and Bessel generating function.
∎

The main contribution in Eqn. (
41
) is therefore given by configurations with mutually distinct
i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}
and without main variables of Types III, VI, B, or C.

5.5
Convergence of the functionals

In this section, we prove that the Dunkl action Eqn. (
41
) converges to the mixed moment of the Laplace transform of the
\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
process.

By Corollary
5.9
, it suffices to consider configurations
(E,\vec{q})
with the following properties.

•

We take
J=\{1,2,\ldots,l\}
,
a_{i}=b_{i}=i
for

.
U=\{l+1,\ldots,l+u\}
for some

. In other words, we take the auxiliary variables to be

for notational simplicity.

•

Let
\delta_{i,p}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
denote the number of jumps of
x_{i}
in
\llbracket C_{p-1}+1,C_{p}\rrbracket
. Then
\delta_{i,p}=0
for

. Let

be the tuple that records all the
\delta_{i,p}

(E,\vec{q})
via
u
).

Take
\epsilon>0
. Let
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\epsilon}(1,2,\ldots,l)
be the subset of
\mathcal{B}(1,2,\ldots,l)
such that for each
p=2,\dots,l
, we have
\tau_{p}>\epsilon(C_{p}-C_{p-1})
or
\tau_{p}=\varnothing
, and
\tilde{\tau}_{p}>\epsilon(C_{p}-C_{p-1})
or
\tilde{\tau}_{p}=\varnothing
.

Lemma 5.10
.

There exists a constant
C>0
uniform in
N
, such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mu_{+}(N)^{M_{1}+\ldots+M_{l}}}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{l}\left[\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{1}}{N}\right)^{M_{j}}+\ldots+\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{N}}{N}\right)^{M_{j}}\right]G_{N}(\vec{x};\beta)\Bigg|_{\vec{x}=0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N(N-1)\cdots(N-l+1)\sum_{(E,\vec{q})\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\epsilon}(1,\ldots,l)\cap\mathcal{C}}w(E,\vec{q})+C\sqrt{\epsilon}+o(1).

Proof.

By Corollary
5.9
, it remains to check that

N(N-1)\cdots(N-l+1)\left|\sum_{(E,\vec{q})\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(1,\ldots,l)\setminus\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\epsilon}(1,2,\ldots,l)}w(E,\vec{q})\right|\leq C\sqrt{\epsilon}.

This follows from Lemma
5.5
and a similar summation over the characters as in the proof of Corollary
5.6
.
∎

In the remainder of this section, we will classify the elements in
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\epsilon}(1,2,\ldots,l)\cap\mathcal{C}
by their data
\vec{\delta}
.
Given a fixed
\vec{\delta}
, in order to characterize the possible Type I local behavior of

, we introduce the

. These are a collection of
\nu_{p}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\cup\varnothing,\ 2\leq p\leq l
, such that
17
17

17

One can also define
\nu_{1}
, which by definition is always
\varnothing
.

•

\nu_{p}=\varnothing
if
\sum_{k=1}^{p-1}\delta_{p,k}=0
,

•

if
\sum_{k=1}^{p-1}\delta_{p,k}>0
,

if and only if
\tau_{p}\neq\varnothing
, and
\tilde{\tau}_{p}>\tau_{p}
or

. In this case
\nu_{p}=\tau_{p}
.

Given
\vec{\delta}
,

(s_{0}<\ldots<s_{n})=(\Delta-1)\cup\Delta\cup\{C_{p}:\ 0\leq p\leq l\}\cup\{C_{p-1}+\nu_{p}-1:\ \nu_{p}\neq\varnothing,\ 2\leq p\leq l\}\cup\{C_{p-1}+\nu_{p}:\ \nu_{p}\neq\varnothing,\ 2\leq p\leq l\}.

Suppose

is also given, let

at the points

.

•

.

•

.

•

(108)

•

Therefore, by Eqn. (

\begin{split}N(N-1)&\cdots(N-l+1)\sum_{(E,\vec{q})\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\epsilon}(1,\ldots,l)\cap\mathcal{C}}w(E,\vec{q})=N(N-1)\cdots(N-l+1)\sum_{\vec{\delta}}\sum_{\vec{\nu}}\sum_{\vec{q}}\sum_{\vec{H}}w(E,\vec{q})\\

Next, we recall the moment expression of the Laplace transform of
\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
from
[
GXZ
, Section 2]
. The purpose of this is to identify the limit in Theorem
5.11
with the object introduced in the concurrent work, while the convergence itself will follow from the arguments presented in this text. The interested reader can find a detailed exposition there. Below we outline the necessary notation and formulas, with some small adaptations to fit our needs.

To begin we define the continuous analogues of the objects we introduced previously. Let
\mathbf{k}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{k}_{l}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,
\mathbf{Q}_{p}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\mathbf{k}_{j}
for
p=1,2,\ldots,l
. Let
\vec{\mathbf{p}}=\{\mathbf{p}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{l+u}
be the
block process
where each
\mathbf{p}_{j}:[0,\mathbf{Q}_{l}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,
\mathbf{p}_{j}(t)\equiv 0
on
(\mathbf{Q}_{j-1},\mathbf{Q}_{l}]
for
1\leq j\leq l
. Let
\mathbf{p}^{0}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{l+u}\mathbf{p}_{j}(t)
. Order
\mathbf{p}_{j}:\ l+1\leq j\leq l+u
so that
\min\{t:\mathbf{p}_{l+1}(t)>0\}<\cdots<\min\{t:\mathbf{p}_{l+u}(t)>0\}
. Let
\vec{\bm{\upsilon}}=(\bm{\upsilon}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\upsilon}_{l})\in(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\cup\{\emptyset\})^{l}
be the
virtual block process
. Let
\mathbf{\Delta}
be the set of discontinuity points of
\vec{\mathbf{p}}
, which locate in
\bigcup_{p=2}^{l}(\mathbf{Q_{p-1}},\mathbf{Q}_{p})
,
\bm{\delta}
be the size of
\mathbf{\Delta}
,
\vec{\bm{\delta}}=(\bm{\delta}_{j,p}:\ 1\leq j\leq l+u,1\leq p\leq l)
be the tuple, where
\bm{\delta}_{j,p}
gives the number of discontinuity points of
\mathbf{p}_{j}
in
(\mathbf{Q}_{p-1},\mathbf{Q}_{p})
, and
\mathbf{H}:\mathbf{\Delta}\cup\{\mathbf{Q}_{p}:0\leq p\leq l\}\cup\{\mathbf{Q}_{p-1}+\bm{\upsilon}_{p}:2\leq p\leq l,\bm{\upsilon}_{p}\neq\varnothing,\ 1\leq p\leq l\}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be a collection of continuous heights. We do not list the detailed definitions of
\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\bm{\upsilon}}
and
\mathbf{H}
, but simply note that all the conditions are parallel to those of our discrete objects
\vec{q}
,
\vec{\delta}
and
\vec{H}
. Given the data
\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\bm{\upsilon}}
, let
\mathcal{H}[\vec{\mathbf{k}}]
be the space of the tuple (
\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\bm{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})
, and

be the Lebesgue measure on
\mathcal{H}[\vec{\mathbf{k}}]
19
19

19

For clarity, here we use a different notation that we separate the discrete sums over
\vec{\bm{\delta}}
and the events
\{\nu_{p}=\varnothing\}
with the continuous integral, while in
[
GXZ
]
,
d(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\bm{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})
denotes the combination of both parts.
. Then by
[
GXZ
