6
Proof of the main theorem

In this section, we finish the proof of Theorem
1.2
, based on Theorem
5.11
. We follow a three-step strategy: in Section
6.1
, we identify the limiting Laplace transform expression of the values
\lambda_{i}^{\prime}(N)
along a subsequence; in Section
6.2
, we identify the Laplace transform expression with that of
\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
; then in Section
6.3
, we prove the uniqueness of the underlying point process.

6.1
Limit Laplace transform

The main result in this section is the following lemma.

Lemma 6.1
.

There exists an increasing sequence of positive integers
N_{1}<N_{2}<\cdots
, such that as
k\to\infty
,
\{\lambda_{i}^{\prime}(N_{k})/\mu_{+}(N_{k})\}_{i=1}^{N_{k}}
converges weakly to a point process
\eta_{1}\geq\eta_{2}\geq\cdots
in
\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
, and for any
\mathbf{k}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{k}_{l}>0
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{l}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\exp\left(\frac{\mathbf{k}_{j}\eta_{i}}{\mu_{+}}\right)\right)\right]=\mathbf{L}_{\beta}(\left(2P_{-1}\sigma\right)^{-2/3}\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\bm{\tau}}=0).

(117)

As preparation for the proof of Lemma
6.1
, we present a deterministic statement. Consider the space
\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
, whose topology is generated by open intervals
(a,b)
and
[-\infty,a)
for all
a<b\in\mathbb{R}
.

For any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
\alpha>0
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote

M_{+}[x,\alpha,N]=\frac{1}{2}(1+N^{-2/3}x)^{2\lfloor\alpha N^{2/3}/2\rfloor},\quad M_{-}[x,\alpha,N]=\frac{1}{2}(1+N^{-2/3}x)^{2\lfloor\alpha N^{2/3}/2\rfloor+1},

and
M[x,\alpha,N]=M_{+}[x,\alpha,N]+M_{-}[x,\alpha,N]
.

Proposition 6.2
.

For integers
1\leq i\leq N
, let
x^{N}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
satisfy
x^{N}_{i}\geq x^{N}_{i+1}
for each
1\leq i\leq N-1
.
Suppose there exists an increasing sequence of positive integers
N_{1}<N_{2}<\cdots
, such that for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
or
\alpha^{-1}\in\mathbb{N}
, as
k\to\infty
, the sum
\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M[x_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}]
converges to a limit in
\mathbb{R}
, and
\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M_{+}[x_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}]
is bounded by a constant independent of
k
(but might depend on
\alpha
).
Then for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, as
k\rightarrow\infty
,
x_{i}^{N_{k}}
converges in
\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
to some limit
x_{i}
, and
for any
\alpha>0
,

\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M[x_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}]\to\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\exp(\alpha x_{i}).

Proposition
6.2
can be viewed as a special case of
[
GXZ
, Proposition 5.5]
, and for completeness we present a proof in Appendix B.

Proof of Lemma
6.1
.
As a special case of Theorem
5.11
, for any
\mathbf{k}>0
, we have the convergence of

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}M[\lambda_{i}^{\prime}(N)/\mu_{+}(N),\mathbf{k},N]\right)^{2}\right]\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}M_{+}[\lambda_{i}^{\prime}(N)/\mu_{+}(N),\mathbf{k},N]\right)^{2}\right],

which implies the tightness of

\sum_{i=1}^{N}M[\lambda_{i}^{\prime}(N)/\mu_{+}(N),\mathbf{k},N]\quad\text{and}\sum_{i=1}^{N}M_{+}[\lambda_{i}^{\prime}(N)/\mu_{+}(N),\mathbf{k},N].

Therefore, there exists a subsequence
\{N_{k}\}_{k=1,2,\ldots}
such that for all
\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{k}^{-1}\in\mathbb{N}
, both

\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M[\lambda_{i}^{\prime}(N_{k})/\mu_{+}(N_{k}),\mathbf{k},N]\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M_{+}[\lambda_{i}^{\prime}(N_{k})/\mu_{+}(N_{k}),\mathbf{k},N]

converge weakly as
k\to\infty
.

By the Skorokhod representation theorem, there exists a probability space on which the convergence holds almost surely, so

\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M[\lambda_{i}^{\prime}(N_{k})/\mu_{+}(N_{k}),\mathbf{k},N]\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M_{+}[\lambda_{i}^{\prime}(N_{k})/\mu_{+}(N_{k}),\mathbf{k},N]

satisfy the conditions in Proposition
6.2
. Then for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\lambda_{i}^{\prime}(N_{k})/\mu_{+}(N_{k})
converges to a limit
\eta_{i}/\mu_{+}
as
\mu_{+}(N_{k})\rightarrow\mu_{+}
, and for any
\mathbf{k}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{k}_{l}>0
, as
k\to\infty

\prod_{j=1}^{l}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M[\lambda_{i}^{\prime}(N_{k})/\mu_{+}(N_{k}),\mathbf{k}_{j},N]\right)\rightarrow\prod_{j=1}^{l}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\exp\left(\frac{\mathbf{k}_{j}\eta_{i}}{\mu_{+}}\right)\right)\quad\text{almost surely}.

Again by Theorem
5.11
, the left-hand side above is uniformly integrable, so the expectation of the right-hand side is equal to the limit expectation of the left-hand side, which is
\mathbf{L}_{\beta}(\left(2P_{-1}\sigma\right)^{-2/3}\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\bm{\tau}}=0)
by Theorem
5.11
.∎

6.2
Matching with Airy(
\beta
)

Theorem 6.3
.

\mathbf{L}_{\beta}(\left(2P_{-1}\sigma\right)^{-2/3}\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\bm{\tau}}=0)
is equal to the mixed moment of Laplace transform of
\tilde{C}\cdot\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
, where
\tilde{C}
is defined in Proposition
1.3
.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.1
,
\mathbf{L}_{\beta}(\left(2P_{-1}\sigma\right)^{-2/3}\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\bm{\tau}}=0)
is the Laplace transform expression of the limit point process
\{\eta_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
in
\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
. Since this limiting expression is universal up to a constant, and our ensembles include a single copy of L
\beta
E as a special case, by the classical result
[
RRV
, Theorem 1.4]
,
\mathbf{L}_{\beta}(\left(2P_{-1}\sigma\right)^{-2/3}\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\bm{\tau}}=0)
is the Laplace transform expression of the
\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
process, rescaled by some constant.

It remains to identify this constant with
\tilde{C}
.
For a single
N\times N

L\beta E
,
\kappa_{l}=1
for all
l=1,2,\ldots
. So by Eqn. (
15
) and (
54
) we have
\tilde{C}=2^{4/3}
,
\mu_{+}=4
,
\sigma=\sqrt{2}
,
P_{-1}=\frac{1}{2}
.
Therefore, the result holds if and only if, by comparison with this special case, the Laplace transform expression is unchanged after replacing
\mathbf{k}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{k}_{l}
by
\frac{\mu_{+}}{4\tilde{C}}\mathbf{k}_{1}
,…,
\frac{\mu_{+}}{4\tilde{C}}\mathbf{k}_{l}
for a general ensemble.
Again by Eqn. (
15
) and (
54
) we have

(2P_{-1}\sigma)^{2/3}\left(\frac{\mu_{+}}{4\tilde{C}}\right)=(2\times\frac{1}{2}\times\sqrt{2})^{2/3}\times 2^{-4/3},

so this is indeed the case.

∎

6.3
Tightness and uniqueness of the limiting point process

Denote the random point configuration of
\{\frac{1}{\tilde{C}}\lambda_{i}^{\prime}(N)\}_{i=1}^{N}
by
\mathcal{X}_{N}
, a sequence of point processes on
\mathbb{R}
. By Lemma
6.1
and Theorem
6.3
, the Laplace transform of
\mathcal{X}_{N}
converges in moments to the Laplace transform of
\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
.

We claim that
\{\mathcal{X}_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 100}}
is tight in the
W^{\#}
topology of boundedly finite Borel measures on
\mathbb{R}
, see
[
DVe1
, A2.6]
for
the definition. By
[
DVe2
, Proposition 11.1.VI]
, this is equivalent to checking that for any compact interval
A\subset\mathbb{R}
, the random variables

N_{A}:=\text{number\ of\ particles\ of\ }\mathcal{X}_{N}\ \text{in\ A}

(we omit the dependence on
N
in the notation for simplicity) are uniformly tight in
N
. It suffices to check
A=[t,\infty)
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
since such sets contain all compact intervals. We have

\frac{N_{A}}{2}\exp\left(\frac{Tt}{\mu_{+}}\right)\leq\sum_{i}\left(1+\frac{\lambda^{\prime}_{i}(N)}{\mu_{+}(N)N^{\frac{2}{3}}}\right)^{\lfloor TN^{\frac{2}{3}}\rfloor}

when
N
is large. The expectation of the right-hand side converges as
N\rightarrow\infty
by Theorem
5.11
. Hence the uniform tightness follows.

It remains to check that the moments of the Laplace transform of
\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
uniquely determine a point process on
\mathbb{R}
. If this holds, then
by the tightness of
\{\mathcal{X}_{N}\}
, every subsequence of
\{\mathcal{X}_{N}\}
has a further subsequence that converges to
\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
, and so does the whole sequence.

By definition of correlation measures
\rho_{l}
of point processes (see
[
DVe1
]
,
[
DVe2
]
), for a point process
\mathcal{X}
in
\mathbb{R}
with particles
\Lambda_{1}\geq\Lambda_{2}\geq\ldots

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{l}\left(\sum_{i}\exp\left(\mathbf{k}_{j}\Lambda_{i}\right)\right)\right]=\int\cdots\int\rho_{l}(dx_{1},\ldots,dx_{l})e^{\mathbf{k}_{1}x_{1}+\ldots+\mathbf{k}_{l}x_{l}}+\text{lower\ order\ terms},

(118)

where the lower order terms give a linear combination of Laplace transforms of
\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{l-1}
of similar form.

Lemma 6.4
.

All
\rho_{l}
are uniquely determined by the Laplace transform above.

Proof.

By iterating from
l=1
in Eqn. (
118
), it remains to show that for any
l=1,2,\ldots
,
\mathbf{k}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{k}_{l}>0
, the Laplace transform

\int\cdots\int\rho_{l}(dx_{1},\ldots,dx_{l})e^{\mathbf{k}_{1}x_{1}+\ldots+\mathbf{k}_{l}x_{l}}

(119)

uniquely determines
\rho_{l}
. It is clear that Eqn. (
119
) is finite for all
(\mathbf{k}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{k}_{l})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{l}
, so fixing some
(S_{1},\ldots,S_{l})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{l}
, we have
\rho_{l}(dx_{1},\ldots,dx_{l})e^{S_{1}x_{1}+\ldots+S_{l}x_{l}}
is in
L^{1}
, i.e., a finite measure, and its Fourier transform is fixed by Eqn. (
119
) under analytic continuation. Then
\rho_{l}(dx_{1},\ldots,dx_{l})e^{S_{1}x_{1}+\ldots+S_{l}x_{l}}
and hence
\rho_{l}(dx_{1},\ldots,dx_{l})
is also fixed.
∎

Recall that by definition, the distribution of
\mathcal{X}
is determined by finite-dimensional distributions of
N_{A_{1}},\ldots,N_{A_{k}}
, for any compact sets
A_{1},\ldots,A_{k}
, and

\mathbb{E}\left[N_{A}(N_{A}-1)\cdots(N_{A}-k+1)\right]=\int_{A}\cdots\int_{A}\rho_{k}(dx_{1},\ldots,dx_{k}).

(120)

So the (joint) moments of the observables
N_{A}
are fixed by correlation measures. It remains to check that for any compact set
A
, the tail probability of
N_{A}
is exponentially decaying, so the moments of
N_{A}
determine the distribution of
N_{A}
. We check this for
A=(\lambda,\infty)
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, which will suffice.

Since
\mathcal{X}=\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
, we use the following result from
[
RRV
, Theorem 1.2]
: for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbb{P}[N_{(\lambda,+\infty)}\geq k]=\mathbb{P}[\eta_{k}>\lambda]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}\kappa(\lambda,dx_{1})\kappa(x_{1},dx_{2})\cdots\kappa(x_{k-1},dx_{k}),

(121)

where
\kappa(x,\cdot)
is the distribution of the first passage time to
-\infty
of diffusion
p(t)
(
B(t)
is a standard Brownian motion)

dp(t)=\left(t-p(t)^{2}\right)dt+\frac{2}{\sqrt{\beta}}dB(t),

(122)

that starts from
+\infty
at time
x
.

Then Eqn. (
121
) can be rewritten as

\mathbb{P}[N_{(\lambda,+\infty)}\geq k]=\mathbb{P}[p(t)\ \text{reaches}\ -\infty\ \text{for\ at\ least}\ k\ \text{times\ when\ starting\ from\ time}\ \lambda].

We collect the facts about
\kappa(x,\cdot)
we need in the next lemma.

Lemma 6.5
.

(1).
\kappa(x,y)=0
for
y<x
.

(2). For any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
\kappa(x,\{+\infty\})>0
.

(3).
\kappa(x,\{+\infty\})
increases with
x
.

Proof.

(1) is clear from the definition. To see (2) we set
k=1
in Eqn. (
121
) and get

\kappa(x,\{+\infty\})=1-\mathbb{P}[\eta_{1}>x]=\mathbb{P}[\eta_{1}\leq x],

(123)

which is clearly in
(0,1)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
(see
[
RRV
, Theorem 1.3]
for a tail estimate of
\eta_{1}
). (3) also follows directly from Eqn. (
123
).
∎

It is clear from the definition that each run of
p(t)
after the restart from
+\infty
is independent. Hence by the above properties of transition measure
\kappa(x,\cdot)
, we have

\mathbb{P}[p(x)\ \text{reaches}\ -\infty\ \text{for\ at\ least}\ k+1\ \text{times\ when\ starting\ from\ time}\ \lambda]\leq(1-\kappa(\lambda,\{+\infty\}))^{k+1},

which is exponentially decaying. This finishes the proof.∎

7
Further studies

7.1
Negative free cumulants

Recall that in this text we assume that for our
\beta
-additions, the free cumulants
\kappa_{1}(N),\kappa_{2}(N),\ldots
are all nonnegative, see Eqn. (
35
). This is necessary because the proofs presented from Section
4
to Section
5
use a probabilistic point of view, that is, we interpret the huge sum arising from the actions of Dunkl operators on
G(z_{1},\ldots,z_{N};\beta)
as a certain functional of a conditional random walk
E(t)
. The increments
X_{i}(N)
of
E(t)
are i.i.d. before conditioning and, as
N\rightarrow\infty
,

X_{1}(N)\implies X_{1},

whose distribution function is given in terms of the free cumulants
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\ldots
and the Voiculescu transform
V(z)
.

Recall also that this assumption includes all cases in which we add Gaussian ensembles with finitely many Laguerre ensembles, and a subset of the cases in which we have subtractions of Laguerre ensembles. We naturally conjecture that if we assume similar convergence for each summand as stated in Section
1.3
, the edge limit of the additions will still be the
\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
process under proper rescaling, even when some
\kappa_{l}
are negative.

It seems that our current proof cannot be directly applied to this case. For simplicity, let us only consider the asymptotics of

\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\mu_{+}(N)N}\right)^{M}\right],

where
M\approx\mathbf{k}N^{2/3}
. There are two main ingredients of our proof that do not work as before: the steepest descent calculation and the functional CLT in Section
4
.

Consider
\kappa_{l}(N)
and
V_{N}(z)
defined as in Eqn. (
10
), (
11
) and (
13
), and take

\alpha_{1}>0
,
\alpha_{2}<0
.
For the steepest descent of such
V_{N}(z)
, we claim without proof that there is a unique root of
V^{{}^{\prime}}_{N}(z)
in
(-\frac{1}{\alpha_{2}},0)
and another in
(0,\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}})
, which we denote by
z_{c,1}(N)
and
z_{c,2}(N)
, respectively. By choosing a contour
C_{z}^{\prime}(N)
symmetric about the real line that crosses both
z_{c,1}(N)
and
z_{c,2}(N)
and only surrounds the pole
z=0
of
V_{N}(z)
, one can check that

|m_{M}(N)|=\frac{1}{M+1}\left|\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{C_{z}^{\prime}(N)}V_{N}(z)^{M+1}dz\right|\sim\frac{1}{\sqrt{M}}|V_{N}(z_{c}(N))|^{M+1},

(124)

where
z_{c}(N)
is whichever of
z_{c,1}(N)
or
z_{c,2}(N)
that maximizes
|V_{N}(z)|
on
C_{z}^{\prime}(N)
. Without loss of generality, suppose
V_{N}(z_{c}(N))>0
. Since the edge behavior is characterized by
m_{M}(N)
for
M\sim N^{\frac{2}{3}}
, we believe that the critical point
z_{c}(N)
in the steepest descent gives the upper edge
\mu_{+}(N)=V(z_{c}(N))
as before.

Question:
Is it always the case that
|z_{c,1}(N)|<\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}}
, so that
V_{N}(z_{c,1}(N))
converges absolutely as
N\to\infty
?

Issues also arise in the use of the functional CLT. If we take the same interpretation of conditional random walk with i.i.d. steps
X_{i}(N)
as before but allow some cumulants to be negative, then
X_{i}(N)
is a formal integer-valued random variable, such that for
l\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}(X_{i}(N)=l)=\frac{\kappa_{l+1}(N)z_{c}(N)^{l}}{V_{N}(z_{c}(N))},

which is negative for some
l
. Everything can be understood in a measure-theoretic sense by replacing the probability measure with a signed measure of total mass 1. However, such formal random objects behave poorly from a probabilistic point of view, with many elementary arguments breaking down. In particular, in Section
4
,
P_{-1}=\mathbb{P}(X_{i}=-1)
gives the asymptotic proportion of down-steps, but our arguments no longer hold when
X_{i}
is not supported by a probability measure, because even the classical weak LLN no longer holds. In addition, it is not clear to us whether one can state the analog for signed measures of Theorems
4.1
and
4.2
that deal with the weak convergence of conditional random walks to conditional Brownian motions. Another obstruction is showing that the tail probability of the random walk decays exponentially, as done in Section
4.4
. We give a simple example to highlight the issue: if one instead considers the i.i.d. sum of formal steps
X_{i}
such that
\mathbb{E}[X_{i}]=0
, with distribution say

\mathbb{P}(X_{i}=1)=\frac{3}{2},\ \mathbb{P}(X_{i}=3)=-\frac{1}{2},

where
\mathbb{P}
denotes a signed measure of total mass 1, then the total variation of the tail “probability” of
X_{1}+\ldots+X_{M}
grows to infinity as
M\rightarrow\infty
. Therefore, the weak convergence of the signed measure does not imply the convergence of moments.

Question:
Can one generalize the probabilistic interpretation of Dunkl action to formal random walks governed by signed measure, or is a different point of view needed?

Question:
Suppose the probabilistic argument can be generalized. Recall also that the sum in Dunkl action does not directly involve the parameter
z_{c}(N)
(the walk starts and ends at 0, so powers of
z_{c}(N)
cancel out after conditioning). However,
z_{c}(N)
and
V_{N}(z_{c}(N))
determine the scaling factor/variance of the random walk. Is this still the case for negative cumulants? If yes, among the two candidates
z_{c,1}(N)
and
z_{c,2}(N)
, what makes the functional “choose”
z_{c}(N)
that maximizes
|V_{N}(z)|
?

7.2
Edge universality for general
\beta
-additions

Going beyond G
\beta
E and L
\beta
E, the
\beta
-additions of general ensembles have been introduced and studied in
[
GM
]
,
[
BCG
]
,
[
Xu
]
,
[
CX
]
and
[
Yao
]
. While all these works focus on the global limits of the empirical measure, it is natural as well to consider its edge limit in a proper sense. Inspired by the results in
[
Ahn
]
and
[
JP
]
, we state a conjecture that extends the Airy edge universality from
\beta=1,2
, as well as the finite additions of Gaussian and Laguerre ensembles, to a large class of general
\beta
-additions.

Let
a(N)=(a_{1}(N)\geq\ldots\geq a_{N}(N))
,
b(N)=(b_{1}(N)\geq\ldots\geq b_{N}(N))
be two sequences of random
N
-tuples in the Weyl chamber
W_{N}
, with exponentially decaying distributions
\mu_{a(N)}
and
\mu_{b(N)}
.

It is known that for general
\beta>0
, the product

G_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta,\mu_{c(N)}):=G_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta,\mu_{a(N)})G_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta,\mu_{b(N)})

defines a unique tempered distribution
\mu_{c(N)}
on
W_{N}
, see e.g.,
[
A
]
for more details. Moreover, when
\beta=1,2,4
,
\mu_{c(N)}
is a legitimate probability measure that gives the distribution of the eigenvalues of

diag(a(N))+U_{N}diag(b(N))U_{N}^{*},

where
U_{N}
is an
N\times N
Haar random orthogonal/unitary/symplectic matrix, respectively. We view
\mu_{c(N)}
as the distribution of a random
N
-tuple
c(N)
in
W_{N}
, consisting of the eigenvalues of the
\beta
-additions of two virtual Hermitian matrices
A(N)
and
B(N)
with eigenvalues
a(N)
,
b(N)
respectively.

We further assume that

•

As
N\rightarrow\infty
, the empirical measures

\mu_{A(N)}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{a_{i}(N)/N}\rightarrow\mu_{A},\quad\mu_{B(N)}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{b_{i}(N)/N}\rightarrow\mu_{B}

for some compactly supported deterministic measures
\mu_{A}
,
\mu_{B}
on
\mathbb{R}
.

•

Let
\mu_{+}(A)
,
\mu_{+}(B)
be the right endpoints of
\operatorname{supp}(\mu_{A})
,
\operatorname{supp}(\mu_{B})
, and
\mu_{+}(A),\mu_{+}(B)>0
.

Then by
[
CX
, Theorem 1.3]
, the empirical measure of
c(N)/N
converges in moments to
\mu_{A}\boxplus\mu_{B}
, where
\boxplus
is the free convolution. We then expect that the largest entries of
c(N)/N
will also converge to
\mathrm{Airy}(\beta)
under proper scaling. Since it is still open whether
c(N)
is supported by a probability measure, we state our conjecture in terms of moments using Dunkl operators.

Conjecture.
Under certain mild conditions on
\mu_{a(N)},\ \mu_{b(N)},\ \mu_{A}
and
\mu_{B}
(see
[
JP
, Definition 2.2 and Assumption 2.3]
), there exists a positive constant
\tilde{C}
and a sequence of positive constants
\mu_{+}(N)
, such that for any
\vec{\mathbf{k}}=(\mathbf{k}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{k}_{l})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{l}
,
M_{j}=\lfloor\mathbf{k}_{j}N^{{2/3}}\rfloor
for
1\leq j\leq l
,
M=\sum_{j=1}^{l}M_{j}
, as
N\rightarrow\infty
,
\mu_{+}(N)\rightarrow\mu_{+},
the right endpoint of
\mu_{A}\boxplus\mu_{B}
, and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mu_{+}(N)^{M}}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{l}\left[\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{1}}{N}\right)^{M_{j}}+\ldots+\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}_{N}}{N}\right)^{M_{j}}\right]G_{N}(\vec{x};\beta,\mu_{c(N)})\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}_{\beta}\left(\frac{\mu_{+}}{2\tilde{C}}\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\bm{\tau}}=0\right).

For
\beta=1,2
, this is proved in
[
Ahn
]
and
[
JP
]
using different approaches. We note that in particular, one can take
a(N)
,
b(N)
to be two deterministic sequences, that satisfy the assumptions in these works. This implies that the Airy statistic arises from mixing two Hermitian matrices, whose only randomness comes from the uniformly distributed eigenvectors. As for the proofs,
[
Ahn
]
also relies on BGF and moment methods, but the operators in that work are a version of Macdonald difference operators and are limited to the case
\beta=2
.
[
JP
]
establishes a Green function comparison theorem for both
\beta=1,2
, and their proof relies on the Hermitian matrix structure, which no longer exists for general
\beta
.

Besides the difficulties discussed in Section
7.1
, for general
\beta
-additions,
G_{N}(\vec{x};\beta,\mu_{c(N)})
no longer has the explicit expression as in Eqn. (
29
). Because of these limitations, we suspect that, in order to prove the full conjecture, it will be essential to acquire a deeper understanding of the combinatorics of multivariate Bessel functions.

Appendix A
Generalized ballot problem

Suppose we have a walk
W(t)
,
t=0,1,2,\ldots,L
, whose possible increments are
\{(1,k)\}_{k=-1,0,1,\ldots}
. We assign a weight of
w_{k}
to a step of vertical increment
k
, and let the weight of each walk be the product of the weights of its increments. Fix the endpoints of all the walks to be starting at
(0,y_{0})
and ending at
(L,0)
. We make the following definitions:

•

Define
Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}(\{w\})
to be the partition function for these walks as a function of their weights
\{w\}=\{w_{-1},w_{0},w_{1},w_{2},\ldots\}
.

•

Define
Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{good}(\{w\})
to be the partition function for the walks constrained to stay strictly above the
x
-axis before the
L^{th}
step. We call such walks “good”.

•

Define
Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{bad}(\{w\})
to be the partition function for the walks that are not “good”. We call such walks “bad”.

We are interested in computing the partition function of the “good” paths. In the literature these types of paths are known as Łukasiewicz paths. See
[
BP
]
, and references therein, for a study of these paths with uniform weights.

Proposition A.1
.

The partition function for the “good” paths can be written as

Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{good}(\{w\})=\frac{y_{0}}{L}Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}(\{w\}).

(125)

Remark A.2
.

When
w_{-1}=w_{1}=1
and all other
w_{i}
are 0, this is equivalent to Bertrand’s ballot problem
[
Ber
]
. Many other variations of the problem have been solved. The variation presented here in Proposition
A.1
is essentially done in
[
GS
, Section 6]
using a generalized version of the “reflection principle”. For completeness, we present a proof below.

Proof.

The last step of a “bad” walk can either be a down-, flat-, or up-step, where the up-step can be of height
k=1,2,\ldots
. Grouping these walks based on their last step, we may write their partition function as

Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{bad}(\{w\})=Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{bad,-}(\{w\})+Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{0}(\{w\})+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{+,k}(\{w\})

(126)

where
Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{bad,-}(\{w\})
is the partition function for “bad” walks that end with a down-step,
Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{0}(\{w\})
is the partition function for walks that end with a flat step, and
Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{+,k}(\{w\})
is the partition function for walks that end with an up-step of height
k
. Note that walks whose last step is a flat step or an up-step are necessarily “bad”.

Each bad walk that ends with a down-step must have some last time (before step
L
) at which it hit the
x
-axis. We can partition the walks in
Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{bad,-}(\{w\})
by the size
k
of the up-step and the height
i=1,\ldots,k
of the walk after that step. Let
Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{bad,-,k,i}(\{w\})
be the partition function for these walks with fixed
k
and
i
.

For each such walk, we can take all the steps after (and including) the last time it hits the
x
-axis and rotate this piece of the walk by 180 degrees. Doing this results in a walk whose last step is an up-step of size
k
. Note that this is invertible: for any walk whose last step is an up-step of size
k
, we can find the last down-step starting from height
-(k-i)
and rotate all of the walk starting from this step. See Example
A.3
. Note that these rotations are weight-preserving. It follows that

Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{bad,-,k,i}(\{w\})=Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{+,k}(\{w\})

for each
k
and
i
. Since for each
k
we have that
i
ranges over
1,\ldots,k
, summing the above over all
i
and
k
results in

Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{bad,-}(\{w\})=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k\;Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{+,k}(\{w\}).

(127)

Let
Z^{+}
,
Z^{0}
and
Z^{-}
denote the partition functions of the walks with positive/zero/negative increment in the last step, respectively. From Eqn. (
126
) and Eqn. (
127
), we see that

\displaystyle Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{good}(\{w\})=

\displaystyle Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}(\{w\})-Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{bad}(\{w\})

(128)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{+}(\{w\})+Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{0}(\{w\})+Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{-}(\{w\})-Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{bad}(\{w\})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{-}(\{w\})-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k\;Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{+,k}(\{w\}).

Now, suppose we fix the number of steps of size
i=-1,0,1,2,\ldots
to be
n_{i}
. Because we fix the endpoints of the walks to be at
(0,y_{0})
and
(L,0)
, we must have

\sum_{i}n_{i}=L\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sum_{i}i\;n_{i}=-y_{0}.

The weight of a walk with this distribution of steps is given by
\prod_{i}w_{i}^{n_{i}}
. The contribution that such walks make to
Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{-}(\{w\})-\sum_{k}k\;Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{+,k}(\{w\})
can be written as

\displaystyle\left(\binom{L-1}{n_{-1}-1,n_{0},n_{1},\ldots}-\sum_{k}k\;\binom{L-1}{n_{-1},n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}-1,\ldots}\right)\prod_{i}w_{i}^{n_{i}}

\displaystyle=\frac{n_{-1}-\sum_{k}k\;n_{k}}{L}\binom{L}{n_{-1},n_{0},n_{1},\ldots}\prod_{i}w_{i}^{n_{i}}

\displaystyle=\frac{y_{0}}{L}\binom{L}{n_{-1},n_{0},n_{1},\ldots}\prod_{i}w_{i}^{n_{i}}.

Finally, summing over all choices of the
n_{i}
, we obtain

Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}^{good}(\{w\})=\frac{y_{0}}{L}Z_{(y_{0},0,L)}(\{w\}).

∎

Example A.3
.

Here we have
y_{0}=2
and
L=9
. Below is a walk in
(\mathrm{bad},-,3,2)
, that is the last step to hit the
x
-axis is a step of size 3 that ends at height 2:

After the rotation, we get

a walk in
(+,3)
.

Note that the walks

in
(\mathrm{bad},-,3,1)
and
(\mathrm{bad},-,3,3)
, respectively, also map to the same walk in
(+,3)
after rotating their highlighted blocks.

Let us now restrict to the types of walks defined in Section
3.3
in order to give a proof of Lemma
3.6
which we restate below as a corollary to Proposition
A.1
. Recall that we consider walks with weight given by

w_{-1}(N)=\frac{1}{z_{c}(N)}\quad\text{and}\quad w_{l-1}(N)=\kappa_{l}(N)z_{c}(N)^{l-1},\quad l\geq 1

where the
\kappa_{l}(N)
are the finite-
N
version of the free cumulants of our matrix additions (
26
) and
z_{c}(N)
is the unique positive zero of their Voiculescu transform

V_{N}(z)=\frac{1}{z}+\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\kappa_{l}(N)z^{l-1}.

Corollary A.4
.

The partition function for the walk bridges from
(0,H)
to
(L,0)
, with weights given in Definition
3.4
, is

\frac{H+1}{L+1}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint\;\left(\frac{z}{z_{c}(N)}\right)^{H}V_{N}(z)^{L+1}\;dz

(129)

where the contour only encloses the pole at
0
.

Proof.

We make a couple of observations. First, note that
V_{N}(z_{c}(N))^{K}
,
K\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, can also be seen as the generating function for walks of length
K
, where the power of
z_{c}(N)
corresponds to the total change in height of the walks. Second, rather than considering walks from
(0,H)
to
(L,0)
that stay weakly above
y=0
, we may consider walks from
(0,H+1)
to
(L+1,0)
that stay strictly above
y=0
. To do this we take each of the original walks, shift them up by one, and add an extra down-step to the end of the walk. This gives a bijection between the sets of walks with the difference in weight given by a factor of
\frac{1}{z_{c}(N)}.

As these walks have a fixed change in height, the power of
z_{c}(N)
is fixed and the same for each walk. It is given by
z_{c}(N)^{-(H+1)}
. By the residue theorem, the partition function for walks from
(0,H+1)
to
(L+1,0)
with no additional restrictions is given by

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint\;\frac{z^{H}}{z_{c}(N)^{H+1}}V_{N}(z)^{L+1}\;dz.

It follows from Proposition
A.1
that the partition function for walks from
(0,H+1)
to
(L+1,0)
staying strictly above the
x
-axis is given by

\frac{H+1}{L+1}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint\;\frac{z^{H}}{z_{c}(N)^{H+1}}V_{N}(z)^{L+1}\;dz.

As mentioned above, the difference in weight between these walks and the walks we consider is a single factor of
\frac{1}{z_{c}(N)}
, and the result follows.
∎

Appendix B
Proof of Proposition
6.2

Proof.

We introduce an auxiliary approximation. For any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
\alpha>0
, and
N\in\mathbb{N}
, define

M_{+}^{\prime}[x,\alpha,N]=\left(\max\{1+N^{-2/3}x,0\}\right)^{2\lfloor\alpha N^{2/3}/2\rfloor}.

First consider the case
x>-N^{2/3}
. We have

|M_{+}^{\prime}[x,\alpha,N]-M[x,\alpha,N]|=N^{-2/3}|x|M_{+}[x,\alpha,N].

Fix
\delta_{-},\delta_{+}>0
. Observe that
|x|\leq\delta_{+}^{-1}(1+N^{-2/3}x)^{\delta_{+}N^{2/3}}
for
x>0
, and
|x|\leq\delta_{-}^{-1}(1+N^{-2/3}x)^{-\delta_{-}N^{2/3}}
for
-N^{2/3}<x<0
. Consequently, for
x>-N^{2/3}
,
\alpha>\delta_{-}
, and sufficiently large
N
,

|M_{+}^{\prime}[x,\alpha,N]-M[x,\alpha,N]|\leq 2N^{-2/3}(\delta_{+}^{-1}M_{+}[x,\alpha+\delta_{+},N]+\delta_{-}^{-1}M_{+}[x,\alpha-\delta_{-},N]).

(130)

For the case
x<-N^{2/3}
, a symmetry argument applies. Let
x^{\prime}=-2N^{2/3}-x>-N^{2/3}
. Then
|M_{+}^{\prime}-M|=|2+xN^{-2/3}|M_{+}
. Since
|2+xN^{-2/3}|=|x^{\prime}|N^{-2/3}
, and
M_{+}
is symmetric with respect to the transformation
x\to x^{\prime}
, the bound (
130
) holds generally for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
(noting that at
x=-N^{2/3}
, the difference is zero).

Take
N=N_{k}
. Since
\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M_{+}[x_{i}^{N_{k}},\mathbf{k},N_{k}]
is bounded for all relevant
\mathbf{k}
, the error term on the right-hand side of Eqn. (
130
) vanishes as
k\to\infty
due to the factor
N_{k}^{-2/3}
. Thus, for any
\alpha>0
, the sequence
\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[x_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}]
converges if and only if
\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M[x_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}]
converges, and their limits are identical.

We now prove the convergence of the first particle
x_{1}^{N_{k}}
.
Since
M_{+}[x_{1}^{N_{k}},1,N_{k}]
is bounded uniformly in
k
, the sequence
x_{1}^{N_{k}}
is bounded from above.
Suppose for the sake of contradiction that
x_{1}^{N_{k}}
does not converge. Then there exist two subsequences of
\{N_{k}\}
, denoted by
\{P_{k}\}
and
\{Q_{k}\}
, such that
x_{1}^{P_{k}}\to a
and
x_{1}^{Q_{k}}\to b
as
k\to\infty
, with
a,b\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
and
a<b
.

For any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
, utilizing the limit
b
along the subsequence
Q_{k}
, we have

\lim_{k\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{P_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[x_{i}^{P_{k}},\alpha,P_{k}]=\lim_{k\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{Q_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[x_{i}^{Q_{k}},\alpha,Q_{k}]\geq\lim_{k\to\infty}M_{+}^{\prime}[x_{1}^{Q_{k}},\alpha,Q_{k}]=\exp(\alpha b).

Conversely, considering the subsequence
P_{k}
where
x_{1}^{P_{k}}\to a
, and noting that
x_{1}^{P_{k}}\geq x_{i}^{P_{k}}
implies an exponential bound, we estimate:

\lim_{k\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{P_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[x_{i}^{P_{k}},\alpha,P_{k}]\leq\exp((\alpha-1)a)\lim_{k\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{P_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[x_{i}^{P_{k}},1,P_{k}].

Combining these inequalities yields

\exp(\alpha(b-a)+a)\leq\lim_{k\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{P_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[x_{i}^{P_{k}},1,P_{k}].

Since
b>a
, the left-hand side grows exponentially with
\alpha
, while the right-hand side is a finite constant independent of
\alpha
. This is a contradiction. Thus,
x_{1}^{N_{k}}
must converge.

Given the convergence of
x_{1}^{N_{k}}
, the first term in the sum converges. Subtracting this term implies that
\sum_{i=2}^{N_{k}}M[x_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}]
converges as
k\to\infty
for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
. By induction, we conclude that
x_{i}^{N_{k}}
converges to some
x_{i}
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

Finally, for any
\alpha>0
, we prove the convergence of the sum to the exponential limit

\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[x_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}]\to\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\exp(\alpha x_{i}).

(131)

Let
X=\limsup_{k\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[x_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}]
. Choose
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
such that
\alpha_{1}^{-1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha_{1}<\alpha<\alpha_{2}
. The convexity inequality
y^{\alpha}\leq y^{\alpha_{1}}+y^{\alpha_{2}}
for
y\geq 0
combined with the hypotheses ensures that
X<\infty
.
For any fixed
j
, since the terms are ordered (
x_{i}\geq x_{i+1}
), we see that for any
j^{\prime}\geq j
we have

M_{+}^{\prime}[x_{j^{\prime}}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}]\leq\frac{1}{j}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[x_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}].

This tail decay implies

\limsup_{k\to\infty}\sum_{i=j}^{N_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[x_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}]\leq(j^{-1}X)^{1-\alpha_{1}/\alpha}\lim_{k\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[x_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha_{1},N_{k}].

Consequently, the contribution of the tail tends to zero as
j\to\infty
:

\lim_{j\to\infty}\limsup_{k\to\infty}\sum_{i=j}^{N_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[x_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}]=0.

Combining this tail property with the pointwise convergence
x_{i}^{N_{k}}\to x_{i}
, we obtain Eqn. (
131
).
∎
